Empirical Risk Minimization with Relative Entropy Regularization
Samir M. Perlaza, Gaetan Bisson, Iñaki Esnaola, Alain Jean-Marie, Stefano Rini

Abstract
The empirical risk minimization (ERM) problem with relative entropy regularization (ERM-RER) is investigated under the assumption that the reference measure is a σ \sigma -finite measure, and not necessarily a probability measure. Under this assumption, which leads to a generalization of the ERM-RER problem allowing a larger degree of flexibility for incorporating prior knowledge, numerous relevant properties are stated. Among these properties, the solution to this problem, if it exists, is shown to be a unique probability measure, mutually absolutely continuous with the reference measure. Such a solution exhibits a probably-approximately-correct guarantee for the ERM problem independently of whether the latter possesses a solution. For a fixed dataset and under a specific condition, the empirical risk is shown to be a sub-Gaussian random variable when the models are sampled from the solution to the ERM-RER problem. The generalization capabilities of the solution to the ERM-RER problem (the Gibbs algorithm) are studied via the sensitivity of the expected empirical risk to deviations from such a solution towards alternative probability measures. Finally, an interesting connection between sensitivity, generalization error, and lautum information is established.



I
Introduction

In statistical machine learning, the problem of empirical risk minimization (ERM) with relative entropy regularization (ERM-RER) has been the workhorse for building probability measures on the set of models, without any additional assumption on the statistical description of the datasets. See for instance
[
3
,
4
,
5
]
and
[
6
]
.
Instead of additional statistical assumptions on the datasets, which are typical in Bayesian methods
[
7
]
, relative entropy regularization requires a reference probability measure on the set of models, which is external to the ERM problem.
Often, such a reference measure represents prior knowledge or side information and is chosen for guiding the search of models towards those inducing low empirical risks with high probability over seen and unseen datasets.
From this perspective, the reference measure can be seen as an additional degree of freedom to improve the generalization capabilities of machine learning algorithms based on ERM-RER, e.g, Gibbs algorithms
[
8
,
9
,
10
,
5
,
11
,
12
,
13
,
14
,
15
,
16
,
17
]
and
[
18
]
.
This new degree of freedom is one of the main motivations for regularizing the ERM problem using relative entropy, or more generally, any
f
-divergence regularization, as discussed in
[
19
,
20
]
and
[
21
]
.
Beyond probability measures, as shown in this paper, the reference measure can be any
\sigma
-finite measure with arbitrary support.
The flexibility introduced by this generalization becomes particularly relevant for the case in which priors are available in the form of probability distributions that can be evaluated up to some normalizing factor, cf.
[
22
]
, or cannot be represented by probability distributions, e.g., equal preferences among elements of infinite countable sets.
For some specific choices of
\sigma
-finite reference measures, the ERM-RER boils down to particular cases of special interest:
(i)
the information-risk minimization problem presented in
[
23
]
;
(ii)
the ERM with differential entropy regularization (ERM-DiffER); and
(iii)
the ERM with discrete entropy regularization (ERM-DisER). See for instance
[
24
,
25
]
and references therein. From this perspective, the proposed ERM-RER formulation yields a unified mathematical framework that comprises a large class of problems.

When the reference measure is a probability measure, the solution to the ERM-RER problem is known to be unique and correspond to a Gibbs probability measure. Such a Gibbs probability measure has been studied using measure theoretic and information theoretic notions in
[
26
,
27
,
28
,
29
,
9
,
30
,
31
,
23
,
32
,
33
]
; statistical physics in
[
3
]
; PAC (Probably Approximatively Correct)-Bayesian learning theory in
[
34
,
35
,
36
,
37
]
; and proved to be of particular interest in classification problems in
[
20
,
5
,
24
,
38
,
39
,
12
]
and
[
40
]
.
In the general case in which the reference is a
\sigma
-finite measure, a solution to the ERM-RER problem does not always exist. Nonetheless, if it exists, it is shown to be a unique Gibbs probability despite the fact that its partition function is defined with respect to a
\sigma
-finite measure.
The condition for the existence is mild and is always satisfied when the reference measure is a probability measure, as highlighted above.
Interestingly, such a solution is mutually absolutely continuous with the reference measure and most of the properties known for the classical ERM-RER problem are shown to hold in the most general case.
For instance, under certain conditions, the empirical risk observed when models are sampled from the ERM-RER-optimal probability measure is a sub-Gaussian random variable that exhibits a PAC guarantee for the ERM problem without regularization.

When the solution to the ERM-RER problem is used to sample models to label unseen patterns, the process is known as the Gibbs algorithm. One of the traditional performance metrics to evaluate the generalization capabilities of machine learning algorithms is the generalization error, for which closed-form expressions in terms of information measures are presented in
[
15
]
. When the reference measure is a probability measure, a closed-form expression for the generalization error of the Gibbs algorithm is presented in
[
9
]
, while upper bounds have been derived in
[
41
,
42
,
43
,
23
,
44
,
33
,
45
,
46
,
31
,
47
,
48
,
49
,
32
,
50
,
51
,
52
,
53
,
18
,
54
,
55
,
34
,
35
,
36
,
37
]
, and references therein.
In this work, a new performance metric coined
sensitivity
, which quantifies the variations of the expected empirical risk due to deviations from the solution of the ERM-RER problem is introduced. The sensitivity is defined as the difference between two quantities:
(a)
The expectation of the empirical risk with respect to the solution to the ERM-RER problem; and
(b)
the expectation of the empirical risk with respect to an alternative measure.
The absolute value of the sensitivity is shown to be upper bounded by a term that is proportional to the squared-root of the relative entropy of the alternative measure with respect to the ERM-RER-optimal measure.
Such bound allows providing lower and upper bounds on the expected empirical risk after a deviation from the ERM-RER-optimal measure towards an alternative probability measure.
More interestingly, the expectation (with respect to the probability distribution of the datasets) of the sensitivity to deviations to a specific measure is shown to be equal to the generalization error of the Gibbs algorithm. Using this result, the closed-form expression for the generalization error of the Gibbs algorithm presented in
[
9
]
is shown to hold even in the case in which the reference measure is a
\sigma
-finite measure.
Moreover, the generalization error is shown to be upper bounded by a term that is proportional to the squared-root of the lautum information between the models and the datasets, cf.
[
56
]
.
This bound is reminiscent of the result in
[
33
, Theorem
1
]
in which a similar bound is presented using the mutual information instead of the lautum information. While
[
33
, Theorem
1
]
follows immediately from the variational representation of relative entropy, c.f.,
[
57
, Lemma
4.18
(Transportation Lemma)]
, the new result follows from the fact that the empirical risk when models are sampled from the ERM-RER-optimal probability measure is a sub-Gaussian random variable.
Interestingly, the new upper-bound does not require any of the conditions in
[
33
, Theorem
1
]
.

The remainder of this work is organized as follows. Section
II
introduces two optimization problems: the ERM and the ERM-RER. The asymmetry of the relative entropy is analyzed in the context of the ERM-RER and two variants, coined
Type-I
and
Type-II
, are distinguished. The former considers the case in which the regularization is the relative entropy of the optimization measure with respect to the reference measure. The latter considers a regularization by the relative entropy of the reference measure with respect to the optimization measure.
Section
III
presents the solution to the ERM-RER problem in the general case and introduces its main properties.
Section
IV
introduces two new classes of reference measures and the solution of the ERM-RER problem is shown to exhibit different properties for each class. This section ends by studying the ERM-RER problem in the special case in which the reference measure is a Gibbs probability measure. This special case exhibits a solution that is identical to the solution to an ERM-RER problem whose reference measure is the same used to build the above mentioned Gibbs measure.
Section
V
studies the properties of the log-partition function of the ERM-RER-optimal probability measure. The first, second, and third cumulants of the empirical risk when the models are sampled from the ERM-RER-optimal measure and the reference measure are respectively characterized.
Section
VI
and Section
VII
study the properties of the expectation and variance of the empirical risk when the models are sampled from the ERM-RER-optimal probability measure. This mean and variance are compared with the mean and variance of the empirical risk when models are sampled from the reference measure.
Section
VIII
introduces several explicit expressions for the cumulant generating function of the empirical risk when the models are sampled from the ERM-RER-optimal measure. Using these equivalent expressions, under a specific condition, it is shown that the empirical risk is a sub-Gaussian random variable when models are sampled from the ERM-RER-optimal measure.
Section
IX
describes the monotonic concentration of the ERM-RER-optimal probability measure when the regularization factor tends to zero.
Section
X
show that the empirical risk when the models are sampled from the ERM-RER-optimal probability measure exhibits a PAC-type guarantee with respect to the ERM problem without regularization.
Finally, Section
XI
studies the sensitivity of the expected empirical risk with respect to deviations from the ERM-RER-optimal measure to alternative measures and shows connections with the generalization error and the lautum information.
Section
XII
ends this work with conclusions and a discussion on the results.

II
Empirical Risk Minimization (ERM)

Let
\mathcal{M}
,
\mathcal{X}
and
\mathcal{Y}
, with
\mathcal{M}\subseteq\mathds{R}^{d}
and
d\in\mathds{N}
, be sets of
models
,
patterns
, and
labels
, respectively.
A pair
(x,y)\in\mathcal{X}\times\mathcal{Y}
is referred to as a
labeled pattern
or as a
data point
.
Given
n
data points, with
n\in\mathds{N}
, denoted by
\left(x_{1},y_{1}\right)
,
\left(x_{2},y_{2}\right)
,
\ldots
,
\left(x_{n},y_{n}\right)
, the corresponding dataset is represented by the tuple

\boldsymbol{z}=\big(\left(x_{1},y_{1}\right),\left(x_{2},y_{2}\right),\ldots,\left(x_{n},y_{n}\right)\big)\in\left(\mathcal{X}\times\mathcal{Y}\right)^{n}.

(1)

Let the function
f:\mathcal{M}\times\mathcal{X}\rightarrow\mathcal{Y}
be such that the label assigned to the pattern
x
according to the model
\boldsymbol{\theta}\in\mathcal{M}
is
f(\boldsymbol{\theta},x)
.
Let also the function

\ell:\mathcal{Y}\times\mathcal{Y}\rightarrow[0,+\infty]

(2)

be such that given a data point
(x,y)\in\mathcal{X}\times\mathcal{Y}
, the risk induced by a model
\boldsymbol{\theta}\in\mathcal{M}
is
\ell\left(f(\boldsymbol{\theta},x),y\right)
.
In the following, the risk function
\ell
is assumed to be nonnegative and for all
y\in\mathcal{Y}
,
\ell\left(y,y\right)=0
.

The
empirical risk
induced by the model
\boldsymbol{\theta}
, with respect to the dataset
\boldsymbol{z}
in (
1
) is determined by the function
\mathsf{L}_{\boldsymbol{z}}:\mathcal{M}\rightarrow[0,+\infty]
, which satisfies

\displaystyle\mathsf{L}_{\boldsymbol{z}}\left(\boldsymbol{\theta}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\ell\left(f(\boldsymbol{\theta},x_{i}),y_{i}\right).

(3)

Using this notation, the ERM consists of the following optimization problem:

\min_{\boldsymbol{\theta}\in\mathcal{M}}\mathsf{L}_{\boldsymbol{z}}\left(\boldsymbol{\theta}\right).

(4)

Let the set of solutions to the ERM problem in (
4
) be denoted by

\mathcal{T}\left(\boldsymbol{z}\right)\triangleq\arg\min_{\boldsymbol{\theta}\in\mathcal{M}}\mathsf{L}_{\boldsymbol{z}}\left(\boldsymbol{\theta}\right).

(5)

Note that if the set
\mathcal{M}
is finite, the ERM problem in (
4
) always possesses a solution, and thus,
\left|\mathcal{T}\left(\boldsymbol{z}\right)\right|>0
. Nonetheless, in general, the ERM problem might not necessarily possess a solution, i.e.,
\left|\mathcal{T}\left(\boldsymbol{z}\right)\right|=0
.

II-A

Notation and Main Assumptions

In the following, given a measurable space
\left(\Omega,\mathscr{F}\right)
, the notation
\triangle\left(\Omega,\mathscr{F}\right)
is used to represent the set of
\sigma
-finite measures that can be defined over
\left(\Omega,\mathscr{F}\right)
. Given a measure
Q\in\triangle\left(\Omega,\mathscr{F}\right)
, the subset
\triangle_{Q}\left(\Omega,\mathscr{F}\right)
of
\triangle\left(\Omega,\mathscr{F}\right)
contains all
\sigma
-finite measures that are absolutely continuous with respect to the measure
Q
. Alternatively, the subset
\bigtriangledown_{Q}\left(\Omega,\mathscr{F}\right)
of
\triangle\left(\Omega,\mathscr{F}\right)
contains all probability measures
P
such that
Q
is absolutely continuous with respect to
P
.
Given a set
\mathcal{A}\subset\mathds{R}^{d}
, the Borel
\sigma
-field over
\mathcal{A}
is denoted by
\mathscr{B}\left(\mathcal{A}\right)
.

The main assumption adopted in this work is that the function
\mathsf{L}_{\boldsymbol{z}}
in (
3
) is measurable with respect to the Borel measurable spaces
\left(\mathcal{M},\mathscr{B}\left(\mathcal{M}\right)\right)
and
\left([0,+\infty],\mathscr{B}\left([0,+\infty]\right)\right)
.

II-B

Relative Entropy Extended to
\sigma
-Finite Measures

In this work, the
relative entropy
, which is usually defined for probability measures, is extended to
\sigma
-finite measures.

Definition 1
(Generalized Relative Entropy)

Given two
\sigma
-finite measures
P
and
Q
on the same measurable space, such that
P
is absolutely continuous with respect to
Q
, the relative entropy of
P
with respect to
Q
is

D\left(P\|Q\right)=\int\frac{\mathrm{d}P}{\mathrm{d}Q}(x)\log\left(\frac{\mathrm{d}P}{\mathrm{d}Q}(x)\right)\mathrm{d}Q(x),

(6)

where the function
\frac{\mathrm{d}P}{\mathrm{d}Q}
is the Radon-Nikodym derivative of
P
with respect to
Q
.

The relative entropy exhibits a property often referred to as the
information inequality

[
58
, Theorem
2.6.3
]
in the case of probability measures on
\left(\Omega,\mathscr{F}\right)
, with
\Omega
a countable set. The following theorem explores this property in a more general scenario.

Theorem 1

If
P
and
Q
are both probability measures on a general measurable space
\left(\Omega,\mathscr{F}\right)
, with
P
absolutely continuous with respect to
Q
, then,

\displaystyle D\left(P\|Q\right)

\displaystyle\geqslant

\displaystyle 0,

(7)

with equality if and only if
P
and
Q
are identical.

Proof:

Consider the function
f:[0,\infty)\to\mathds{R}
such that for all
x\in(0,+\infty)
,
f(x)=x\log(x)
and
f(0)=0
. Note that
f
is strictly convex.
If
P
and
Q
are both probability measures on the measurable space
\left(\Omega,\mathscr{F}\right)
, the following holds:

\displaystyle D\left(P\|Q\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int\frac{\mathrm{d}P}{\mathrm{d}Q}(x)\log\left(\frac{\mathrm{d}P}{\mathrm{d}Q}(x)\right)\mathrm{d}Q(x)

(8)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int f\left(\frac{\mathrm{d}P}{\mathrm{d}Q}(x)\right)\mathrm{d}Q(x)

(9)

\displaystyle\geqslant

\displaystyle f\left(\int\frac{\mathrm{d}P}{\mathrm{d}Q}(x)\mathrm{d}Q(x)\right)

(10)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle f\left(1\right)

(11)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 0,

(12)

where the inequality (
11
) follows from Jensen’s inequality
[
59
, Section
6.3.5
]
. Equality in (
11
) holds if and only if for all
x\in\mathrm{supp}\,Q
,
\frac{\mathrm{d}P}{\mathrm{d}Q}(x)=1
, which implies that both
P
and
Q
are identical.
This completes proof.
∎If
Q
is not a probability measure, then it might be observed that
D\left(P\|Q\right)<0
. Consider for instance the case in which
P
is a zero-mean Gaussian probability measure with variance
\sigma^{2}
and
Q
is the Lebesgue measure on
\left(\mathds{R},\mathscr{B}\left(\mathds{R}\right)\right)
.
Hence, the Radon-Nikodym derivative
\frac{\mathrm{d}P}{\mathrm{d}Q}
is the Gaussian probability density function such that for all
x\in~\mathds{R}
,

\displaystyle\frac{\mathrm{d}P}{\mathrm{d}Q}(x)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\sqrt{2\pi\sigma^{2}}}\exp\left(-\frac{x^{2}}{2\sigma^{2}}\right).

(13)

Under this assumption, the relative entropy of
P
with respect to
Q
is the negative of the differential entropy of
P
. That is,

\displaystyle D\left(P\|Q\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\frac{1}{2}\log\left(2\pi\epsilon\sigma^{2}\right),

(14)

with
\epsilon
being Néper’s constant. See for instance
[
58
, Example
8.1.2
]
.
Hence,
D\left(P\|Q\right)
is negative for all
\sigma^{2}\in\left(\frac{1}{2\pi\epsilon},+\infty\right)
and nonnegative for all
\sigma^{2}\in\left(0,\frac{1}{2\pi\epsilon}\right]
.
Finally, note also that

\displaystyle\lim_{\sigma^{2}\to 0}D\left(P\|Q\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle+\infty,\mbox{ and}

(15)

\displaystyle\lim_{\sigma^{2}\to+\infty}D\left(P\|Q\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\infty.

(16)

A central observation from (
14
) is that the equality
D\left(P\|Q\right)=0
does not necessarily imply that
P
and
Q
are identical measures. For instance, when
\sigma^{2}=\frac{1}{2\pi\epsilon}
in (
15
), it holds that
D\left(P\|Q\right)=0
, while
P
is a Gaussian probability measure and
Q
is the Lebesgue measure.

The following property, known for the case of probability measures as the
joint-convexity of the relative entropy
, is extended by the following theorem.

Theorem 2

Let
P_{1}
and
P_{2}
be two probability measures and
Q_{1}
and
Q_{2}
be two
\sigma
-finite measures, all on the same measurable space. For all
i\in\{1,2\}
, let
P_{i}
be absolutely continuous with respect to
Q_{i}
. Then, for all
\lambda\in[0,1]
,

\displaystyle D\left(\lambda P_{1}+(1-\lambda)P_{2}\|\lambda Q_{1}+(1-\lambda)Q_{2}\right)

(17)

\displaystyle\leqslant

\displaystyle\lambda D\left(P_{1}\|Q_{1}\right)+(1-\lambda)D\left(P_{2}\|Q_{2}\right).

Equality in (
17
) holds if and only if
P_{1}=P_{2}
and
Q_{1}=Q_{2}
.

Proof:

The proof is presented in Appendix
A
.
∎

II-C

ERM with Relative Entropy Regularization

Given a dataset, the
expected empirical risk
induced by a measure
P\in\Delta\left(\mathcal{M},\mathscr{B}\left(\mathcal{M}\right)\right)
is defined as follows.

Definition 2
(Expected Empirical Risk)

Let
P
be a probability measure in
\Delta\left(\mathcal{M},\mathscr{B}\left(\mathcal{M}\right)\right)
. The expected empirical risk with respect to the dataset
\boldsymbol{z}
in (
1
) induced by the measure
P
is

\mathsf{R}_{\boldsymbol{z}}\left(P\right)=\int\mathsf{L}_{\boldsymbol{z}}\left(\boldsymbol{\theta}\right)\mathrm{d}P(\boldsymbol{\theta}),

(18)

where the function
\mathsf{L}_{\boldsymbol{z}}
is defined in (
3
).

The ERM-RER problem is parametrized by a
\sigma
-finite measure in
\triangle\left(\mathcal{M},\mathscr{B}\left(\mathcal{M}\right)\right)
and a positive real, which are referred to as the
reference measure
and the
regularization factor
, respectively.
Let
Q\in\triangle\left(\mathcal{M},\mathscr{B}\left(\mathcal{M}\right)\right)
be a
\sigma
-finite measure and let
\lambda
be a positive real. The ERM-RER problem, with parameters
Q
and
\lambda
, consists of the following optimization problem:

\displaystyle\min_{P\in\triangle_{Q}\left(\mathcal{M},\mathscr{B}\left(\mathcal{M}\right)\right)}

\displaystyle\mathsf{R}_{\boldsymbol{z}}\left(P\right)+\lambda D\left(P\|Q\right),

(19a)

\displaystyle\mathrm{s.~t.}

\displaystyle\int\mathrm{d}P\left(\boldsymbol{\theta}\right)=1,

(19b)

where the dataset
\boldsymbol{z}
is in (
1
), and the functional
\mathsf{R}_{\boldsymbol{z}}
is defined in (
18
).

II-D

Type-I and Type-II Relative Entropy Regularization

The optimization problem in (
19
) is coined
Type-I
ERM-RER in
[
60
]
in the aim of distinguishing it from the optimization problem

\displaystyle\min_{P\in\bigtriangledown_{Q}\left(\mathcal{M},\mathscr{B}\left(\mathcal{M}\right)\right)}

\displaystyle\mathsf{R}_{\boldsymbol{z}}\left(P\right)+\lambda D\left(Q\|P\right),

(20a)

\displaystyle\mathrm{s.~t.}

\displaystyle\int\mathrm{d}P\left(\boldsymbol{\theta}\right)=1,

(20b)

which is coined
Type-II
ERM-RER.

The
Type-II
ERM-RER problem in (
20
), when
Q
is a probability measure, exhibits a solution that is identical to the solution to the following
Type-I
ERM-RER problem
[
60
, Theorem
1
]
:

\displaystyle\min_{P\in\triangle_{Q}\left(\mathcal{M},\mathscr{B}\left(\mathcal{M}\right)\right)}

\displaystyle\int\log\left(\beta+\mathsf{L}_{\boldsymbol{z}}\left(\boldsymbol{\nu}\right)\right)\mathrm{d}P\left(\boldsymbol{\nu}\right)+D\left(P\|Q\right),

(21a)

\displaystyle\mathrm{s.~t.}

\displaystyle\int\mathrm{d}P\left(\boldsymbol{\theta}\right)=1,

(21b)

where
\beta
is a constant chosen to satisfy

\displaystyle\int\frac{\lambda}{\beta+\mathsf{L}_{\boldsymbol{z}}\left(\boldsymbol{\nu}\right)}\mathrm{d}Q(\boldsymbol{\nu})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 1.

(21c)

Essentially, by appropriately transforming the objective function, an equivalence can be established between
Type-I
and
Type-II
ERM-RER problems.
Hence, without loss of generality, the remainder of this work focuses exclusively on
Type-I
ERM-RER, which is simply referred to as ERM-RER.

III
The Solution to the ERM-RER Problem

The solution to the ERM-RER problem in (
19
) is presented in terms of two objects. First, the function
K_{Q,\boldsymbol{z}}:\mathds{R}\rightarrow\mathds{R}\cup\{+\infty\}
such that for all
t\in\mathds{R}
,

\displaystyle K_{Q,\boldsymbol{z}}\left(t\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\log\left(\int\exp\left(t\;\mathsf{L}_{\boldsymbol{z}}\left(\boldsymbol{\theta}\right)\right)\mathrm{d}Q(\boldsymbol{\theta})\right),

(22)

with
\mathsf{L}_{\boldsymbol{z}}
in (
3
). Second, the set
\mathcal{K}_{Q,\boldsymbol{z}}\subset(0,+\infty)
, which is defined by

\displaystyle\mathcal{K}_{Q,\boldsymbol{z}}

\displaystyle\triangleq

\displaystyle\left\{s\in(0,+\infty):\;K_{Q,\boldsymbol{z}}\left(-\frac{1}{s}\right)<+\infty\right\}.

(23)

The notation for the function
K_{Q,\boldsymbol{z}}
and the set
\mathcal{K}_{Q,\boldsymbol{z}}
are chosen such that their parametrization by (or dependence on) the dataset
\boldsymbol{z}
in (
1
) and the
\sigma
-finite measure
Q
in (
19
) are highlighted.

The following lemma describes the set
\mathcal{K}_{Q,\boldsymbol{z}}
.

Lemma 1

The set
\mathcal{K}_{Q,\boldsymbol{z}}
in (
23
) is a convex subset of
\mathds{R}
. If the measure
Q
in (
19
) is a probability measure, then, the set
\mathcal{K}_{Q,\boldsymbol{z}}
in (
23
) satisfies

\mathcal{K}_{Q,\boldsymbol{z}}=(0,+\infty).

(24)

Proof:

The proof is presented in Appendix
B
.
∎Using this notation, the solution to the ERM-RER problem in (
19
) is presented by the following theorem.

Theorem 3

If
\lambda\in\mathcal{K}_{Q,\boldsymbol{z}}
, with
\mathcal{K}_{Q,\boldsymbol{z}}
in (
23
), the solution to the optimization problem in (
19
) is a unique probability measure, denoted by
P^{\left(Q,\lambda\right)}_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}|\boldsymbol{Z}=\boldsymbol{z}}
, which satisfies for all
\boldsymbol{\theta}\in\mathrm{supp}\,Q
,

\displaystyle\frac{\mathrm{d}P^{\left(Q,\lambda\right)}_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}|\boldsymbol{Z}=\boldsymbol{z}}}{\mathrm{d}Q}\left(\boldsymbol{\theta}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\exp\left(-K_{Q,\boldsymbol{z}}\left(-\frac{1}{\lambda}\right)-\frac{1}{\lambda}\mathsf{L}_{\boldsymbol{z}}\left(\boldsymbol{\theta}\right)\right),

(25)

where the function
\mathsf{L}_{\boldsymbol{z}}
is defined in (
3
) and the function
K_{Q,\boldsymbol{z}}
is defined in (
22
).

Proof:

The proof is presented in Appendix
C
.
∎

Contrary to the ERM problem in (
4
), which does not necessarily possess a solution, the ERM-RER problem in (
19
) always possess a solution when
Q
is a probability measure. This is essentially because the set
\mathcal{K}_{Q,\boldsymbol{z}}
is the set of all positive reals (Lemma
1
), and thus, the condition
\lambda\in\mathcal{K}_{Q,\boldsymbol{z}}
is always verified. On the contrary, when
Q
is a
\sigma
-finite measure, the solution to the ERM-RER problem in (
19
) depends on whether
\lambda\in\mathcal{K}_{Q,\boldsymbol{z}}
. If the solution exists, it is
P^{\left(Q,\lambda\right)}_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}|\boldsymbol{Z}=\boldsymbol{z}}
in (
25
), which is unique and corresponds to a Gibbs probability measure
[
61
]
.
The function
K_{Q,\boldsymbol{z}}
is often referred to as the
log-partition function
, see for instance,
[
62
, Section
7.3.1
]
.

The following lemma shows that the Radon-Nikodym derivative in (
25
) is both nonnegative and finite.

Lemma 2

The Radon-Nikodym derivative
\frac{\mathrm{d}P^{\left(Q,\lambda\right)}_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}|\boldsymbol{Z}=\boldsymbol{z}}}{\mathrm{d}Q}
in (
25
) satisfies for all
\boldsymbol{\theta}\in\mathrm{supp}\,Q
that

\frac{\mathrm{d}P^{\left(Q,\lambda\right)}_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}|\boldsymbol{Z}=\boldsymbol{z}}}{\mathrm{d}Q}\left(\boldsymbol{\theta}\right)<+\infty.

(26)

Moreover, it is strictly positive almost surely with respect to
Q
.

Proof:

The proof is presented in Appendix
D
.
∎

Theorem
3
has shown that the probability measure
P^{\left(Q,\lambda\right)}_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}|\boldsymbol{Z}=\boldsymbol{z}}
is absolutely continuous with respect to the measure
Q
.
The following lemma shows that the converse is also true.

Lemma 3

The
\sigma
-finite measure
Q
and the probability measure
P^{\left(Q,\lambda\right)}_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}|\boldsymbol{Z}=\boldsymbol{z}}
in (
25
) are mutually absolutely continuous.

Proof:

The proof is presented in Appendix
E
.
∎The relevance of Lemma
3
is that it shows that if
\lambda\in\mathcal{K}_{Q,\boldsymbol{z}}
, the corresponding collections of negligible sets with respect to the measures
P^{\left(Q,\lambda\right)}_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}|\boldsymbol{Z}=\boldsymbol{z}}
and
Q
are identical.
The following lemma shows that the negligible sets with respect to the measure
P^{\left(Q,\lambda\right)}_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}|\boldsymbol{Z}=\boldsymbol{z}}
in (
25
) are invariant with respect to
\lambda
.

Lemma 4

For all
\left(\alpha,\beta\right)\in\mathcal{K}_{Q,\boldsymbol{z}}\times\mathcal{K}_{Q,\boldsymbol{z}}
, with
\mathcal{K}_{Q,\boldsymbol{z}}
in (
23
), assume that the probability measures
P^{\left(Q,\alpha\right)}_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}|\boldsymbol{Z}=\boldsymbol{z}}~
and
P^{(Q,\beta)}_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}|\boldsymbol{Z}=\boldsymbol{z}}
satisfy (
25
) with
\lambda=\alpha
and
\lambda=\beta
, respectively. Then,
P^{\left(Q,\alpha\right)}_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}|\boldsymbol{Z}=\boldsymbol{z}}~
and
P^{(Q,\beta)}_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}|\boldsymbol{Z}=\boldsymbol{z}}
are mutually absolutely continuous.

Proof:

The proof is presented in Appendix
F
.
∎

Particular assumptions on the set
\mathcal{M}
and the reference measure
Q
lead to well-known instances of the ERM-RER problem in (
19
), as discussed hereunder.

III-A

Examples

Three examples are of particular interest:

(a)
The set
\mathcal{M}\subset\mathds{R}^{d}
is countable and the measure
Q
is the counting measure in
\left(\mathcal{M},\mathscr{B}\left(\mathcal{M}\right)\right)
, which leads to the ERM-DisER problem;

(b)
The set
\mathcal{M}
is an uncountable subset of
\mathds{R}^{d}
, and
Q
is the Lebesgue measure on
\left(\mathcal{M},\mathscr{B}\left(\mathcal{M}\right)\right)
, which leads to the ERM-DiffER problem; and

(c)
The set
\mathcal{M}
and the measure
Q
form a Borel probability measure space
\left(\mathcal{M},\mathscr{B}\left(\mathcal{M}\right),Q\right)
, which leads to the information-risk minimization problem.

III-A
1
ERM with Discrete Entropy Regularization

When the set
\mathcal{M}\subset\mathds{R}^{d}
is countable and the
\sigma
-finite measure
Q
in (
19
) is the counting measure in
\left(\mathcal{M},\mathscr{B}\left(\mathcal{M}\right)\right)
, given a probability measure
P\in\triangle\left(\mathcal{M},\mathscr{B}\left(\mathcal{M}\right)\right)
, the Radon-Nikodym derivative
\frac{\mathrm{d}P}{\mathrm{d}Q}
is a probability mass function, denoted by
p
. Thus, the relative entropy
D\left(P\|Q\right)
is equivalent to the negative of the discrete entropy induced by
p

[
58
, Chapter
2
]
, denoted by
H(p)
.
In this case, the ERM-RER in (
19
) can be re-written as the following ERM-DisER problem:

\displaystyle\min_{p}

∑_
θ
∈
M

L
_
z
(
θ
) p(
θ
) -
λ
H( p ),

(27)

where the optimization domain in (
27
) is the set of probability mass functions that can be defined over the measure space
\triangle\left(\mathcal{M},\mathscr{B}\left(\mathcal{M}\right)\right)
.
In this special case, the probability measure
P^{\left(Q,\lambda\right)}_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}|\boldsymbol{Z}=\boldsymbol{z}}
in (
25
) whose probability mass function is the solution to the ERM-DisER problem in (
27
) satisfies

\displaystyle\frac{\mathrm{d}P^{\left(Q,\lambda\right)}_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}|\boldsymbol{Z}=\boldsymbol{z}}}{\mathrm{d}Q}\left(\boldsymbol{\theta}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{\exp\left(-\frac{\mathsf{L}_{\boldsymbol{z}}\left(\boldsymbol{\theta}\right)}{\lambda}\right)}{\displaystyle\sum_{\boldsymbol{\nu}\in\mathcal{M}}\exp\left(-\frac{\mathsf{L}_{\boldsymbol{z}}\left(\boldsymbol{\nu}\right)}{\lambda}\right)},

(28)

which describes the discrete Gibbs probability measure on
\triangle\left(\mathcal{M},\mathscr{B}\left(\mathcal{M}\right)\right)
, with temperature parameter
\lambda
, and energy function
\mathsf{L}_{\boldsymbol{z}}
in (
3
).

III-A
2
ERM with Differential Entropy Regularization

When
\mathcal{M}\subseteq\mathds{R}^{d}
is uncountable and the
\sigma
-finite measure
Q
in (
19
) is the Lebesgue measure in
\left(\mathcal{M},\mathscr{B}\left(\mathcal{M}\right)\right)
, for all probability measures
P\in\Delta_{Q}\left(\mathcal{M},\mathscr{B}\left(\mathcal{M}\right)\right)
, the Radon-Nikodym derivative
\frac{\mathrm{d}P}{\mathrm{d}Q}
is a probability density function, denoted by
g
.
Thus, the relative entropy
D\left(P\|Q\right)
is equivalent to the negative of the differential entropy induced by
g

[
58
, Chapter
8
]
, denoted by
h(g)
.
In this special case, the ERM-RER in (
19
) can be re-written as the following ERM-DiffER problem:

\displaystyle\min_{g}

∫_
M

L
_
z
(
θ
) g(
θ
) d
θ
-
λ
h( g ),

(29)

where the optimization domain in (
29
) is the set of probability density functions that can be defined over the measure space
\left(\mathcal{M},\mathscr{B}\left(\mathcal{M}\right)\right)
.
The probability measure
P^{\left(Q,\lambda\right)}_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}|\boldsymbol{Z}=\boldsymbol{z}}
in (
25
) whose probability density function is the solution to the ERM-RER problem in (
29
) satisfies

\displaystyle\frac{\mathrm{d}P^{\left(Q,\lambda\right)}_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}|\boldsymbol{Z}=\boldsymbol{z}}}{\mathrm{d}Q}\left(\boldsymbol{\theta}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{\exp\left(-\frac{\mathsf{L}_{\boldsymbol{z}}\left(\boldsymbol{\theta}\right)}{\lambda}\right)}{\displaystyle\int_{\mathcal{M}}\exp\left(-\frac{\mathsf{L}_{\boldsymbol{z}}\left(\boldsymbol{\nu}\right)}{\lambda}\right)\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{\nu}},

(30)

which describes the absolutely continuous Gibbs probability measure with temperature parameter
\lambda
and energy function
\mathsf{L}_{\boldsymbol{z}}
in (
3
).

Both, the ERM-DiffER and ERM-DisER problems are closely related to those typically arising while using Jayne’s maximum entropy principle
[
63
,
64
]
for classification problems such as those in
[
38
,
39
,
24
]
, and
[
65
]
.

III-A
3
Information-Risk Minimization

When
Q
is a probability measure, the ERM-RER in (
19
) is equivalent to the
information-risk minimization
(IRM) problem in
[
23
]
.
The IRM problem in (
19
) is known to possess a unique solution equal to the Gibbs probability measure in (
25
), as independently shown in
[
23
,
61
,
66
,
33
,
67
]
and
[
68
]
.

III-B

Bounds on the Radon-Nikodym Derivative

The Radon-Nikodym derivative
\frac{\mathrm{d}P^{\left(Q,\lambda\right)}_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}|\boldsymbol{Z}=\boldsymbol{z}}}{\mathrm{d}Q}
in (
25
) is greater for models inducing smaller empirical risks, as shown by the following corollary of Theorem
3
.

Corollary 1

The Radon-Nikodym derivative
\frac{\mathrm{d}P^{\left(Q,\lambda\right)}_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}|\boldsymbol{Z}=\boldsymbol{z}}}{\mathrm{d}Q}
in (
25
) satisfies for all
\left(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{1},\boldsymbol{\theta}_{2}\right)\in\mathrm{supp}\,Q\times\mathrm{supp}\,Q
, with
\mathsf{L}_{\boldsymbol{z}}\left(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{2}\right)\leqslant\mathsf{L}_{\boldsymbol{z}}\left(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{1}\right)
, that

\frac{\mathrm{d}P^{\left(Q,\lambda\right)}_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}|\boldsymbol{Z}=\boldsymbol{z}}}{\mathrm{d}Q}\left(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{1}\right)\leqslant\frac{\mathrm{d}P^{\left(Q,\lambda\right)}_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}|\boldsymbol{Z}=\boldsymbol{z}}}{\mathrm{d}Q}\left(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{2}\right),

(31)

with equality if and only if
\mathsf{L}_{\boldsymbol{z}}\left(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{1}\right)=\mathsf{L}_{\boldsymbol{z}}\left(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{2}\right)
.

The intuition that follows from corollary
1
is that under the assumption that the ERM problem in (
4
) possesses a solution in the support of the reference measure, i.e.,
\mathcal{T}\left(\boldsymbol{z}\right)\cap\mathrm{supp}\,Q
is not empty, with
\mathcal{T}\left(\boldsymbol{z}\right)
in (
5
), the maximum of the function
\frac{\mathrm{d}P^{\left(Q,\lambda\right)}_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}|\boldsymbol{Z}=\boldsymbol{z}}}{\mathrm{d}Q}
in (
25
) is achieved by the models in
\mathcal{T}\left(\boldsymbol{z}\right)\cap\mathrm{supp}\,Q
.
When the Radon-Nikodym derivative
\frac{\mathrm{d}P^{\left(Q,\lambda\right)}_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}|\boldsymbol{Z}=\boldsymbol{z}}}{\mathrm{d}Q}
in (
25
) is either the probability mass function in (
28
) or the probability density function in (
30
), Corollary
1
shows that the elements of the set
\mathcal{T}\left(\boldsymbol{z}\right)\cap\mathrm{supp}\,Q
are the
modes
of the corresponding probability density function or probability mass function.

III-C

Asymptotes of the Radon-Nikodym Derivative

The following lemma describes the asymptotic behavior of the Radon-Nikodym derivative
\frac{\mathrm{d}P^{\left(Q,\lambda\right)}_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}|\boldsymbol{Z}=\boldsymbol{z}}}{\mathrm{d}Q}
in (
25
) when the regulariation factor increases, i.e.,
\lambda\rightarrow+\infty
and the reference measure
Q
is a probability measure.

Lemma 5

Let the measure
Q
in (
19
) be a probability measure. Then, for all
\boldsymbol{\theta}\in\mathrm{supp}\,Q
, the Radon-Nikodym derivative
\frac{\mathrm{d}P^{\left(Q,\lambda\right)}_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}|\boldsymbol{Z}=\boldsymbol{z}}}{\mathrm{d}Q}
in (
25
) satisfies

\displaystyle\lim_{\lambda\rightarrow+\infty}\frac{\mathrm{d}P^{\left(Q,\lambda\right)}_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}|\boldsymbol{Z}=\boldsymbol{z}}}{\mathrm{d}Q}\left(\boldsymbol{\theta}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 1.

(32)

Proof:

From Theorem
3
, it follows that for all
\boldsymbol{\theta}\in\mathrm{supp}\,Q
,

\displaystyle\lim_{\lambda\rightarrow+\infty}\frac{\mathrm{d}P^{\left(Q,\lambda\right)}_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}|\boldsymbol{Z}=\boldsymbol{z}}}{\mathrm{d}Q}\left(\boldsymbol{\theta}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\lim_{\lambda\rightarrow+\infty}\frac{\exp\left(-\frac{\mathsf{L}_{\boldsymbol{z}}\left(\boldsymbol{\theta}\right)}{\lambda}\right)}{\displaystyle\int\exp\left(-\frac{\mathsf{L}_{\boldsymbol{z}}\left(\boldsymbol{\nu}\right)}{\lambda}\right)\mathrm{d}Q\left(\boldsymbol{\nu}\right)}

(33)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\displaystyle\int\mathrm{d}Q\left(\boldsymbol{\nu}\right)}

(34)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 1,

(35)

where the function
\mathsf{L}_{\boldsymbol{z}}
is defined in (
3
). This completes the proof.
∎

Lemma
5
unveils the fact that, when
Q
is a probability measure, in the limit when
\lambda\rightarrow+\infty
, both probability measures
P^{\left(Q,\lambda\right)}_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}|\boldsymbol{Z}=\boldsymbol{z}}
and
Q
are identical.
This is consistent with the fact that when
\lambda
tends to infinity, the optimization problem in (
19
) boils down to exclusively minimizing the relative entropy. Such minimum is zero and is observed when both probability measures
P^{\left(Q,\lambda\right)}_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}|\boldsymbol{Z}=\boldsymbol{z}}
and
Q
are identical (Theorem
1
).
Such intuition breaks when the reference measure is a
\sigma
-finite measure, but not a probability measure. In such a case, the relative entropy term in (
19
) might be negative and a minimum might not exist. See for instance, the case of the relative entropy between a Gaussian measure and the Lebesgue measure in (
14
), which satisfies (
16
).

The limit of the Radon-Nikodym derivative
\frac{\mathrm{d}P^{\left(Q,\lambda\right)}_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}|\boldsymbol{Z}=\boldsymbol{z}}}{\mathrm{d}Q}
in (
25
), when
\lambda
tends to zero from the right, can be studied using the following set

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{\boldsymbol{z}}\left(\delta\right)

\displaystyle\triangleq

\displaystyle\left\{\boldsymbol{\theta}\in\mathcal{M}:\mathsf{L}_{\boldsymbol{z}}\left(\boldsymbol{\theta}\right)\leqslant\delta\right\},

(36)

where the function
\mathsf{L}_{\boldsymbol{z}}
is defined in (
3
) and
\delta\in[0,+\infty)
.
In particular consider the nonnegative real

\delta_{Q,\boldsymbol{z}}^{\star}\triangleq\inf\left\{\delta\in[0,+\infty):Q\left(\mathcal{L}_{\boldsymbol{z}}\left(\delta\right)\right)>0\right\}.

(37)

Let also
\mathcal{L}^{\star}_{Q,\boldsymbol{z}}
be the following level set of the empirical risk function
\mathsf{L}_{\boldsymbol{z}}
in (
3
):

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}^{\star}_{Q,\boldsymbol{z}}

\displaystyle\triangleq

\displaystyle\left\{\boldsymbol{\theta}\in\mathrm{supp}\,Q:\mathsf{L}_{\boldsymbol{z}}\left(\boldsymbol{\theta}\right)=\delta_{Q,\boldsymbol{z}}^{\star}\right\}.

(38)

Using this notation, the limit of the Radon-Nikodym derivative
\frac{\mathrm{d}P^{\left(Q,\lambda\right)}_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}|\boldsymbol{Z}=\boldsymbol{z}}}{\mathrm{d}Q}
in (
25
), when
\lambda
tends to zero from the right, is described by the following lemma.

Lemma 6

If
Q\left(\mathcal{L}^{\star}_{Q,\boldsymbol{z}}\right)>0
, with the set
\mathcal{L}^{\star}_{Q,\boldsymbol{z}}
in (
38
) and
Q
the
\sigma
-finite measure in (
19
), then for all
\boldsymbol{\theta}\in\mathrm{supp}\,Q
, the Radon-Nikodym derivative
\frac{\mathrm{d}P^{\left(Q,\lambda\right)}_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}|\boldsymbol{Z}=\boldsymbol{z}}}{\mathrm{d}Q}
in (
25
) satisfies

\displaystyle\lim_{\lambda\rightarrow 0^{+}}\frac{\mathrm{d}P^{\left(Q,\lambda\right)}_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}|\boldsymbol{Z}=\boldsymbol{z}}}{\mathrm{d}Q}\left(\boldsymbol{\theta}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{Q\left(\mathcal{L}^{\star}_{Q,\boldsymbol{z}}\right)}\mathds{1}_{\left\{\boldsymbol{\theta}\in\mathcal{L}^{\star}_{Q,\boldsymbol{z}}\right\}}.

(39)

Alternatively, if
Q\left(\mathcal{L}^{\star}_{Q,\boldsymbol{z}}\right)=0
. Then, for all
\boldsymbol{\theta}\in\mathrm{supp}\,Q
,

\displaystyle\lim_{\lambda\rightarrow 0^{+}}\frac{\mathrm{d}P^{\left(Q,\lambda\right)}_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}|\boldsymbol{Z}=\boldsymbol{z}}}{\mathrm{d}Q}\left(\boldsymbol{\theta}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left\{\begin{array}[]{cl}+\infty&\text{ if }\boldsymbol{\theta}\in\mathcal{L}^{\star}_{Q,\boldsymbol{z}}\\
0&\text{ otherwise}.\end{array}\right.

Proof:

The proof is presented in Appendix
G
.
∎

Consider that
Q\left(\mathcal{L}^{\star}_{Q,\boldsymbol{z}}\right)>0
, with
\mathcal{L}^{\star}_{Q,\boldsymbol{z}}
in (
38
). Under this assumption, from Lemma
6
, it holds that the probability measure
P^{\left(Q,\lambda\right)}_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}|\boldsymbol{Z}=\boldsymbol{z}}
asymptotically concentrates on the set
\mathcal{L}^{\star}_{Q,\boldsymbol{z}}
when
\lambda
tends to zero from the right.
More specifically, note that for all measurable sets
\mathcal{A}\subseteq\mathcal{L}^{\star}_{Q,\boldsymbol{z}}\cap\mathrm{supp}\,Q
, it holds that

\displaystyle\lim_{\lambda\rightarrow 0^{+}}P^{\left(Q,\lambda\right)}_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}|\boldsymbol{Z}=\boldsymbol{z}}\left(\mathcal{A}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\lim_{\lambda\rightarrow 0^{+}}\int_{\mathcal{A}}\frac{\mathrm{d}P^{\left(Q,\lambda\right)}_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}|\boldsymbol{Z}=\boldsymbol{z}}}{\mathrm{d}Q}\left(\boldsymbol{\theta}\right)\mathrm{d}Q\left(\boldsymbol{\theta}\right)

(43)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int\lim_{\lambda\rightarrow 0^{+}}\frac{\mathrm{d}P^{\left(Q,\lambda\right)}_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}|\boldsymbol{Z}=\boldsymbol{z}}}{\mathrm{d}Q}\left(\boldsymbol{\theta}\right)\mathds{1}_{\left\{\boldsymbol{\theta}\in\mathcal{A}\right\}}\mathrm{d}Q\left(\boldsymbol{\theta}\right)

(44)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int\frac{1}{Q\left(\mathcal{L}^{\star}_{Q,\boldsymbol{z}}\right)}\mathds{1}_{\left\{\boldsymbol{\theta}\in\mathcal{L}^{\star}_{Q,\boldsymbol{z}}\right\}}\mathds{1}_{\left\{\boldsymbol{\theta}\in\mathcal{A}\right\}}\mathrm{d}Q\left(\boldsymbol{\theta}\right)

(45)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{Q\left(\mathcal{L}^{\star}_{Q,\boldsymbol{z}}\right)}\int\mathds{1}_{\left\{\boldsymbol{\theta}\in\mathcal{A}\right\}}\mathrm{d}Q\left(\boldsymbol{\theta}\right)

(46)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{Q\left(\mathcal{A}\right)}{Q\left(\mathcal{L}^{\star}_{Q,\boldsymbol{z}}\right)},

(47)

where the equality in (
44
) follows from Lemma
2
and the dominated convergence theorem
[
59
, Theorem
2.6.9
]
. The equality in (
45
) follows from Lemma
6
.
In the particular case in which
\mathcal{A}=\mathcal{L}^{\star}_{Q,\boldsymbol{z}}
in (
47
), it holds that
\displaystyle\lim_{\lambda\rightarrow 0^{+}}P^{\left(Q,\lambda\right)}_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}|\boldsymbol{Z}=\boldsymbol{z}}\left(\mathcal{L}^{\star}_{Q,\boldsymbol{z}}\right)

=

1
, which verifies the asymptotic concentration of the probability measure
P^{\left(Q,\lambda\right)}_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}|\boldsymbol{Z}=\boldsymbol{z}}
on the set
\mathcal{L}^{\star}_{Q,\boldsymbol{z}}
.

Another interesting observation is that the Radon-Nikodym derivative
\frac{\mathrm{d}P^{\left(Q,\lambda\right)}_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}|\boldsymbol{Z}=\boldsymbol{z}}}{\mathrm{d}Q}
in (
25
) is a constant among the elements of the set
\mathcal{L}^{\star}_{Q,\boldsymbol{z}}
.
This can be assimilated to a uniform distribution of the probability among the elements of the set
\mathcal{L}^{\star}_{Q,\boldsymbol{z}}
in the limit when
\lambda
tends to zero from the right, as previously highlighted in
[
26
,
27
,
28
]
and
[
29
]
. This becomes more evident in the case in which the set
\mathcal{M}
is finite and
Q
is the counting measure. In such a case, the asymptotic probability of each of the elements in
\mathcal{L}^{\star}_{Q,\boldsymbol{z}}
when
\lambda
tends to zero from the right is
\frac{1}{\left|\mathcal{L}^{\star}_{Q,\boldsymbol{z}}\right|}
.

Consider now that
Q\left(\mathcal{L}^{\star}_{Q,\boldsymbol{z}}\right)=0
, with
\mathcal{L}^{\star}_{Q,\boldsymbol{z}}
in (
38
). Under this assumption, in the asymptotic regime when
\lambda\to 0
, the measure
P^{\left(Q,\lambda\right)}_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}|\boldsymbol{Z}=\boldsymbol{z}}
is not a probability measure but either the trivial measure or the infinite measure.
This is typically the case in which
\mathcal{M}=\mathds{R}^{d}
, the measure
Q
is absolutely continuous with respect to the Lebesgue measure, and the solution to the ERM problem in (
4
) has a unique solution on the support of
Q
, i.e.,
\mathcal{L}^{\star}_{Q,\boldsymbol{z}}=\mathcal{T}\left(\boldsymbol{z}\right)
and
\left|\mathcal{T}\left(\boldsymbol{z}\right)\right|=1
, which implies
Q(\mathcal{L}^{\star}_{Q,\boldsymbol{z}})~=~0
.

An interesting question, which is left out of the scope of this paper, is the rate at which
P^{\left(Q,\lambda\right)}_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}|\boldsymbol{Z}=\boldsymbol{z}}
converges to such limiting measure. The interested reader is referred to
[
26
,
29
]
, and references therein.

The following lemma shows that independently of whether the set
\mathcal{L}^{\star}_{Q,\boldsymbol{z}}
is negligible with respect to the measure
Q
, the limit when
\lambda
tends to zero from the right of
P^{\left(Q,\lambda\right)}_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}|\boldsymbol{Z}=\boldsymbol{z}}\left(\mathcal{L}^{\star}_{Q,\boldsymbol{z}}\right)
is equal to one.

Lemma 7

The measure
P^{\left(Q,\lambda\right)}_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}|\boldsymbol{Z}=\boldsymbol{z}}
in (
25
) and the set
\mathcal{L}^{\star}_{Q,\boldsymbol{z}}
in (
38
) satisfy,

\displaystyle\lim_{\lambda\rightarrow 0^{+}}P^{\left(Q,\lambda\right)}_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}|\boldsymbol{Z}=\boldsymbol{z}}\left(\mathcal{L}^{\star}_{Q,\boldsymbol{z}}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 1.

(48)

Proof:

The proof is presented in Appendix
H
.
∎Note that if the ERM problem in (
4
) possesses at least one solution and such solution is within the support of the measure
Q
, i.e.,
\mathcal{T}\left(\boldsymbol{z}\right)\cap\mathrm{supp}\,Q\neq\emptyset
, then, when
\lambda
tends to zero from the right, the probability measure
P^{\left(Q,\lambda\right)}_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}|\boldsymbol{Z}=\boldsymbol{z}}
asymptotically concentrates on the solution (or the set of solutions within the support of
Q
) to the ERM problem in
(
4
)
.
Alternatively, in the case in which
\mathcal{L}^{\star}_{Q,\boldsymbol{z}}\cap\mathcal{T}\left(\boldsymbol{z}\right)=\emptyset
, when
\lambda
tends to zero from the right, the probability measure
P^{\left(Q,\lambda\right)}_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}|\boldsymbol{Z}=\boldsymbol{z}}
asymptotically concentrates on a set that does not contain the set of solutions to the ERM problem in
(
4
)
.
This observation leads to the introduction to two new classes of reference measures, namely,
coherent
and
consistent
measures, in the following section.

IV
Reference Measures

This section introduces two classes of reference measures, namely
coherent
and
consistent
measures, and discusses the special case of Gibbs reference measures.

IV-A

Coherent and Consistent Reference Measures

A class of reference measures of particular importance to establish connections between the set of solutions to the ERM problem in (
4
) and the solution to the ERM-RER problem in (
19
) is that of
coherent
measures. Let
\rho^{\star}\geqslant 0
be the infimum of the empirical risk
\mathsf{L}_{\boldsymbol{z}}
in (
3
). That is,

\displaystyle\rho^{\star}\triangleq\inf\{\mathsf{L}_{\boldsymbol{z}}\left(\boldsymbol{\theta}\right):\boldsymbol{\theta}\in\mathcal{M}\}.

(49)

Using this notation, coherent measures are defined as follows.

Definition 3
(Coherent Measures)

The
\sigma
-finite measure
Q
in (
19
) is said to be coherent if, for all
\delta\in\left(\rho^{\star},+\infty\right)
, with
\rho^{\star}
in (
49
), it holds that

Q\left(\mathcal{L}_{\boldsymbol{z}}\left(\delta\right)\right)>0,

(50)

where the set
\mathcal{L}_{\boldsymbol{z}}\left(\delta\right)
is defined in (
36
).

When the reference measure
Q
in the EMR-RER problem in (
19
) is a coherent measure, it holds that for all
\delta>\rho^{\star}
, the set
\mathcal{L}_{\boldsymbol{z}}\left(\delta\right)
in (
36
) exhibits positive probability with respect to the probability measure
P^{\left(Q,\lambda\right)}_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}|\boldsymbol{Z}=\boldsymbol{z}}
in (
25
).
The following lemma highlights this observation.

Lemma 8

The probability measure
P^{\left(Q,\lambda\right)}_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}|\boldsymbol{Z}=\boldsymbol{z}}
in (
25
) satisfies for all
\delta\in\left(\rho^{\star},+\infty\right)
, with
\rho^{\star}
in (
49
), that

\displaystyle P^{\left(Q,\lambda\right)}_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}|\boldsymbol{Z}=\boldsymbol{z}}\left(\mathcal{L}_{\boldsymbol{z}}\left(\delta\right)\right)

\displaystyle>

\displaystyle 0,

(51)

with
\mathcal{L}_{\boldsymbol{z}}\left(\delta\right)
in (
36
),
if and only if the
\sigma
-finite measure
Q
in (
19
) is coherent.

Proof:

The proof is presented in Appendix
I
.
∎

Under the assumption that the ERM problem in (
4
) possesses a solution, it holds that

\displaystyle\min_{\theta\in\mathcal{M}}\mathsf{L}_{\boldsymbol{z}}\left(\boldsymbol{\theta}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\inf\{\mathsf{L}_{\boldsymbol{z}}\left(\boldsymbol{\theta}\right):\boldsymbol{\theta}\in\mathcal{M}\}.

(52)

Hence, when the
\sigma
-finite measure
Q
in (
19
) is coherent, then

\displaystyle\delta_{Q,\boldsymbol{z}}^{\star}=\rho^{\star},

(53)

with
\delta_{Q,\boldsymbol{z}}^{\star}
in (
37
) and
\rho^{\star}
in (
49
), which implies that

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}^{\star}_{Q,\boldsymbol{z}}

\displaystyle\subseteq

\displaystyle\mathcal{T}\left(\boldsymbol{z}\right),

(54)

with
\mathcal{T}\left(\boldsymbol{z}\right)
in (
5
) and
\mathcal{L}^{\star}_{Q,\boldsymbol{z}}
in (
38
).
This observation, together with Lemma
7
, leads to the following result.

Lemma 9

Assume that the ERM problem in (
4
) possesses a solution. Then, the probability measure
P^{\left(Q,\lambda\right)}_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}|\boldsymbol{Z}=\boldsymbol{z}}
in (
25
) and the sets
\mathcal{T}\left(\boldsymbol{z}\right)
in (
5
) and
\mathcal{L}^{\star}_{Q,\boldsymbol{z}}
in (
38
) satisfy

\displaystyle\lim_{\lambda\rightarrow 0^{+}}P^{\left(Q,\lambda\right)}_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}|\boldsymbol{Z}=\boldsymbol{z}}\left(\mathcal{L}^{\star}_{Q,\boldsymbol{z}}\cap\mathcal{T}\left(\boldsymbol{z}\right)\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 1,

(55)

if and only if the
\sigma
-finite measure
Q
in (
19
) is coherent.

Proof:

The proof follows by observing that if
Q
is a coherent measure and the ERM problem in (
4
) possesses a solution, the inclusion in (
54
) holds.
Thus, from Lemma
7
, the equality in (
55
) holds.
Alternatively, when the measure
Q
in (
19
) is noncoherent, then
\delta_{Q,\boldsymbol{z}}^{\star}>\rho^{\star}
, which implies that
\mathcal{L}^{\star}_{Q,\boldsymbol{z}}\cap\mathcal{T}\left(\boldsymbol{z}\right)=\emptyset
. Hence, from Lemma
7
, it follows that

\displaystyle\lim_{\lambda\rightarrow 0^{+}}P^{\left(Q,\lambda\right)}_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}|\boldsymbol{Z}=\boldsymbol{z}}\left(\mathcal{L}^{\star}_{Q,\boldsymbol{z}}\cap\mathcal{T}\left(\boldsymbol{z}\right)\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 0,

(56)

and completes the proof.
∎

The relevance of coherent measures in ERM-RER problems is well highlighted by Lemma
9
. Essentially, when the ERM problem in (
4
) possesses at least one solution, the concentration of the probability measure
P^{\left(Q,\lambda\right)}_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}|\boldsymbol{Z}=\boldsymbol{z}}
in (
25
) on the set (or a subset) of solutions to the ERM problem in (
4
) occurs asymptotically when
\lambda
tends to zero from the right, if only if the reference measure
Q
in (
19
) is coherent.
Nonetheless, such asymptotic concentration is not a guarantee that for strictly positive values of
\lambda
in (
19
), the set
\mathcal{T}\left(\boldsymbol{z}\right)
in (
5
) and the measure
P^{\left(Q,\lambda\right)}_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}|\boldsymbol{Z}=\boldsymbol{z}}
in (
25
) satisfy
P^{\left(Q,\lambda\right)}_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}|\boldsymbol{Z}=\boldsymbol{z}}\left(\mathcal{T}\left(\boldsymbol{z}\right)\right)>0
. In order to ensure this, another class of reference measures, known as
consistent measures
, is introduced.

Definition 4
(Consistent Measure)

The
\sigma
-finite measure
Q
in (
19
) is said to be consistent if
Q\left(\mathcal{L}^{\star}_{Q,\boldsymbol{z}}\right)>0
, with
\mathcal{L}^{\star}_{Q,\boldsymbol{z}}
in (
38
).

Note that every consistent measure is not necessarily coherent. For instance, if
Q
is consistent but
\delta_{Q,\boldsymbol{z}}^{\star}>\rho^{\star}
, with
\rho^{\star}
in (
49
) and
\delta_{Q,\boldsymbol{z}}^{\star}
in (
37
), then, for all
\delta\in(\rho^{\star},\delta_{Q,\boldsymbol{z}}^{\star})
, it follows that
Q\left(\mathcal{L}_{\boldsymbol{z}}\left(\delta\right)\right)
= 0, and thus,
Q
is not coherent.
Alternatively, every coherent measure is not necessarily consistent. For instance, if
\left|\mathcal{L}^{\star}_{Q,\boldsymbol{z}}\right|=1
and
Q
is coherent and absolutely continuous with respect to the Lebesgue measure, it follows that
Q\left(\mathcal{L}^{\star}_{Q,\boldsymbol{z}}\right)=0
, and thus,
Q
is not consistent.

The relevance of consistent measures is highlighted by the following lemma.

Lemma 10

The probability measure
P^{\left(Q,\lambda\right)}_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}|\boldsymbol{Z}=\boldsymbol{z}}
in (
25
) and the set
\mathcal{L}^{\star}_{Q,\boldsymbol{z}}
in (
38
) satisfy

\displaystyle P^{\left(Q,\lambda\right)}_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}|\boldsymbol{Z}=\boldsymbol{z}}\left(\mathcal{L}^{\star}_{Q,\boldsymbol{z}}\right)

\displaystyle>

\displaystyle 0,

(57)

if and only if the
\sigma
-finite measure
Q
in (
19
) is consistent.

Proof:

When
Q
is nonconsistent, it holds that
Q\left(\mathcal{L}^{\star}_{Q,\boldsymbol{z}}\right)=0
and thus, from the fact that the measure
P^{\left(Q,\lambda\right)}_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}|\boldsymbol{Z}=\boldsymbol{z}}
in (
25
) is absolutely continuous with respect to
Q
, it holds that
P^{\left(Q,\lambda\right)}_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}|\boldsymbol{Z}=\boldsymbol{z}}\left(\mathcal{L}^{\star}_{Q,\boldsymbol{z}}\right)=0
.
When
Q
is consistent, it holds that
Q\left(\mathcal{L}^{\star}_{Q,\boldsymbol{z}}\right)>0
. Moreover, for all
\boldsymbol{\theta}\in\mathcal{L}^{\star}_{Q,\boldsymbol{z}}
, it holds that
\mathsf{L}_{\boldsymbol{z}}\left(\boldsymbol{\theta}\right)<+\infty
and thus, from Lemma
2
, it follows that
\frac{\mathrm{d}P^{\left(Q,\lambda\right)}_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}|\boldsymbol{Z}=\boldsymbol{z}}}{\mathrm{d}Q}\left(\boldsymbol{\theta}\right)>0
. Hence,

\displaystyle P^{\left(Q,\lambda\right)}_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}|\boldsymbol{Z}=\boldsymbol{z}}\left(\mathcal{L}^{\star}_{Q,\boldsymbol{z}}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int_{\mathcal{L}^{\star}_{Q,\boldsymbol{z}}}\mathrm{d}P^{\left(Q,\lambda\right)}_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}|\boldsymbol{Z}=\boldsymbol{z}}\left(\boldsymbol{\theta}\right)

(58)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int_{\mathcal{L}^{\star}_{Q,\boldsymbol{z}}}\frac{\mathrm{d}P^{\left(Q,\lambda\right)}_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}|\boldsymbol{Z}=\boldsymbol{z}}}{\mathrm{d}Q}\left(\boldsymbol{\theta}\right)\mathrm{d}Q\left(\boldsymbol{\theta}\right)>0,

(59)

which completes the proof.
∎

The following lemma highlights a central property of consistent measures when the ERM problem in (
4
) possesses a solution.

Lemma 11

Assume that the ERM problem in (
4
) possesses a solution in the support of
Q
.
The probability measure
P^{\left(Q,\lambda\right)}_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}|\boldsymbol{Z}=\boldsymbol{z}}
in (
25
) and the sets
\mathcal{T}\left(\boldsymbol{z}\right)
in (
5
) and
\mathcal{L}^{\star}_{Q,\boldsymbol{z}}
in (
38
) satisfy

\displaystyle P^{\left(Q,\lambda\right)}_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}|\boldsymbol{Z}=\boldsymbol{z}}\left(\mathcal{L}^{\star}_{Q,\boldsymbol{z}}\cap\mathcal{T}\left(\boldsymbol{z}\right)\right)

\displaystyle>

\displaystyle 0,

(60)

if and only if the
\sigma
-finite measure
Q
in (
19
) is consistent.

Proof:

The proof follows from Lemma
10
by noticing that when the ERM problem in (
4
) possesses a solution in the support of
Q
, the inclusion in (
54
) holds.
∎

The distinction between coherent and consistent measures becomes more evident under certain conditions.
Consider the case in which
\mathcal{M}
is finite. In this case, if the solution to the ERM problem in (
4
) is in the support of the
\sigma
-finite measure
Q
, then
Q
is both coherent and consistent. This is essentially because all measurable singletons (models) in
\mathrm{supp}\,Q
exhibit positive measure with respect to
Q
. Alternatively, if the solution to the ERM problem in (
4
) is not in the support of
Q
, then
Q
is consistent but not coherent.
Consider the case in which
\mathcal{M}
is the set
\mathds{R}^{d}
; the loss function
\ell
in (
2
) is continuous; and the ERM problem in (
4
) admits a unique solution. In this case, any probability measure
Q
absolutely continuous with respect to the Lebesgue measure is a coherent measure, but it is not a consistent measure. Alternatively, if the set of solutions to the ERM problem in (
4
) exhibits positive Lebesgue measure, then, the measure
Q
is both coherent and consistent.

IV-B

Gibbs Reference Measures

In model selection, a natural idea is to proceed by successive approximations in the seek of lower computation complexity. From this perspective, one might wonder whether the solution to a
current instance of an ERM-RER problem might serve as reference measure for
the next instance. In this section, it is shown that this yields no
benefit. Composing two successive ERM-REM problems boils down
to a unique ERM-RER problem with the initial reference measure and a particular regularization factor.
Under the assumption that
\lambda\in\mathcal{K}_{Q,\boldsymbol{z}}
, with
\mathcal{K}_{Q,\boldsymbol{z}}
in (
23
), the problem of interest is:

\displaystyle\min_{P\in\triangle_{Q}\left(\mathcal{M},\mathscr{B}\left(\mathcal{M}\right)\right)}

\displaystyle\mathsf{R}_{\boldsymbol{z}}\left(P\right)+\alpha D\left(P\|P^{\left(Q,\lambda\right)}_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}|\boldsymbol{Z}=\boldsymbol{z}}\right),

(61a)

\displaystyle\mathrm{s.~t.}

\displaystyle\int\mathrm{d}P\left(\boldsymbol{\theta}\right)=1,

(61b)

where
\alpha>0
; the reference measure
P^{\left(Q,\lambda\right)}_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}|\boldsymbol{Z}=\boldsymbol{z}}
, which satisfies (
25
), is the solution of the ERM-RER problem in (
19
); and the functional
\mathsf{R}_{\boldsymbol{z}}
is defined in (
18
).
From Theorem
3
, the solution to the ERM-RER problem in (
61
), which is denoted by
P^{\left(P^{\left(Q,\lambda\right)}_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}|\boldsymbol{Z}=\boldsymbol{z}},\alpha\right)}_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}|\boldsymbol{Z}=\boldsymbol{z}}
, satisfies for all
\theta\in\mathrm{supp}\,Q
that

\displaystyle\frac{\mathrm{d}P^{\left(P^{\left(Q,\lambda\right)}_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}|\boldsymbol{Z}=\boldsymbol{z}},\alpha\right)}_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}|\boldsymbol{Z}=\boldsymbol{z}}}{\mathrm{d}P^{\left(Q,\lambda\right)}_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}|\boldsymbol{Z}=\boldsymbol{z}}}\left(\boldsymbol{\theta}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\exp\left(-K_{P^{\left(Q,\lambda\right)}_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}|\boldsymbol{Z}=\boldsymbol{z}},\boldsymbol{z}}\left(-\frac{1}{\alpha}\right)-\frac{1}{\alpha}\mathsf{L}_{\boldsymbol{z}}\left(\boldsymbol{\theta}\right)\right).

(62)

The log-partition functions
K_{Q,\boldsymbol{z}}
in (
22
) and
K_{P^{\left(Q,\lambda\right)}_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}|\boldsymbol{Z}=\boldsymbol{z}},\boldsymbol{z}}
in (
62
) are strongly related, as shown by the following lemma.

Lemma 12

The functions
K_{Q,\boldsymbol{z}}
in (
22
) and
K_{P^{\left(Q,\lambda\right)}_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}|\boldsymbol{Z}=\boldsymbol{z}},\boldsymbol{z}}
in (
62
) satisfy for all
t\in\mathds{R}
,

\displaystyle K_{P^{\left(Q,\lambda\right)}_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}|\boldsymbol{Z}=\boldsymbol{z}},\boldsymbol{z}}\left(t\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle K_{Q,\boldsymbol{z}}\left(t-\frac{1}{\lambda}\right)-K_{Q,\boldsymbol{z}}\left(-\frac{1}{\lambda}\right).

(63)

Moreover, for all
t\leqslant 0
,

\displaystyle K_{P^{\left(Q,\lambda\right)}_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}|\boldsymbol{Z}=\boldsymbol{z}},\boldsymbol{z}}\left(t\right)

\displaystyle\leqslant

\displaystyle 0.

(64)

Proof:

The proof of (
63
) relies on the fact that for all
t\in\left\{\nu\in\mathds{R}:K_{P^{\left(Q,\lambda\right)}_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}|\boldsymbol{Z}=\boldsymbol{z}},\boldsymbol{z}}(\nu)<\infty\right\}
, the function
K_{P^{\left(Q,\lambda\right)}_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}|\boldsymbol{Z}=\boldsymbol{z}},\boldsymbol{z}}
in (
62
) satisfies

\displaystyle K_{P^{\left(Q,\lambda\right)}_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}|\boldsymbol{Z}=\boldsymbol{z}},\boldsymbol{z}}\left(t\right)

(65)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\log\left(\int\exp\left(t\;\mathsf{L}_{\boldsymbol{z}}\left(\boldsymbol{\theta}\right)\right)\mathrm{d}P^{\left(Q,\lambda\right)}_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}|\boldsymbol{Z}=\boldsymbol{z}}(\boldsymbol{\theta})\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\log\left(\int\exp\left(t\;\mathsf{L}_{\boldsymbol{z}}\left(\boldsymbol{\theta}\right)\right)\frac{\mathrm{d}P^{\left(Q,\lambda\right)}_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}|\boldsymbol{Z}=\boldsymbol{z}}}{\mathrm{d}Q}(\boldsymbol{\theta})\;\mathrm{d}Q(\boldsymbol{\theta})\right)

(66)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\log\left(\int\exp\left(\left(t-\frac{1}{\lambda}\right)\;\mathsf{L}_{\boldsymbol{z}}\left(\boldsymbol{\theta}\right)-K_{Q,\boldsymbol{z}}\left(-\frac{1}{\lambda}\right)\right)\;\mathrm{d}Q(\boldsymbol{\theta})\right)

(67)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\log\left(\int\exp\left(\left(t-\frac{1}{\lambda}\right)\;\mathsf{L}_{\boldsymbol{z}}\left(\boldsymbol{\theta}\right)\right)\;\mathrm{d}Q(\boldsymbol{\theta})\right)-K_{Q,\boldsymbol{z}}\left(-\frac{1}{\lambda}\right)

(68)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle K_{Q,\boldsymbol{z}}\left(t-\frac{1}{\lambda}\right)-K_{Q,\boldsymbol{z}}\left(-\frac{1}{\lambda}\right),

(69)

where the equality in (
67
) follows from (
25
).
Moreover, from Lemma
15
, it follows that the function
K_{P^{\left(Q,\lambda\right)}_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}|\boldsymbol{Z}=\boldsymbol{z}},\boldsymbol{z}}
is continuous and nondecresing.
Let
s^{\star}\in\mathds{R}\cup\{+\infty\}
be defined by

\displaystyle s^{\star}

\displaystyle\triangleq

\displaystyle\sup\left\{\nu\in\mathds{R}:K_{P^{\left(Q,\lambda\right)}_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}|\boldsymbol{Z}=\boldsymbol{z}},\boldsymbol{z}}(\nu)<\infty\right\}.

(70)

If
s^{\star}=+\infty
, then for all
t\in\mathds{R}
,
K_{P^{\left(Q,\lambda\right)}_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}|\boldsymbol{Z}=\boldsymbol{z}},\boldsymbol{z}}\left(t\right)<+\infty
, and the proof of (
63
) is completed.

Alternatively, if
s^{\star}<+\infty
, it follows that for all
t>s^{\star}
,
K_{P^{\left(Q,\lambda\right)}_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}|\boldsymbol{Z}=\boldsymbol{z}},\boldsymbol{z}}(t)=+\infty
, which implies that
K_{Q,\boldsymbol{z}}\left(t-\frac{1}{\lambda}\right)=+\infty
, as the function
K_{Q,\boldsymbol{z}}
is also continuous (Lemma
15
) and
K_{Q,\boldsymbol{z}}\left(-\frac{1}{\lambda}\right)<\infty
(due to the choice of
\lambda
). Hence, in this case, the equality in (
63
) is of the form
+\infty=+\infty
.
This completes the proof of (
63
).

The proof of (
64
) follows by noticing that for all
t\leqslant 0
and for all
\boldsymbol{\theta}\in\mathrm{supp}\,Q
, it holds that
\exp\left(t\;\mathsf{L}_{\boldsymbol{z}}\left(\boldsymbol{\theta}\right)\right)\leqslant 1
. Hence,

\displaystyle K_{P^{\left(Q,\lambda\right)}_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}|\boldsymbol{Z}=\boldsymbol{z}},\boldsymbol{z}}\left(t\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\log\left(\int\exp\left(t\;\mathsf{L}_{\boldsymbol{z}}\left(\boldsymbol{\theta}\right)\right)\mathrm{d}P^{\left(Q,\lambda\right)}_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}|\boldsymbol{Z}=\boldsymbol{z}}(\boldsymbol{\theta})\right)

(71)

\displaystyle\leqslant

\displaystyle\log\left(\int\mathrm{d}P^{\left(Q,\lambda\right)}_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}|\boldsymbol{Z}=\boldsymbol{z}}(\boldsymbol{\theta})\right)

(72)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 0,

(73)

which completes the proof.
∎

The following lemma establishes that the solution to the ERM-RER problem in (
61
) is identical to the solution to another ERM-RER problem of the form

\displaystyle\min_{P\in\triangle_{Q}\left(\mathcal{M},\mathscr{B}\left(\mathcal{M}\right)\right)}

\displaystyle\mathsf{R}_{\boldsymbol{z}}\left(P\right)+\left(\frac{1}{\frac{1}{\alpha}+\frac{1}{\lambda}}\right)D\left(P\|Q\right),

(74a)

\displaystyle\mathrm{s.~t.}

\displaystyle\int\mathrm{d}P\left(\boldsymbol{\theta}\right)=1,

(74b)

with
\lambda\in\mathcal{K}_{Q,\boldsymbol{z}}
, with
\mathcal{K}_{Q,\boldsymbol{z}}
in (
23
), and whose solution, denoted by
P^{\left(Q,\frac{1}{\frac{1}{\lambda}+\frac{1}{\alpha}}\right)}_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}|\boldsymbol{Z}=\boldsymbol{z}}
, satisfies for all
\boldsymbol{\theta}\in\mathrm{supp}\,Q
,

\displaystyle\frac{\mathrm{d}P^{\left(Q,\frac{1}{\frac{1}{\lambda}+\frac{1}{\alpha}}\right)}_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}|\boldsymbol{Z}=\boldsymbol{z}}}{\mathrm{d}Q}(\boldsymbol{\theta})

(75)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\exp\left(-K_{Q,\boldsymbol{z}}\left(-\frac{1}{\lambda}-\frac{1}{\alpha}\right)-\left(\frac{1}{\lambda}+\frac{1}{\alpha}\right)\mathsf{L}_{\boldsymbol{z}}\left(\boldsymbol{\theta}\right)\right).

The formal statement is as follows.

Lemma 13

Let
\alpha\in\left(0,+\infty\right)
and
\lambda\in\mathcal{K}_{Q,\boldsymbol{z}}
, with
\mathcal{K}_{Q,\boldsymbol{z}}
in (
23
). Then, the probability measures
P^{\left(P^{\left(Q,\lambda\right)}_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}|\boldsymbol{Z}=\boldsymbol{z}},\alpha\right)}_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}|\boldsymbol{Z}=\boldsymbol{z}}
in (
62
) and
P^{\left(Q,\frac{1}{\frac{1}{\lambda}+\frac{1}{\alpha}}\right)}_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}|\boldsymbol{Z}=\boldsymbol{z}}
in (
75
) are identical.

Proof:

For all
\boldsymbol{\theta}\in\mathrm{supp}\,Q
,

\displaystyle\frac{\mathrm{d}P^{\left(P^{\left(Q,\lambda\right)}_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}|\boldsymbol{Z}=\boldsymbol{z}},\alpha\right)}_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}|\boldsymbol{Z}=\boldsymbol{z}}}{\mathrm{d}Q}(\boldsymbol{\theta})

(76)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{\mathrm{d}P^{\left(P^{\left(Q,\lambda\right)}_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}|\boldsymbol{Z}=\boldsymbol{z}},\alpha\right)}_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}|\boldsymbol{Z}=\boldsymbol{z}}}{\mathrm{d}P^{\left(Q,\lambda\right)}_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}|\boldsymbol{Z}=\boldsymbol{z}}}(\boldsymbol{\theta})\frac{\mathrm{d}P^{\left(Q,\lambda\right)}_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}|\boldsymbol{Z}=\boldsymbol{z}}}{\mathrm{d}Q}\left(\boldsymbol{\theta}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\exp\Bigg(-K_{P^{\left(Q,\lambda\right)}_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}|\boldsymbol{Z}=\boldsymbol{z}},\boldsymbol{z}}\left(-\frac{1}{\alpha}\right)-K_{Q,\boldsymbol{z}}\left(-\frac{1}{\lambda}\right)

\displaystyle-\left(\frac{1}{\alpha}+\frac{1}{\lambda}\right)\mathsf{L}_{\boldsymbol{z}}\left(\boldsymbol{\theta}\right)\Bigg)

(77)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\exp\left(-K_{Q,\boldsymbol{z}}\left(-\frac{1}{\alpha}-\frac{1}{\lambda}\right)-\left(\frac{1}{\alpha}+\frac{1}{\lambda}\right)\mathsf{L}_{\boldsymbol{z}}\left(\boldsymbol{\theta}\right)\right)

(78)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{\mathrm{d}P^{\left(Q,\frac{1}{\frac{1}{\lambda}+\frac{1}{\alpha}}\right)}_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}|\boldsymbol{Z}=\boldsymbol{z}}}{\mathrm{d}Q}(\boldsymbol{\theta}),

(79)

where
the equality in (
76
) follows from the fact that the measure
P^{\left(P^{\left(Q,\lambda\right)}_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}|\boldsymbol{Z}=\boldsymbol{z}},\alpha\right)}_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}|\boldsymbol{Z}=\boldsymbol{z}}
is absolutely continuous with respect to
P^{\left(Q,\lambda\right)}_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}|\boldsymbol{Z}=\boldsymbol{z}}
and
P^{\left(Q,\lambda\right)}_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}|\boldsymbol{Z}=\boldsymbol{z}}
is absolutely continuous with respect to the measure
Q
;
the equality in (
77
) follows from Lemma
12
; and
the equality in (
79
) follows from Theorem
3
.

For all measurable subsets
\mathcal{A}
of
\mathcal{M}
, the following holds:

\displaystyle P^{\left(P^{\left(Q,\lambda\right)}_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}|\boldsymbol{Z}=\boldsymbol{z}},\alpha\right)}_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}|\boldsymbol{Z}=\boldsymbol{z}}\left(\mathcal{A}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int_{\mathcal{A}}\frac{\mathrm{d}P^{\left(P^{\left(Q,\lambda\right)}_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}|\boldsymbol{Z}=\boldsymbol{z}},\alpha\right)}_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}|\boldsymbol{Z}=\boldsymbol{z}}}{\mathrm{d}Q}(\boldsymbol{\theta})\mathrm{d}Q(\boldsymbol{\theta})

(80)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int_{\mathcal{A}}\frac{\mathrm{d}P^{\left(Q,\frac{1}{\frac{1}{\lambda}+\frac{1}{\alpha}}\right)}_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}|\boldsymbol{Z}=\boldsymbol{z}}}{\mathrm{d}Q}\mathrm{d}Q(\boldsymbol{\theta})

(81)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int_{\mathcal{A}}\mathrm{d}P^{\left(Q,\frac{1}{\frac{1}{\lambda}+\frac{1}{\alpha}}\right)}_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}|\boldsymbol{Z}=\boldsymbol{z}}(\boldsymbol{\theta})

(82)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle P^{\left(Q,\frac{1}{\frac{1}{\lambda}+\frac{1}{\alpha}}\right)}_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}|\boldsymbol{Z}=\boldsymbol{z}}(\mathcal{A}),

(83)

where the equality in (
81
) follows from (
79
). This completes the proof.
∎

The following theorem establishes a relation between the solutions to the following optimization problems

\displaystyle\min_{P\in\triangle_{Q}\left(\mathcal{M},\mathscr{B}\left(\mathcal{M}\right)\right)}

\displaystyle\mathsf{R}_{\boldsymbol{z}}\left(P\right),

(84a)

s. t.

\displaystyle D\left(P\|P^{\left(Q,\lambda\right)}_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}|\boldsymbol{Z}=\boldsymbol{z}}\right)\leqslant c,\mbox{ and }

(84c)

\displaystyle\int\mathrm{d}P(\boldsymbol{\theta})=1,

and

\displaystyle\min_{P\in\triangle_{Q}\left(\mathcal{M},\mathscr{B}\left(\mathcal{M}\right)\right)}

\displaystyle\mathsf{R}_{\boldsymbol{z}}\left(P\right)+\omega D\left(P\|Q\right),

(85a)

\displaystyle\mathrm{s.~t.}

\displaystyle\int\mathrm{d}P\left(\boldsymbol{\theta}\right)=1,

(85b)

with

c>0
and
\omega\in\mathcal{K}_{Q,\boldsymbol{z}}
, with
\mathcal{K}_{Q,\boldsymbol{z}}
in (
23
), two constants;

P^{\left(Q,\lambda\right)}_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}|\boldsymbol{Z}=\boldsymbol{z}}
the probability measure in (
25
); and

\mathsf{R}_{\boldsymbol{z}}
the functional in (
18
).

From Theorem
3
, the solution to the ERM-RER problem in (
85
), which is denoted by
P^{\left(Q,\omega\right)}_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}|\boldsymbol{Z}=\boldsymbol{z}}
, satisfies for all
\theta\in\mathrm{supp}\,Q
that

\displaystyle\frac{\mathrm{d}P^{\left(Q,\omega\right)}_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}|\boldsymbol{Z}=\boldsymbol{z}}}{\mathrm{d}Q}\left(\boldsymbol{\theta}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\exp\left(-K_{Q,\boldsymbol{z}}\left(-\frac{1}{\omega}\right)-\frac{1}{\omega}\mathsf{L}_{\boldsymbol{z}}\left(\boldsymbol{\theta}\right)\right),

(86)

where the function
K_{Q,\boldsymbol{z}}
is in (
22
).

The following theorem formalizes the relation between both optimization problems.

Theorem 4

Assume that
c
and
\omega
in (
84
) and (
85
) satisfy

\displaystyle D\left(P^{\left(Q,\omega\right)}_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}|\boldsymbol{Z}=\boldsymbol{z}}\|P^{\left(Q,\lambda\right)}_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}|\boldsymbol{Z}=\boldsymbol{z}}\right)=c,

(87)

with
P^{\left(Q,\lambda\right)}_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}|\boldsymbol{Z}=\boldsymbol{z}}
and
P^{\left(Q,\omega\right)}_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}|\boldsymbol{Z}=\boldsymbol{z}}
being the probability measures in (
25
) and (
86
), respectively.
Then, the solution to the optimization problem in (
84
) is the probability measure
P^{\left(Q,\omega\right)}_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}|\boldsymbol{Z}=\boldsymbol{z}}
.

Proof:

The proof is presented in Appendix
J
.
∎

V
The Log-Partition Function

This section introduces some properties of the log-partition function
K_{Q,\boldsymbol{z}}
in (
22
) using the notion of
separable
empirical risk functions.

V-A

Separable Empirical Risk Functions

Separable empirical risk functions are defined with respect to a measure
P\in\triangle\left(\mathcal{M}\right)
.

Definition 5
(Separable Empirical Risk Function)

The empirical risk function
\mathsf{L}_{\boldsymbol{z}}
in (
3
) is said to be separable with respect to a
\sigma
-finite measure
P\in\triangle\left(\mathcal{M}\right)
, if there exist a positive real
c>0
and two subsets
\mathcal{A}
and
\mathcal{B}
of
\mathcal{M}
that are nonnegligible with respect to
P
, and for all
(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{1},\boldsymbol{\theta}_{2})\in\mathcal{A}\times\mathcal{B}
,

\displaystyle\mathsf{L}_{\boldsymbol{z}}\left(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{1}\right)

\displaystyle<c<

\displaystyle\mathsf{L}_{\boldsymbol{z}}\left(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{2}\right)<+\infty.

(88)

In a nutshell, a nonseparable empirical risk function with respect to the measure
Q
is a constant almost surely. More specifically, there exists a real
a\geqslant 0
, such that

Q\left(\left\{\boldsymbol{\theta}\in\mathcal{M}:\mathsf{L}_{\boldsymbol{z}}\left(\boldsymbol{\theta}\right)=a\right\}\right)=1.

(89)

From this perspective, nonseparable empirical risk functions exhibit little practical interest for model selection.

The definition of separability in Definition
5
and Lemma
3
lead to the following lemma.

Lemma 14

The empirical risk function
\mathsf{L}_{\boldsymbol{z}}
in (
3
) is separable with respect to the
\sigma
-finite measure
Q
in (
19
) if and only if it is separable with respect to the probability measure
P^{\left(Q,\lambda\right)}_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}|\boldsymbol{Z}=\boldsymbol{z}}
in (
25
).

Proof:

Consider first that the function
\mathsf{L}_{\boldsymbol{z}}
is separable with respect to the
\sigma
-finite measure
Q
. Hence, there exist a positive real
c>0
and two subsets
\mathcal{A}
and
\mathcal{B}
of
\mathcal{M}
that are nonnegligible with respect to
Q
, such that for all
(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{1},\boldsymbol{\theta}_{2})\in\mathcal{A}\times\mathcal{B}
the inequality in (
88
) holds. Hence, from (
88
) the following inequalities hold:

\displaystyle-\frac{1}{\lambda}\mathsf{L}_{\boldsymbol{z}}\left(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{1}\right)

\displaystyle>

\displaystyle-\frac{c}{\lambda}>-\frac{1}{\lambda}\mathsf{L}_{\boldsymbol{z}}\left(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{2}\right)>-\infty,\mbox{ and }

(90)

\displaystyle\exp\left(-\frac{1}{\lambda}\mathsf{L}_{\boldsymbol{z}}\left(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{1}\right)\right)

\displaystyle>

\displaystyle\exp\left(-\frac{c}{\lambda}\right)>\exp\left(-\frac{1}{\lambda}\mathsf{L}_{\boldsymbol{z}}\left(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{2}\right)\right)>0.

(91)

This implies that

\displaystyle\frac{\mathrm{d}P^{\left(Q,\lambda\right)}_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}|\boldsymbol{Z}=\boldsymbol{z}}}{\mathrm{d}Q}\left(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{1}\right)

\displaystyle>

\displaystyle\exp\left(-K_{Q,\boldsymbol{z}}\left(-\frac{1}{\lambda}\right)-\frac{c}{\lambda}\right)

(92)

\displaystyle>

\displaystyle\frac{\mathrm{d}P^{\left(Q,\lambda\right)}_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}|\boldsymbol{Z}=\boldsymbol{z}}}{\mathrm{d}Q}\left(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{2}\right)

(93)

\displaystyle>

\displaystyle 0.

(94)

Using the inequality in (
92
) and the facts that
Q\left(\mathcal{A}\right)>0
and
Q\left(\mathcal{B}\right)>0
, the following holds

\displaystyle P^{\left(Q,\lambda\right)}_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}|\boldsymbol{Z}=\boldsymbol{z}}\left(\mathcal{A}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int_{\mathcal{A}}\frac{\mathrm{d}P^{\left(Q,\lambda\right)}_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}|\boldsymbol{Z}=\boldsymbol{z}}}{\mathrm{d}Q}\left(\boldsymbol{\theta}\right)\mathrm{d}Q\left(\boldsymbol{\theta}\right)>0,

(95)

and

\displaystyle P^{\left(Q,\lambda\right)}_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}|\boldsymbol{Z}=\boldsymbol{z}}\left(\mathcal{B}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int_{\mathcal{B}}\frac{\mathrm{d}P^{\left(Q,\lambda\right)}_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}|\boldsymbol{Z}=\boldsymbol{z}}}{\mathrm{d}Q}\left(\boldsymbol{\theta}\right)\mathrm{d}Q\left(\boldsymbol{\theta}\right)>0.

(96)

which implies that the function
\mathsf{L}_{\boldsymbol{z}}
is separable with respect to the probability measure
P^{\left(Q,\lambda\right)}_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}|\boldsymbol{Z}=\boldsymbol{z}}
.

Consider now that the function
\mathsf{L}_{\boldsymbol{z}}
is separable with respect to the probability measure
P^{\left(Q,\lambda\right)}_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}|\boldsymbol{Z}=\boldsymbol{z}}
. Hence, there exist a positive real
c>0
and two subsets
\mathcal{A}
and
\mathcal{B}
of
\mathcal{M}
that are nonnegligible with respect to
P^{\left(Q,\lambda\right)}_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}|\boldsymbol{Z}=\boldsymbol{z}}
, such that for all
(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{1},\boldsymbol{\theta}_{2})\in\mathcal{A}\times\mathcal{B}
the inequality in (
88
) holds. More specifically,
P^{\left(Q,\lambda\right)}_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}|\boldsymbol{Z}=\boldsymbol{z}}\left(\mathcal{A}\right)>0
and
P^{\left(Q,\lambda\right)}_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}|\boldsymbol{Z}=\boldsymbol{z}}\left(\mathcal{B}\right)>0
.
From Lemma
2
and the inequality in (
88
), it follows that for all pairs
(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{1},\boldsymbol{\theta}_{2})\in\mathcal{A}\times\mathcal{B}
,
\frac{\mathrm{d}P^{\left(Q,\lambda\right)}_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}|\boldsymbol{Z}=\boldsymbol{z}}}{\mathrm{d}Q}\left(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{1}\right)>0
and
\frac{\mathrm{d}P^{\left(Q,\lambda\right)}_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}|\boldsymbol{Z}=\boldsymbol{z}}}{\mathrm{d}Q}\left(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{2}\right)>0
.
Hence, from the fact that
P^{\left(Q,\lambda\right)}_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}|\boldsymbol{Z}=\boldsymbol{z}}\left(\mathcal{A}\right)>0
and
P^{\left(Q,\lambda\right)}_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}|\boldsymbol{Z}=\boldsymbol{z}}\left(\mathcal{B}\right)>0
, it follows that
Q\left(\mathcal{A}\right)>0
and
Q\left(\mathcal{B}\right)>0
, which implies that the function
\mathsf{L}_{\boldsymbol{z}}
is separable with respect to the
\sigma
-finite measure
Q
.
This completes the proof.
∎Lemma
14
shows that separable empirical risk functions, and only these functions, lead to ERM-RER-optimal probability measures from which models are sampled with different probabilities. For the case of nonseparable empirical risk functions, all models are sampled from the ERM-RER-optimal probability measure with the same probability.

V-B

Properties of the Log-Partition Function

The log-partition function
K_{Q,\boldsymbol{z}}
in (
22
) is a nondecreasing continuous convex function as shown by the following lemmas.

Lemma 15

The function
K_{Q,\boldsymbol{z}}
in (
22
) is nondecreasing and differentiable infinitely many times in the interior of
\left\{t\in\mathds{R}:K_{Q,\boldsymbol{z}}(t)<+\infty\right\}
.

Proof:

The proof is presented in Appendix
K
.
∎

Lemma 16

The function
K_{Q,\boldsymbol{z}}
in (
22
) is convex in
\left\{t\in\mathds{R}:K_{Q,\boldsymbol{z}}(t)<+\infty\right\}
. Moreover, it is strictly convex if and only if the empirical risk function
\mathsf{L}_{\boldsymbol{z}}
in (
3
) is separable with respect to the
\sigma
-finite measure
Q
in (
19
).

Proof:

The proof is presented in Appendix
L
.
∎

In Lemma
15
, it has been established that the log-partition function
K_{Q,\boldsymbol{z}}
in (
22
) is differentiable infinitely many times in the interval
\left\{t\in\mathds{R}:K_{Q,\boldsymbol{z}}(t)<+\infty\right\}
.
Let the
m
-th derivative of the function
K_{Q,\boldsymbol{z}}
in (
22
) be denoted by
K^{(m)}_{Q,\boldsymbol{z}}:\mathds{R}\rightarrow\mathds{R}
, with
m\in\mathds{N}
. Hence, for all
s\in\mathcal{K}_{Q,\boldsymbol{z}}
,

\displaystyle K^{(m)}_{Q,\boldsymbol{z}}\left(-\frac{1}{s}\right)

\displaystyle\triangleq

\displaystyle\frac{\mathrm{d}^{m}}{\mathrm{d}t^{m}}K_{Q,\boldsymbol{z}}\left(t\right)\Bigr|_{t=-\frac{1}{s}}.

(97)

The following lemma provides explicit expressions for the first, second and third derivatives of the function
K_{Q,\boldsymbol{z}}
in (
22
).

Lemma 17

The first, second and third derivatives of the function
K_{Q,\boldsymbol{z}}
in (
22
), denoted respectively by
K^{(1)}_{Q,\boldsymbol{z}}
,
K^{(2)}_{Q,\boldsymbol{z}}
, and
K^{(3)}_{Q,\boldsymbol{z}}
, satisfy for all
\lambda\in\mathrm{int}\mathcal{K}_{Q,\boldsymbol{z}}
, with
\mathcal{K}_{Q,\boldsymbol{z}}
in (
23
),

\displaystyle K^{(1)}_{Q,\boldsymbol{z}}\left(-\frac{1}{\lambda}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int\mathsf{L}_{\boldsymbol{z}}\left(\boldsymbol{\theta}\right)\mathrm{d}P^{\left(Q,\lambda\right)}_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}|\boldsymbol{Z}=\boldsymbol{z}}(\boldsymbol{\theta}),

(98)
