\displaystyle K^{(2)}_{Q,\boldsymbol{z}}\left(-\frac{1}{\lambda}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int\left(\mathsf{L}_{\boldsymbol{z}}\left(\boldsymbol{\theta}\right)-K^{(1)}_{Q,\boldsymbol{z}}\left(-\frac{1}{\lambda}\right)\right)^{2}\mathrm{d}P^{\left(Q,\lambda\right)}_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}|\boldsymbol{Z}=\boldsymbol{z}}(\boldsymbol{\theta}),

(99)

\displaystyle K^{(3)}_{Q,\boldsymbol{z}}\left(-\frac{1}{\lambda}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int\left(\mathsf{L}_{\boldsymbol{z}}\left(\boldsymbol{\theta}\right)-K^{(1)}_{Q,\boldsymbol{z}}\left(-\frac{1}{\lambda}\right)\right)^{3}\mathrm{d}P^{\left(Q,\lambda\right)}_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}|\boldsymbol{Z}=\boldsymbol{z}}(\boldsymbol{\theta}),

(100)

where the function
\mathsf{L}_{\boldsymbol{z}}
is defined in (
3
) and the measure
P^{\left(Q,\lambda\right)}_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}|\boldsymbol{Z}=\boldsymbol{z}}
satisfies (
25
).

Proof:

The proof is presented in Appendix
M
.
∎From Lemma
17
, it follows that if
\boldsymbol{\Theta}\sim P^{\left(Q,\lambda\right)}_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}|\boldsymbol{Z}=\boldsymbol{z}}
, with
P^{\left(Q,\lambda\right)}_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}|\boldsymbol{Z}=\boldsymbol{z}}
in (
25
), the random variable

W\triangleq\mathsf{L}_{\boldsymbol{z}}\left(\boldsymbol{\Theta}\right),

(101)

with the function
\mathsf{L}_{\boldsymbol{z}}
in (
3
), possesses a mean, variance, and third cumulant that are equivalent to
K^{(1)}_{Q,\boldsymbol{z}}\left(-\frac{1}{\lambda}\right)
in (
98
),
K^{(2)}_{Q,\boldsymbol{z}}\left(-\frac{1}{\lambda}\right)
in (
99
), and
K^{(3)}_{Q,\boldsymbol{z}}\left(-\frac{1}{\lambda}\right)
in (
100
), respectively.

Note that if there exists a
\delta>0
such that the log-partition function
K_{Q,\boldsymbol{z}}
is differentiable within the open interval
(-\delta,\delta)
and
Q
in (
19
) is a probability measure, the function
K_{Q,\boldsymbol{z}}
is the cumulant generating function of the random variable

\displaystyle V\triangleq\mathsf{L}_{\boldsymbol{z}}\left(\boldsymbol{\Theta}\right),\mbox{ with $\boldsymbol{\Theta}\sim Q$}.

(102)

The following lemma leverages this observation.

Lemma 18

Assume that
Q
in (
19
) is a probability measure and that there exists real
\delta>0
such that the log-partition function
K_{Q,\boldsymbol{z}}
in (
22
) is differentiable within
(-\delta,\delta)
. Then, the first, second and third derivatives of
K_{Q,\boldsymbol{z}}
, denoted respectively by
K^{(1)}_{Q,\boldsymbol{z}}
,
K^{(2)}_{Q,\boldsymbol{z}}
, and
K^{(3)}_{Q,\boldsymbol{z}}
, satisfy

\displaystyle K^{(1)}_{Q,\boldsymbol{z}}\left(0\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int\mathsf{L}_{\boldsymbol{z}}\left(\boldsymbol{\theta}\right)\mathrm{d}Q(\boldsymbol{\theta}),

(103)

\displaystyle K^{(2)}_{Q,\boldsymbol{z}}\left(0\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int\left(\mathsf{L}_{\boldsymbol{z}}\left(\boldsymbol{\theta}\right)-K^{(1)}_{Q,\boldsymbol{z}}\left(0\right)\right)^{2}\mathrm{d}Q(\boldsymbol{\theta}),

(104)

\displaystyle K^{(3)}_{Q,\boldsymbol{z}}\left(0\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int\left(\mathsf{L}_{\boldsymbol{z}}\left(\boldsymbol{\theta}\right)-K^{(1)}_{Q,\boldsymbol{z}}\left(0\right)\right)^{3}\mathrm{d}Q(\boldsymbol{\theta}),

(105)

where the function
\mathsf{L}_{\boldsymbol{z}}
is defined in (
3
).

Proof:

The proof follows along the same arguments of the proof of Lemma
17
.
∎

The mean, variance, and third cumulant of the random variable
V
in (
102
) are
K^{(1)}_{Q,\boldsymbol{z}}\left(0\right)
in (
103
),
K^{(2)}_{Q,\boldsymbol{z}}\left(0\right)
in (
104
), and
K^{(3)}_{Q,\boldsymbol{z}}\left(0\right)
in (
105
), respectively.

VI
Expectation of the Empirical Risk

The mean of the random variable
W
in (
101
) is equivalent to the expectation of the empirical risk function
\mathsf{L}_{\boldsymbol{z}}
with respect to the probability measure
P^{\left(Q,\lambda\right)}_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}|\boldsymbol{Z}=\boldsymbol{z}}
in (
25
), which is equal to
\mathsf{R}_{\boldsymbol{z}}\left(P^{\left(Q,\lambda\right)}_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}|\boldsymbol{Z}=\boldsymbol{z}}\right)
, with the functional
\mathsf{R}_{\boldsymbol{z}}
in (
18
). Often,
\mathsf{R}_{\boldsymbol{z}}\left(P^{\left(Q,\lambda\right)}_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}|\boldsymbol{Z}=\boldsymbol{z}}\right)
is referred to as the ERM-RER-optimal expected empirical risk to emphasize that this is the expected value of the empirical risk when models are sampled from the solution of the ERM-RER problem in (
19
).
The following corollary of Lemma
17
formalizes this observation.

Corollary 2

The probability measure
P^{\left(Q,\lambda\right)}_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}|\boldsymbol{Z}=\boldsymbol{z}}
in (
25
) verifies that

\mathsf{R}_{\boldsymbol{z}}\left(P^{\left(Q,\lambda\right)}_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}|\boldsymbol{Z}=\boldsymbol{z}}\right)=K^{(1)}_{Q,\boldsymbol{z}}\left(-\frac{1}{\lambda}\right),

(106)

where the functional
\mathsf{R}_{\boldsymbol{z}}
and the function
K^{(1)}_{Q,\boldsymbol{z}}
are defined in (
18
) and (
98
), respectively.

The expected empirical risk
\mathsf{R}_{\boldsymbol{z}}\left(P^{\left(Q,\lambda\right)}_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}|\boldsymbol{Z}=\boldsymbol{z}}\right)
in (
106
) exhibits the following property.

Theorem 5

The expected empirical risk
\mathsf{R}_{\boldsymbol{z}}\left(P^{\left(Q,\lambda\right)}_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}|\boldsymbol{Z}=\boldsymbol{z}}\right)
in (
106
) is nondecreasing with
\lambda\in\mathcal{K}_{Q,\boldsymbol{z}}
, with
\mathcal{K}_{Q,\boldsymbol{z}}
in (
23
).
Moreover,
\mathsf{R}_{\boldsymbol{z}}\left(P^{\left(Q,\lambda\right)}_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}|\boldsymbol{Z}=\boldsymbol{z}}\right)
is strictly increasing with
\lambda\in\mathcal{K}_{Q,\boldsymbol{z}}
if and only if the function
\mathsf{L}_{\boldsymbol{z}}
in (
3
) is separable with respect to the measure
Q
.

Proof:

The proof is presented in Appendix
N
.
∎The expected empirical risk
\mathsf{R}_{\boldsymbol{z}}\left(P^{\left(Q,\lambda\right)}_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}|\boldsymbol{Z}=\boldsymbol{z}}\right)
in (
106
) has been shown to be nondecreasing with
\lambda
in
[
9
, Appendix E.4]
for the special case in which
Q
is a probability measure.

A question that arises from Theorem
5
is whether the value
\mathsf{R}_{\boldsymbol{z}}\left(P^{\left(Q,\lambda\right)}_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}|\boldsymbol{Z}=\boldsymbol{z}}\right)
in (
106
) can be made arbitrarily close to
\delta_{Q,\boldsymbol{z}}^{\star}
, with
\delta_{Q,\boldsymbol{z}}^{\star}
in (
37
), by making
\lambda
arbitrarily small.
The following lemma shows that the value
\mathsf{R}_{\boldsymbol{z}}\left(P^{\left(Q,\lambda\right)}_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}|\boldsymbol{Z}=\boldsymbol{z}}\right)
is often bounded away from
\delta_{Q,\boldsymbol{z}}^{\star}
, even for arbitrarily small values of
\lambda
.

Lemma 19

The expected empirical risk
\mathsf{R}_{\boldsymbol{z}}\left(P^{\left(Q,\lambda\right)}_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}|\boldsymbol{Z}=\boldsymbol{z}}\right)
in (
106
) satisfies,

\mathsf{R}_{\boldsymbol{z}}\left(P^{\left(Q,\lambda\right)}_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}|\boldsymbol{Z}=\boldsymbol{z}}\right)\geqslant\delta_{Q,\boldsymbol{z}}^{\star},

(107)

where
\delta_{Q,\boldsymbol{z}}^{\star}
is defined in (
37
).
Moreover, the inequality in (
107
) is strict if and only if the function
\mathsf{L}_{\boldsymbol{z}}
in (
3
) is separable with respect to the measure
Q
in (
19
).

Proof:

The proof is presented in Appendix
O
.
∎

In the asymptotic regime when
\lambda
tends to zero, the expected empirical risk
\mathsf{R}_{\boldsymbol{z}}\left(P^{\left(Q,\lambda\right)}_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}|\boldsymbol{Z}=\boldsymbol{z}}\right)
in (
106
) is equal to
\delta_{Q,\boldsymbol{z}}^{\star}
, as shown by the following lemma.

Theorem 6

The expected empirical risk
\mathsf{R}_{\boldsymbol{z}}\left(P^{\left(Q,\lambda\right)}_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}|\boldsymbol{Z}=\boldsymbol{z}}\right)
in (
106
) satisfies,

\displaystyle\lim_{\lambda\rightarrow 0^{+}}\mathsf{R}_{\boldsymbol{z}}\left(P^{\left(Q,\lambda\right)}_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}|\boldsymbol{Z}=\boldsymbol{z}}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\delta_{Q,\boldsymbol{z}}^{\star},

(108)

where
\delta_{Q,\boldsymbol{z}}^{\star}
is defined in (
37
).

Proof:

The proof is presented in Appendix
P
.
∎

The following lemma determines the value of the objective function of the ERM-RER problem in (
19
) when it is evaluated at its solution. This result appeared first in
[
11
, Lemma
3
]
.

Lemma 20
(Lemma
3
in
[
11
]
)

The probability measure
P^{\left(Q,\lambda\right)}_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}|\boldsymbol{Z}=\boldsymbol{z}}
in (
25
) and the
\sigma
-finite measure
Q
in (
19
) satisfy

\displaystyle-\lambda K_{Q,\boldsymbol{z}}\left(-\frac{1}{\lambda}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mathsf{R}_{\boldsymbol{z}}\left(P^{\left(Q,\lambda\right)}_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}|\boldsymbol{Z}=\boldsymbol{z}}\right)+\lambda D\left(P^{\left(Q,\lambda\right)}_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}|\boldsymbol{Z}=\boldsymbol{z}}\|Q\right)

(109)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mathsf{R}_{\boldsymbol{z}}\left(Q\right)-\lambda D\left(Q\|P^{\left(Q,\lambda\right)}_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}|\boldsymbol{Z}=\boldsymbol{z}}\right)

(110)

where
the functional
\mathsf{R}_{\boldsymbol{z}}
is defined in (
18
); and
the function
K_{Q,\boldsymbol{z}}
is defined in (
22
).

Proof:

From Theorem
3
, it follows that for all
\boldsymbol{\theta}\in\mathrm{supp}\,Q
,

\displaystyle\log\left(\frac{\mathrm{d}P^{\left(Q,\lambda\right)}_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}|\boldsymbol{Z}=\boldsymbol{z}}}{\mathrm{d}Q}\left(\boldsymbol{\theta}\right)\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-K_{Q,\boldsymbol{z}}\left(-\frac{1}{\lambda}\right)-\frac{1}{\lambda}\mathsf{L}_{\boldsymbol{z}}\left(\boldsymbol{\theta}\right),

(111)

where the function
\mathsf{L}_{\boldsymbol{z}}
is defined in (
3
).
Thus,

\displaystyle D\left(P^{\left(Q,\lambda\right)}_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}|\boldsymbol{Z}=\boldsymbol{z}}\|Q\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int\log\left(\frac{\mathrm{d}P^{\left(Q,\lambda\right)}_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}|\boldsymbol{Z}=\boldsymbol{z}}}{\mathrm{d}Q}\left(\boldsymbol{\theta}\right)\right)\mathrm{d}P^{\left(Q,\lambda\right)}_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}|\boldsymbol{Z}=\boldsymbol{z}}\left(\boldsymbol{\theta}\right)

(112)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-K_{Q,\boldsymbol{z}}\left(-\frac{1}{\lambda}\right)\hskip-2.15277pt-\hskip-2.15277pt\frac{1}{\lambda}\int\hskip-2.15277pt\mathsf{L}_{\boldsymbol{z}}\left(\boldsymbol{\theta}\right)\mathrm{d}P^{\left(Q,\lambda\right)}_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}|\boldsymbol{Z}=\boldsymbol{z}}\left(\boldsymbol{\theta}\right)

(113)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-K_{Q,\boldsymbol{z}}\left(-\frac{1}{\lambda}\right)-\frac{1}{\lambda}\mathsf{R}_{\boldsymbol{z}}\left(P^{\left(Q,\lambda\right)}_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}|\boldsymbol{Z}=\boldsymbol{z}}\right),

(114)

where the functional
\mathsf{R}_{\boldsymbol{z}}
is defined in (
18
). This completes the proof of (
109
).

From Lemma
3
and (
111
), it follows that

\displaystyle D\left(Q\|P^{\left(Q,\lambda\right)}_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}|\boldsymbol{Z}=\boldsymbol{z}}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\int\log\left(\frac{\mathrm{d}P^{\left(Q,\lambda\right)}_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}|\boldsymbol{Z}=\boldsymbol{z}}}{\mathrm{d}Q}\left(\boldsymbol{\theta}\right)\right)\mathrm{d}Q\left(\boldsymbol{\theta}\right)

(115)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle K_{Q,\boldsymbol{z}}\left(-\frac{1}{\lambda}\right)+\frac{1}{\lambda}\int\mathsf{L}_{\boldsymbol{z}}\left(\boldsymbol{\theta}\right)\mathrm{d}Q\left(\boldsymbol{\theta}\right)

(116)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle K_{Q,\boldsymbol{z}}\left(-\frac{1}{\lambda}\right)+\frac{1}{\lambda}\mathsf{R}_{\boldsymbol{z}}\left(Q\right),

(117)

which completes the proof of (
110
).
∎The following corollary of Lemma
20
characterizes the difference between the expected values of the random variables
W
and
V
in (
101
) and (
102
), respectively.

Corollary 3

If measures
Q
and
P^{\left(Q,\lambda\right)}_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}|\boldsymbol{Z}=\boldsymbol{z}}
in (
25
) are both probability measures, then,

\displaystyle\mathsf{R}_{\boldsymbol{z}}\left(Q\right)-\mathsf{R}_{\boldsymbol{z}}\left(P^{\left(Q,\lambda\right)}_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}|\boldsymbol{Z}=\boldsymbol{z}}\right)

(118)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\lambda\left(D\left(Q\|P^{\left(Q,\lambda\right)}_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}|\boldsymbol{Z}=\boldsymbol{z}}\right)+D\left(P^{\left(Q,\lambda\right)}_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}|\boldsymbol{Z}=\boldsymbol{z}}\|Q\right)\right).

The right-hand side of (
118
) is a symmetrized Kullback-Liebler divergence, also known as Jeffrey’s divergence
[
69
]
, between the measures
Q
and
P^{\left(Q,\lambda\right)}_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}|\boldsymbol{Z}=\boldsymbol{z}}
.
More importantly, when
Q
is a probability measure, it follows that
D\left(P^{\left(Q,\lambda\right)}_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}|\boldsymbol{Z}=\boldsymbol{z}}\|Q\right)\geqslant 0
and
D\left(Q\|P^{\left(Q,\lambda\right)}_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}|\boldsymbol{Z}=\boldsymbol{z}}\right)\geqslant 0
, which leads to the following corollary from Lemma
20
.

Corollary 4

If the
\sigma
-finite measure
Q
in (
19
) is a probability measure, then, the probability measure
P^{\left(Q,\lambda\right)}_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}|\boldsymbol{Z}=\boldsymbol{z}}
in (
25
) satisfies

\displaystyle\mathsf{R}_{\boldsymbol{z}}\left(P^{\left(Q,\lambda\right)}_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}|\boldsymbol{Z}=\boldsymbol{z}}\right)\leqslant\mathsf{R}_{\boldsymbol{z}}\left(Q\right),

(119)

where, the functional
\mathsf{R}_{\boldsymbol{z}}
is defined in (
18
).

VII
Variance of the Empirical Risk

In Lemma
15
, it has been established that if there exists a
\delta>0
such that the log-partition function
K_{Q,\boldsymbol{z}}
in (
22
) is finite within the open interval
(-\delta,\delta)
, the log-partition function
K_{Q,\boldsymbol{z}}
is differentiable infinitely many times within the interval
(-\infty,\delta)
. This together with the
mean value theorem

[
70
, Theorem
5.10
]
lead to the following characterization of the differences of the values
K^{(2)}_{Q,\boldsymbol{z}}\left(-\frac{1}{t}\right)
and
K^{(2)}_{Q,\boldsymbol{z}}\left(0\right)
, with
t>0
.

Lemma 21

If the measure
Q
in (
19
) is a probability measure and there exists a
\delta>0
such that the function
K_{Q,\boldsymbol{z}}
in (
22
) is differentiable within the open interval
(-\delta,\delta)
, then for all
t>0
,

\displaystyle K^{(2)}_{Q,\boldsymbol{z}}\left(-\frac{1}{t}\right)-K^{(2)}_{Q,\boldsymbol{z}}\left(0\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\frac{1}{t}K^{(3)}_{Q,\boldsymbol{z}}\left(-\frac{1}{\beta}\right)<+\infty,

(120)

for some
\beta\in\left(t,+\infty\right)
, where the functions
K^{(2)}_{Q,\boldsymbol{z}}
, and
K^{(3)}_{Q,\boldsymbol{z}}
are defined in (
97
).

Proof:

The proof is an immediate consequence of Lemma
15
and the mean value theorem
[
70
, Theorem
5.10
]
.
∎The relevance of Lemma
21
lies on the fact that
K^{(2)}_{Q,\boldsymbol{z}}\left(-\frac{1}{\lambda}\right)
and
K^{(2)}_{Q,\boldsymbol{z}}\left(0\right)
are the variances of the random variables
W
in (
101
) and
V
in (
102
). See Lemma
17
and Lemma
18
.
Under the assumptions of Lemma
21
, it follows that the function
K^{(3)}_{Q,\boldsymbol{z}}
is continuous in
(-\infty,\delta)
, where
\delta>0
. Hence, for all
t>0
, the function
K^{(3)}_{Q,\boldsymbol{z}}
achieves a maximum and a minimum within the interval
\left[-\frac{1}{t},0\right]
. Such extrema allow providing lower and upper bounds on the variance
K^{(2)}_{Q,\boldsymbol{z}}\left(-\frac{1}{\lambda}\right)
of the random variable
W
in terms of the variance
K^{(2)}_{Q,\boldsymbol{z}}\left(0\right)
of the random variable
V
, as shown hereunder.

Corollary 5

If the measure
Q
in (
19
) is a probability measure and there exists a
\delta>0
such that the function
K_{Q,\boldsymbol{z}}
in (
22
) is differentiable within the open interval
(-\delta,\delta)
, then for all
t>0
,

\displaystyle K^{(2)}_{Q,\boldsymbol{z}}\left(0\right)-\frac{1}{t}c_{2}

\displaystyle\leqslant K^{(2)}_{Q,\boldsymbol{z}}\left(-\frac{1}{t}\right)\leqslant

\displaystyle K^{(2)}_{Q,\boldsymbol{z}}\left(0\right)-\frac{1}{t}c_{1},

(121)

where,

\displaystyle c_{1}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\min_{s\in\left[-\frac{1}{t},0\right]}K^{(3)}_{Q,\boldsymbol{z}}\left(s\right)\mbox{ and }

(122)

\displaystyle c_{2}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\max_{s\in\left[-\frac{1}{t},0\right]}K^{(3)}_{Q,\boldsymbol{z}}\left(s\right),

(123)

and the functions
K^{(2)}_{Q,\boldsymbol{z}}
, and
K^{(3)}_{Q,\boldsymbol{z}}
are defined in (
97
).

The inequality in (
121
) reveals that under the assumptions of Corollary
5
, in the asymptotic regime when
t\to+\infty
, the variances of the random variables
W
in (
101
) and
V
in (
102
) are identical.
Additionally, unlike the means
K^{(1)}_{Q,\boldsymbol{z}}\left(-\frac{1}{\lambda}\right)
and
K^{(1)}_{Q,\boldsymbol{z}}\left(0\right)
of the random variables
W
and
V
, which satisfy
K^{(1)}_{Q,\boldsymbol{z}}\left(-\frac{1}{\lambda}\right)\leqslant K^{(1)}_{Q,\boldsymbol{z}}\left(0\right)
(Corollary
4
), their variances
K^{(2)}_{Q,\boldsymbol{z}}\left(-\frac{1}{\lambda}\right)
and
K^{(2)}_{Q,\boldsymbol{z}}\left(0\right)
might satisfy
K^{(2)}_{Q,\boldsymbol{z}}\left(-\frac{1}{\lambda}\right)<K^{(2)}_{Q,\boldsymbol{z}}\left(0\right)
or
K^{(2)}_{Q,\boldsymbol{z}}\left(-\frac{1}{\lambda}\right)\geqslant K^{(2)}_{Q,\boldsymbol{z}}\left(0\right)
depending on whether the function
K^{(3)}_{Q,\boldsymbol{z}}
is positive or negative within the interval
[-\frac{1}{\lambda},0]
.

Lemma
21
shows that the monotonicity of the expectation of the random variable
W
in (
101
) with respect to
\lambda
, stated by Theorem
5
, is not a property exhibited by the variance nor the third cumulant. The following example highlights this observation.

Example 1

Consider the ERM-RER problem in (
19
), under the assumption that
Q
is a probability measure and the empirical risk function
\mathsf{L}_{\boldsymbol{z}}
in (
3
) is such that for all
\boldsymbol{\theta}\in\mathcal{M}
,

\mathsf{L}_{\boldsymbol{z}}\left(\boldsymbol{\theta}\right)=\left\{\begin{array}[]{lcl}0&\text{ if }&\boldsymbol{\theta}\in\mathcal{A}\\
1&\text{ if }&\boldsymbol{\theta}\in\mathcal{M}\setminus\mathcal{A},\end{array}\right.

(124)

where the sets
\mathcal{A}\subset\mathcal{M}
and
\mathcal{M}\setminus\mathcal{A}
are nonnegligible with respect to the reference probability measure
Q
.
In this case, the function
K_{Q,\boldsymbol{z}}
in (
22
) satisfies for all
\lambda>0
,

\displaystyle K_{Q,\boldsymbol{z}}\left(-\frac{1}{\lambda}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\log\left(Q\left(\mathcal{A}\right)+\exp\left(-\frac{1}{\lambda}\right)\left(1-Q\left(\mathcal{A}\right)\right)\right).

(125)

The derivatives
K^{(1)}_{Q,\boldsymbol{z}}
,
K^{(2)}_{Q,\boldsymbol{z}}
, and
K^{(3)}_{Q,\boldsymbol{z}}
in (
97
) of the function
K_{Q,\boldsymbol{z}}
in (
125
) satisfy for all
\lambda>0
,

\displaystyle K^{(1)}_{Q,\boldsymbol{z}}\left(-\frac{1}{\lambda}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{\exp\left(-\frac{1}{\lambda}\right)\left(1-Q\left(\mathcal{A}\right)\right)}{Q\left(\mathcal{A}\right)+\exp\left(-\frac{1}{\lambda}\right)\left(1-Q\left(\mathcal{A}\right)\right)};

(126)

\displaystyle K^{(2)}_{Q,\boldsymbol{z}}\left(-\frac{1}{\lambda}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{Q\left(\mathcal{A}\right)\left(1-Q\left(\mathcal{A}\right)\right)\exp\left(-\frac{1}{\lambda}\right)}{\left(Q\left(\mathcal{A}\right)+\exp\left(-\frac{1}{\lambda}\right)\left(1-Q\left(\mathcal{A}\right)\right)\right)^{2}};\mbox{ and }

(127)

\displaystyle K^{(3)}_{Q,\boldsymbol{z}}\left(-\frac{1}{\lambda}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle K^{(2)}_{Q,\boldsymbol{z}}\left(-\frac{1}{\lambda}\right)

(128)

\displaystyle\left(\frac{Q\left(\mathcal{A}\right)-\left(1-Q\left(\mathcal{A}\right)\right)\exp\left(-\frac{1}{\lambda}\right)}{Q\left(\mathcal{A}\right)+\exp\left(-\frac{1}{\lambda}\right)\left(1-Q\left(\mathcal{A}\right)\right)}\right).

Note that
K^{(3)}_{Q,\boldsymbol{z}}\left(-\frac{1}{\lambda}\right)>0
if and only if

Q\left(\mathcal{A}\right)-\left(1-Q\left(\mathcal{A}\right)\right)\exp\left(-\frac{1}{\lambda}\right)>0.

(129)

Assume that
Q\left(\mathcal{A}\right)\geqslant\frac{1}{2}
. Thus, it holds that for all
\lambda>0
, the inequality in (
129
) is always satisfied. This follows from observing that for all
\lambda>0
,

\displaystyle\exp\left(-\frac{1}{\lambda}\right)<1\leqslant\frac{Q\left(\mathcal{A}\right)}{1-Q\left(\mathcal{A}\right)}.

(130)

Hence, if
Q\left(\mathcal{A}\right)\geqslant\frac{1}{2}
, for all decreasing sequences of positive reals
\lambda_{1}>\lambda_{2}>\ldots>0
, it holds that

\displaystyle\frac{1}{4}\geqslant K^{(2)}_{Q,\boldsymbol{z}}\left(-\frac{1}{\lambda_{1}}\right)>K^{(2)}_{Q,\boldsymbol{z}}\left(-\frac{1}{\lambda_{2}}\right)>\ldots>0.

(131)

Alternatively, assume that
Q\left(\mathcal{A}\right)<\frac{1}{2}
. In this case, the inequality in (
129
) is satisfied if and only if

\lambda<\left(\log\left(\frac{1-Q\left(\mathcal{A}\right)}{Q\left(\mathcal{A}\right)}\right)\right)^{-1}.

(132)

Hence, if
Q\left(\mathcal{A}\right)<\frac{1}{2}
, then for all decreasing sequences of positive reals

\left(\log\left(\frac{1-Q\left(\mathcal{A}\right)}{Q\left(\mathcal{A}\right)}\right)\right)^{-1}>\lambda_{1}>\lambda_{2}>\ldots>0,

it holds that

\displaystyle\frac{1}{4}>K^{(2)}_{Q,\boldsymbol{z}}\left(-\frac{1}{\lambda_{1}}\right)>K^{(2)}_{Q,\boldsymbol{z}}\left(-\frac{1}{\lambda_{2}}\right)>\ldots>0.

(133)

Moreover, for all decreasing sequences of positive reals

\lambda_{1}>\lambda_{2}>\ldots>\left(\log\left(\frac{1-Q\left(\mathcal{A}\right)}{Q\left(\mathcal{A}\right)}\right)\right)^{-1},

it holds that

\displaystyle K^{(2)}_{Q,\boldsymbol{z}}\left(-\frac{1}{\lambda_{1}}\right)<K^{(2)}_{Q,\boldsymbol{z}}\left(-\frac{1}{\lambda_{2}}\right)<\ldots<\frac{1}{4}.

(134)

The upperbound by
\frac{1}{4}
in (
131
), (
133
) and (
134
) follows by noticing that the value
K^{(2)}_{Q,\boldsymbol{z}}\left(-\frac{1}{\lambda}\right)
is maximized when
\lambda=\left(\log\left(\frac{1-Q\left(\mathcal{A}\right)}{Q\left(\mathcal{A}\right)}\right)\right)^{-1}
and
K^{(2)}_{Q,\boldsymbol{z}}\left(-\frac{1}{\lambda}\right)=\frac{1}{4}
.

Example
1
provides important insights on the choice of the reference measure
Q
. Note for instance that when the reference measure assigns a probability to the set of models
\mathcal{T}\left(\boldsymbol{z}\right)
in (
5
) that is greater than or equal to the probability of suboptimal models
\mathcal{M}\setminus\mathcal{T}\left(\boldsymbol{z}\right)
, i.e.,
Q\left(\mathcal{T}\left(\boldsymbol{z}\right)\right)\geqslant\frac{1}{2}
, the variance is strictly decreasing to zero when
\lambda
decreases. See for instance, Figure
1
and Figure
2
. That is, when the reference measure assigns higher probability to the set of solutions to the ERM problem in (
4
), the variance is monotone with respect to the parameter
\lambda
.

Alternatively, when the reference measure assigns a probability to the set
\mathcal{T}\left(\boldsymbol{z}\right)
that is smaller than the probability of the set
\mathcal{M}\setminus\mathcal{T}\left(\boldsymbol{z}\right)
, i.e.,
Q\left(\mathcal{T}\left(\boldsymbol{z}\right)\right)<\frac{1}{2}
, there exists a critical point for
\lambda
at
\left(\log\left(\frac{1-Q\left(\mathcal{A}\right)}{Q\left(\mathcal{A}\right)}\right)\right)^{-1}
. See for instance, Figure
3
. More importantly, such a critical point can be arbitrarily close to zero depending on the value
Q\left(\mathcal{A}\right)
.
The variance strictly decreases when
\lambda
decreases beyond the value
\left(\log\left(\frac{1-Q\left(\mathcal{A}\right)}{Q\left(\mathcal{A}\right)}\right)\right)^{-1}
. Otherwise, reducing
\lambda
above the value
\left(\log\left(\frac{1-Q\left(\mathcal{A}\right)}{Q\left(\mathcal{A}\right)}\right)\right)^{-1}
increases the variance.

In general, these observations suggest that reference measures
Q
that allocate small measures to the sets containing the set
\mathcal{T}\left(\boldsymbol{z}\right)
might require reducing the value
\lambda
beyond a small threshold in order to observe small values of
K^{(2)}_{Q,\boldsymbol{z}}\left(-\frac{1}{\lambda}\right)
, which is the variance of the random variable
W
, in (
101
).
These observations are central to understanding the concentration of probability that occurs when
\lambda
decreases to zero, as discussed in Section
IX
.

Fig. 1:
Mean
K^{(1)}_{Q,\boldsymbol{z}}\left(-\frac{1}{\lambda}\right)
, variance
K^{(2)}_{Q,\boldsymbol{z}}\left(-\frac{1}{\lambda}\right)
, and third central moment
K^{(3)}_{Q,\boldsymbol{z}}\left(-\frac{1}{\lambda}\right)
of the empirical risk in Example
1
, with
Q\left(\mathcal{A}\right)=\frac{3}{4}

Fig. 2:
Mean
K^{(1)}_{Q,\boldsymbol{z}}\left(-\frac{1}{\lambda}\right)
, variance
K^{(2)}_{Q,\boldsymbol{z}}\left(-\frac{1}{\lambda}\right)
, and third central moment
K^{(3)}_{Q,\boldsymbol{z}}\left(-\frac{1}{\lambda}\right)
of the empirical risk in Example
1
, with
Q\left(\mathcal{A}\right)=\frac{1}{2}

Fig. 3:
Mean
K^{(1)}_{Q,\boldsymbol{z}}\left(-\frac{1}{\lambda}\right)
, variance
K^{(2)}_{Q,\boldsymbol{z}}\left(-\frac{1}{\lambda}\right)
, and third central moment
K^{(3)}_{Q,\boldsymbol{z}}\left(-\frac{1}{\lambda}\right)
of the empirical risk in Example
1
, with
Q\left(\mathcal{A}\right)=\frac{1}{4}

VIII
Cumulant Generating Function of the Empirical Risk

Consider the transport of the measure
P^{\left(Q,\lambda\right)}_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}|\boldsymbol{Z}=\boldsymbol{z}}
in (
25
) from
\left(\mathcal{M},\mathscr{B}\left(\mathcal{M}\right)\right)
to
\left([0,+\infty],\mathscr{B}\left([0,+\infty]\right)\right)
through the function
\mathsf{L}_{\boldsymbol{z}}
in (
3
).
Denote the resulting probability measure in
\left([0,+\infty],\mathscr{B}\left([0,+\infty]\right)\right)
by
P^{(Q,\lambda)}_{W|\boldsymbol{Z}=\boldsymbol{z}}
.
That is, for all
\mathcal{A}\in\mathscr{B}\left([0,+\infty]\right)
,

P^{(Q,\lambda)}_{W|\boldsymbol{Z}=\boldsymbol{z}}\left(\mathcal{A}\right)=P^{\left(Q,\lambda\right)}_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}|\boldsymbol{Z}=\boldsymbol{z}}\left(\mathsf{L}_{\boldsymbol{z}}^{-1}\left(\mathcal{A}\right)\right),

(135)

where the term
\mathsf{L}_{\boldsymbol{z}}^{-1}\left(\mathcal{A}\right)
represents the set

\displaystyle\mathsf{L}_{\boldsymbol{z}}^{-1}\left(\mathcal{A}\right)

\displaystyle\triangleq

\displaystyle\left\{\boldsymbol{\nu}\in\mathcal{M}:\mathsf{L}_{\boldsymbol{z}}(\boldsymbol{\nu})\in\mathcal{A}\right\}.

(136)

Note that the random variable
W
in (
101
) induces the probability measure
P^{(Q,\lambda)}_{W|\boldsymbol{Z}=\boldsymbol{z}}
in
\left([0,+\infty],\mathscr{B}\left([0,+\infty]\right)\right)
.
The objective of this section is to study the properties of the cumulant generating function of the probability measure
P^{(Q,\lambda)}_{W|\boldsymbol{Z}=\boldsymbol{z}}
, denoted by
J_{\boldsymbol{z},Q,\lambda}:\mathds{R}\rightarrow\mathds{R}\cup\{+\infty\}
, which satisfies for all
t\in\mathds{R}
,

\displaystyle J_{\boldsymbol{z},Q,\lambda}(t)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\log\left(\int\exp\left(tw\right)\mathrm{d}P^{(Q,\lambda)}_{W|\boldsymbol{Z}=\boldsymbol{z}}(w)\right)

(137)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\log\left(\int\exp\left(t\,\mathsf{L}_{\boldsymbol{z}}\left(\boldsymbol{\theta}\right)\right)\mathrm{d}P^{\left(Q,\lambda\right)}_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}|\boldsymbol{Z}=\boldsymbol{z}}(\boldsymbol{\theta})\right),

(138)

where the equality in (
138
) follows from
[
59
, Theorem
1.6.12
]
.

The following lemma provides an expression for
J_{\boldsymbol{z},Q,\lambda}
in terms of the log-partition function
K_{Q,\boldsymbol{z}}
in (
22
).

Lemma 22

If
\lambda\in\mathcal{K}_{Q,\boldsymbol{z}}
, with
\mathcal{K}_{Q,\boldsymbol{z}}
in (
23
), then, the function
J_{\boldsymbol{z},Q,\lambda}
in (
137
), verifies for all
t\in\mathds{R}
,

\displaystyle J_{\boldsymbol{z},Q,\lambda}(t)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle K_{P^{\left(Q,\lambda\right)}_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}|\boldsymbol{Z}=\boldsymbol{z}},\boldsymbol{z}}\left(t\right)

(139)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle K_{Q,\boldsymbol{z}}\left(t-\frac{1}{\lambda}\right)-K_{Q,\boldsymbol{z}}\left(-\frac{1}{\lambda}\right)

(140)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{m=1}^{+\infty}\frac{t^{m}}{m!}K_{Q,\boldsymbol{z}}^{(m)}\left(-\frac{1}{\lambda}\right),

(141)

with the function
K_{Q,\boldsymbol{z}}
in (
22
) and the function
K_{Q,\boldsymbol{z}}^{(m)}
in (
97
).

Proof:

The proof of (
139
) follows immediately from (
22
) and (
138
).
The proof of (
140
) follows from Lemma
12
.
Finally, the proof of (
141
) follows by observing that a Taylor expansion of the function
K_{Q,\boldsymbol{z}}
in (
22
) at the point
-\frac{1}{\lambda}
, yields for all
t\in\left\{\nu\in\mathds{R}:K_{Q,\boldsymbol{z}}(\nu)<+\infty\right\}
,

\displaystyle K_{Q,\boldsymbol{z}}\left(t\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle K_{Q,\boldsymbol{z}}\left(-\frac{1}{\lambda}\right)+\sum_{s=1}^{+\infty}\frac{K^{(s)}_{Q,\boldsymbol{z}}\left(-\frac{1}{\lambda}\right)}{s!}\left(t+\frac{1}{\lambda}\right)^{s}.

(142)

Choosing
\alpha\in\left\{\nu\in\mathds{R}:K_{Q,\boldsymbol{z}}(\nu-\frac{1}{\lambda})<+\infty\right\}
such that
t=\alpha-\frac{1}{\lambda}
in (
142
) yields

\displaystyle K_{Q,\boldsymbol{z}}\left(\alpha-\frac{1}{\lambda}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle K_{Q,\boldsymbol{z}}\left(-\frac{1}{\lambda}\right)+\sum_{s=1}^{+\infty}\frac{\alpha^{s}}{s!}K^{(s)}_{Q,\boldsymbol{z}}\left(-\frac{1}{\lambda}\right),

(143)

which implies that for all
t\in\left\{\nu\in\mathds{R}:K_{Q,\boldsymbol{z}}(\nu-\frac{1}{\lambda})<+\infty\right\}
,

\displaystyle K_{Q,\boldsymbol{z}}\left(t-\frac{1}{\lambda}\right)-K_{Q,\boldsymbol{z}}\left(-\frac{1}{\lambda}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{s=1}^{+\infty}\frac{t^{s}}{s!}K^{(s)}_{Q,\boldsymbol{z}}\left(-\frac{1}{\lambda}\right).

(144)

Let
s^{\star}\in\mathds{R}\cup\{+\infty\}
be defined by

\displaystyle s^{\star}

\displaystyle\triangleq

\displaystyle\sup\left\{\nu\in\mathds{R}:K_{Q,\boldsymbol{z}}\left(\nu-\frac{1}{\lambda}\right)<\infty\right\}.

(145)

If
s^{\star}=+\infty
, then for all
t\in\mathds{R}
,
K_{Q,\boldsymbol{z}}\left(t-\frac{1}{\lambda}\right)-K_{Q,\boldsymbol{z}}\left(-\frac{1}{\lambda}\right)<+\infty
, and thus,

\displaystyle+\infty>J_{\boldsymbol{z},Q,\lambda}(t)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle K_{P^{\left(Q,\lambda\right)}_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}|\boldsymbol{Z}=\boldsymbol{z}},\boldsymbol{z}}\left(t\right)

(146)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle K_{Q,\boldsymbol{z}}\left(t-\frac{1}{\lambda}\right)-K_{Q,\boldsymbol{z}}\left(-\frac{1}{\lambda}\right)

(147)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{m=1}^{+\infty}\frac{t^{m}}{m!}K_{Q,\boldsymbol{z}}^{(m)}\left(-\frac{1}{\lambda}\right).

(148)

Alternatively, if
s^{\star}<+\infty
, it follows that for all
t>s^{\star}
,
K_{Q,\boldsymbol{z}}\left(t-\frac{1}{\lambda}\right)=+\infty
. From the fact that the function
K_{Q,\boldsymbol{z}}
is continuous (Lemma
15
) and
K_{Q,\boldsymbol{z}}\left(-\frac{1}{\lambda}\right)<\infty
(due to the fact that
\lambda\in\mathcal{K}_{Q,\boldsymbol{z}}
in (
23
)), it follows that

\displaystyle+\infty=J_{\boldsymbol{z},Q,\lambda}(t)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle K_{Q,\boldsymbol{z}}\left(t-\frac{1}{\lambda}\right)-K_{Q,\boldsymbol{z}}\left(-\frac{1}{\lambda}\right)

(149)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{m=1}^{+\infty}\frac{t^{m}}{m!}K_{Q,\boldsymbol{z}}^{(m)}\left(-\frac{1}{\lambda}\right),

(150)

which implies that
\sum_{m=1}^{+\infty}\frac{t^{m}}{m!}K_{Q,\boldsymbol{z}}^{(m)}\left(-\frac{1}{\lambda}\right)=+\infty
.
Hence, in this case, the equality in (
141
) is of the form
+\infty=+\infty
.
This completes the proof.
∎

Alternative expressions for
J_{\boldsymbol{z},Q,\lambda}
in (
137
) are provided hereunder.

Lemma 23

If
\lambda\in\mathcal{K}_{Q,\boldsymbol{z}}
, with
\mathcal{K}_{Q,\boldsymbol{z}}
in (
23
), then, the function
J_{\boldsymbol{z},Q,\lambda}
in (
137
), verifies for all
t\in\left(0,+\infty\right)
,

\displaystyle J_{\boldsymbol{z},Q,\lambda}\left(-\frac{1}{t}\right)

(151)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\frac{1}{t}\mathsf{R}_{\boldsymbol{z}}\left(P^{\left(Q,\frac{1}{\frac{1}{\lambda}+\frac{1}{t}}\right)}_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}|\boldsymbol{Z}=\boldsymbol{z}}\right)-D\left(P^{\left(Q,\frac{1}{\frac{1}{\lambda}+\frac{1}{t}}\right)}_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}|\boldsymbol{Z}=\boldsymbol{z}}\|P^{\left(Q,\lambda\right)}_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}|\boldsymbol{Z}=\boldsymbol{z}}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\frac{1}{t}\mathsf{R}_{\boldsymbol{z}}\left(P^{\left(Q,\lambda\right)}_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}|\boldsymbol{Z}=\boldsymbol{z}}\right)+D\left(P^{\left(Q,\lambda\right)}_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}|\boldsymbol{Z}=\boldsymbol{z}}\|P^{\left(Q,\frac{1}{\frac{1}{\lambda}+\frac{1}{t}}\right)}_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}|\boldsymbol{Z}=\boldsymbol{z}}\right)

(152)

\displaystyle\leqslant

\displaystyle 0,

(153)

where the functional
\mathsf{R}_{\boldsymbol{z}}
is in (
18
); the function
K_{P^{\left(Q,\lambda\right)}_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}|\boldsymbol{Z}=\boldsymbol{z}},\boldsymbol{z}}
is in (
62
); and the probability measures
P^{\left(Q,\lambda\right)}_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}|\boldsymbol{Z}=\boldsymbol{z}}
and
P^{\left(Q,\frac{1}{\frac{1}{\lambda}+\frac{1}{t}}\right)}_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}|\boldsymbol{Z}=\boldsymbol{z}}
are respectively in (
25
) and (
75
).

Proof:

The proof of (
151
) follows from (
109
) in Lemma
20
by observing that for all
t\in(0,+\infty)
,

\displaystyle-tK_{P^{\left(Q,\lambda\right)}_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}|\boldsymbol{Z}=\boldsymbol{z}},\boldsymbol{z}}\left(-\frac{1}{t}\right)

(154)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mathsf{R}_{\boldsymbol{z}}\left(P^{\left(P^{\left(Q,\lambda\right)}_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}|\boldsymbol{Z}=\boldsymbol{z}},t\right)}_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}|\boldsymbol{Z}=\boldsymbol{z}}\right)+tD\left(P^{\left(P^{\left(Q,\lambda\right)}_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}|\boldsymbol{Z}=\boldsymbol{z}},t\right)}_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}|\boldsymbol{Z}=\boldsymbol{z}}\|P^{\left(Q,\lambda\right)}_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}|\boldsymbol{Z}=\boldsymbol{z}}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mathsf{R}_{\boldsymbol{z}}\left(P^{\left(Q,\frac{1}{\frac{1}{\lambda}+\frac{1}{t}}\right)}_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}|\boldsymbol{Z}=\boldsymbol{z}}\right)+tD\left(P^{\left(Q,\frac{1}{\frac{1}{\lambda}+\frac{1}{t}}\right)}_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}|\boldsymbol{Z}=\boldsymbol{z}}\|P^{\left(Q,\lambda\right)}_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}|\boldsymbol{Z}=\boldsymbol{z}}\right),

(155)

where the equality in (
155
) follows from Lemma
13
.
The proof of (
152
) follows from (
110
) in Lemma
20
by observing that for all
t\in(0,+\infty)
,

\displaystyle-tK_{P^{\left(Q,\lambda\right)}_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}|\boldsymbol{Z}=\boldsymbol{z}},\boldsymbol{z}}\left(-\frac{1}{t}\right)

(156)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mathsf{R}_{\boldsymbol{z}}\left(P^{\left(Q,\lambda\right)}_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}|\boldsymbol{Z}=\boldsymbol{z}}\right)-tD\left(P^{\left(Q,\lambda\right)}_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}|\boldsymbol{Z}=\boldsymbol{z}}\|P^{\left(P^{\left(Q,\lambda\right)}_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}|\boldsymbol{Z}=\boldsymbol{z}},t\right)}_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}|\boldsymbol{Z}=\boldsymbol{z}}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mathsf{R}_{\boldsymbol{z}}\left(P^{\left(Q,\lambda\right)}_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}|\boldsymbol{Z}=\boldsymbol{z}}\right)-tD\left(P^{\left(Q,\lambda\right)}_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}|\boldsymbol{Z}=\boldsymbol{z}}\|P^{\left(Q,\frac{1}{\frac{1}{\lambda}+\frac{1}{t}}\right)}_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}|\boldsymbol{Z}=\boldsymbol{z}}\right),

(157)

where the equality in (
157
) follows from Lemma
13
,
which completes the proof.
∎

From Lemma
15
and Lemma
22
, it follows that the function
J_{\boldsymbol{z},Q,\lambda}
in (
137
) is increasing and differentiable infinitely many times in the interior of
\left\{t\in\mathds{R}:K_{Q,\boldsymbol{z}}\left(t-\frac{1}{\lambda}\right)<+\infty\right\}
. Moreover, note that
\left(-\infty,\frac{1}{\lambda}\right]\subset\left\{t\in\mathds{R}:K_{Q,\boldsymbol{z}}\left(t-\frac{1}{\lambda}\right)<+\infty\right\}
.
Denote by
J^{(m)}_{\boldsymbol{z},Q,\lambda}:\mathds{R}\rightarrow\mathds{R}\cup\{+\infty\}
, with
m\in\mathds{N}
, the
m
-th derivative of the function
J_{\boldsymbol{z},Q,\lambda}
in (
137
). That is, for all
s\in\mathds{R}
,

J^{(m)}_{\boldsymbol{z},Q,\lambda}(s)=\frac{\mathrm{d}^{m}}{\mathrm{d}t^{m}}J_{\boldsymbol{z},Q,\lambda}(t)\Bigr|_{t=s}.

(158)

From Lemma
22
, it follows that for all
m\in\mathds{N}
, and for all
\alpha\in\mathds{R}
, the following holds,

J^{(m)}_{\boldsymbol{z},Q,\lambda}(\alpha)=K^{(m)}_{Q,\boldsymbol{z}}\left(\alpha-\frac{1}{\lambda}\right),

(159)

where the function
K^{(m)}_{Q,\boldsymbol{z}}
denotes the
m
-th derivative of the function
K_{Q,\boldsymbol{z}}
in (
22
). See for instance, Lemma
17
.
The equality in (
159
) establishes a relation between the cumulant generating function
J_{\boldsymbol{z},Q,\lambda}
and the function
K_{Q,\boldsymbol{z}}
. This observation becomes an alternative proof to Lemma
17
.

The following theorem presents the relation between the cumulant generating function
J_{\boldsymbol{z},Q,\lambda}
and the functions
K^{(1)}_{Q,\boldsymbol{z}}
and
K^{(2)}_{Q,\boldsymbol{z}}
in (
98
) and (
99
).

Theorem 7

For all
\alpha\in\mathds{R}
, the function
J_{\boldsymbol{z},Q,\lambda}
in (
137
) verifies the following equality

J_{\boldsymbol{z},Q,\lambda}(\alpha)=\alpha K^{(1)}_{Q,\boldsymbol{z}}\left(-\frac{1}{\lambda}\right)+\frac{1}{2}\alpha^{2}K^{(2)}_{Q,\boldsymbol{z}}\left(\xi_{\alpha}\right)

(160)

with

\displaystyle\xi_{\alpha}\in\left(\min\left\{-\frac{1}{\lambda},\alpha-\frac{1}{\lambda}\right\},\max\left\{-\frac{1}{\lambda},\alpha-\frac{1}{\lambda}\right\}\right),

(161)

where the functions
K^{(1)}_{Q,\boldsymbol{z}}
and
K^{(2)}_{Q,\boldsymbol{z}}
are defined in (
98
) and (
99
), respectively.

Proof:

From Lemma
15
, it follows that the function
K_{Q,\boldsymbol{z}}
is differentiable infinitely many times in the interior of
\left\{t\in\mathds{R}:K_{Q,\boldsymbol{z}}(t)<+\infty\right\}
.
Then, a Taylor expansion of the function
K_{Q,\boldsymbol{z}}
in (
22
) at the point
-\frac{1}{\lambda}
yields for all
t\in\left\{\nu\in\mathds{R}:K_{Q,\boldsymbol{z}}(\nu)<+\infty\right\}
,

\displaystyle K_{Q,\boldsymbol{z}}\left(t\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle K_{Q,\boldsymbol{z}}\left(-\frac{1}{\lambda}\right)

(162)

\displaystyle+\sum_{s=1}^{+\infty}\frac{1}{s!}\left(t+\frac{1}{\lambda}\right)^{s}K^{(s)}_{Q,\boldsymbol{z}}\left(-\frac{1}{\lambda}\right).

Choosing
t=\alpha-\frac{1}{\lambda}
, with
\alpha\in\left\{\nu\in\mathds{R}:K_{Q,\boldsymbol{z}}(\nu-\frac{1}{\lambda})<+\infty\right\}
in (
162
), it holds from the Taylor-Lagrange theorem
[
71
, Theorem
2.5.4
]
that

\displaystyle K_{Q,\boldsymbol{z}}\left(\alpha-\frac{1}{\lambda}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle K_{Q,\boldsymbol{z}}\left(-\frac{1}{\lambda}\right)+\alpha K^{(1)}_{Q,\boldsymbol{z}}\left(-\frac{1}{\lambda}\right)

(163)

\displaystyle+\frac{1}{2}\alpha^{2}K^{(2)}_{Q,\boldsymbol{z}}\left(\xi\right),

where
\xi\in\left(\min\{-\frac{1}{\lambda},\alpha-\frac{1}{\lambda}\},\max\{-\frac{1}{\lambda},\alpha-\frac{1}{\lambda}\},\right)
. The proof is completed by noticing that from Lemma
22
, it holds that
J_{\boldsymbol{z},Q,\lambda}(\alpha)=K_{Q,\boldsymbol{z}}\left(\alpha-\frac{1}{\lambda}\right)-K_{Q,\boldsymbol{z}}\left(-\frac{1}{\lambda}\right)
.
∎

In (
160
), the term
\xi_{\alpha}
depends on
\alpha
via (
161
). The focus is now on the term
K^{(2)}_{Q,\boldsymbol{z}}\left(\xi_{\alpha}\right)
, when
\alpha\in\left\{t\in\mathds{R}:J_{\boldsymbol{z},Q,\lambda}(t)<+\infty\right\}
.

Theorem 8

The function
J_{\boldsymbol{z},Q,\lambda}
in (
137
) verifies the following inequality for all
\alpha\in\left\{t\in\mathds{R}:J_{\boldsymbol{z},Q,\lambda}(t)<+\infty\right\}
,

J_{\boldsymbol{z},Q,\lambda}(\alpha)\leqslant\alpha K^{(1)}_{Q,\boldsymbol{z}}\left(-\frac{1}{\lambda}\right)+\frac{1}{2}\alpha^{2}\beta_{Q,\boldsymbol{z}}^{2}

(164)

where
\beta_{Q,\boldsymbol{z}}
satisfies

\displaystyle\beta_{Q,\boldsymbol{z}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sup\left\{\sqrt{K^{(2)}_{Q,\boldsymbol{z}}\left(\alpha\right)}:\alpha\in\left(-\infty,b-\frac{1}{\lambda}\right)\right\},

(165)

with

\displaystyle b

\displaystyle\triangleq

\displaystyle\sup\left\{t\in\mathds{R}:J_{\boldsymbol{z},Q,\lambda}(t)<+\infty\right\},

(166)

and the functions
K^{(1)}_{Q,\boldsymbol{z}}
and
K^{(2)}_{Q,\boldsymbol{z}}
are defined in (
98
) and (
99
), respectively.

Proof:

From Lemma
22
, it holds that

\displaystyle\left\{t\in\mathds{R}:J_{\boldsymbol{z},Q,\lambda}(t)<+\infty\right\}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left\{t\in\mathds{R}:K_{Q,\boldsymbol{z}}\left(t-\frac{1}{\lambda}\right)<+\infty\right\},

which
implies that the set
\left\{t\in\mathds{R}:J_{\boldsymbol{z},Q,\lambda}(t)<+\infty\right\}
contains an interval of the form
\left(-\infty,b\right)
and is contained within an interval of the form
\left(-\infty,b\right]
, with
b
in (
166
). This follows from the fact that the function
K_{Q,\boldsymbol{z}}
is continuous and nondecreasing (Lemma
15
).
Note also that
K_{Q,\boldsymbol{z}}(0)=0
, and thus,
\frac{1}{\lambda}\in\left\{t\in\mathds{R}:J_{\boldsymbol{z},Q,\lambda}(t)<+\infty\right\}
, which implies that
b>\frac{1}{\lambda}
.

From Theorem
7
, it follows that for all
\alpha\in\left(-\infty,b\right)
,

\displaystyle J_{\boldsymbol{z},Q,\lambda}(\alpha)

\displaystyle\leqslant

\displaystyle\alpha K^{(1)}_{Q,\boldsymbol{z}}\left(-\frac{1}{\lambda}\right)+\frac{1}{2}\alpha^{2}\sigma^{2}_{\alpha},

(167)

where
\sigma_{\alpha}
is

\displaystyle\sigma_{\alpha}

\displaystyle\triangleq

\displaystyle\sup\left\{\sqrt{K^{(2)}_{Q,\boldsymbol{z}}\left(\xi\right)}:\xi\in\left(\min\left\{-\,\frac{1}{\lambda},\alpha-\,\frac{1}{\lambda}\right\},\max\left\{-\,\frac{1}{\lambda},\alpha-\,\frac{1}{\lambda}\right\}\right)\right\}.

In the asymptotic regime, when
\alpha\to-\infty
, it holds that

\displaystyle\lim_{\alpha\to-\infty}\sigma_{\alpha}

\displaystyle\triangleq

\displaystyle\sup\left\{\sqrt{K^{(2)}_{Q,\boldsymbol{z}}\left(\xi\right)}:\xi\in\left(-\infty,-\frac{1}{\lambda}\right)\right\},

and when
\alpha\to b
, it holds that

\displaystyle\lim_{\alpha\to b}\sigma_{\alpha}

\displaystyle\triangleq

\displaystyle\sup\left\{\sqrt{K^{(2)}_{Q,\boldsymbol{z}}\left(\xi\right)}:\xi\in\left(-\frac{1}{\lambda},b-\frac{1}{\lambda}\right)\right\}.

Thus, for all
\alpha\in\left(-\infty,b\right)
,

\displaystyle\sigma_{\alpha}

\displaystyle\leqslant

\displaystyle\beta_{Q,\boldsymbol{z}},

with
\beta_{Q,\boldsymbol{z}}
defined in (
165
), which completes the proof.
∎The main implication of Theorem
8
is that if
\beta_{Q,\boldsymbol{z}}
in (
165
) is finite, the random variable
W
in (
101
) is a sub-Gaussian random variable with sub-Gaussianity parameter
\beta_{Q,\boldsymbol{z}}

[
57
, Section
2.3
]
.
This follows by noticing that the function
J_{\boldsymbol{z},Q,\lambda}
in (
138
) is the cumulant generating function of the random variable
W
. Hence, whenever
\beta_{Q,\boldsymbol{z}}
is finite, when the models are sampled from the ERM-RER optimal measure
P^{\left(Q,\lambda\right)}_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}|\boldsymbol{Z}=\boldsymbol{z}}
in (
25
), the empirical risk with respect to the dataset
\boldsymbol{z}
is a sub-Gaussian random variable with sub-Gaussianity parameter
\beta_{Q,\boldsymbol{z}}
.
The following corollary of Theorem
8
highlights this observation.

Corollary 6

If
\beta_{Q,\boldsymbol{z}}
in (
165
) is finite, the random variable
W
in (
101
) is a sub-Gaussian random variable with sub-Gaussianity parameter
\beta_{Q,\boldsymbol{z}}
in (
165
).

IX
Concentration of Probability

Consider the following set,

\displaystyle\mathcal{N}_{Q,\boldsymbol{z}}(\lambda)

\displaystyle\triangleq

\displaystyle\left\{\boldsymbol{\theta}\in\mathcal{M}:\mathsf{L}_{\boldsymbol{z}}\left(\boldsymbol{\theta}\right)\leqslant\mathsf{R}_{\boldsymbol{z}}\left(P^{\left(Q,\lambda\right)}_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}|\boldsymbol{Z}=\boldsymbol{z}}\right)\right\},

(168)

where the function
\mathsf{L}_{\boldsymbol{z}}
is defined by (
3
); the functional
\mathsf{R}_{\boldsymbol{z}}
is defined by (
18
); and the probability measure
P^{\left(Q,\lambda\right)}_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}|\boldsymbol{Z}=\boldsymbol{z}}
is in (
25
).
This section introduces two results.
First, in Theorem
9
, it is shown that when
\lambda
tends to zero, the set
\mathcal{N}_{Q,\boldsymbol{z}}(\lambda)
forms an indexed family of sets that is monotonic and decreases to the set

\mathcal{N}_{Q,\boldsymbol{z}}^{\star}\triangleq\mathcal{L}_{\boldsymbol{z}}\left(\delta_{Q,\boldsymbol{z}}^{\star}\right),

(169)

where
\delta_{Q,\boldsymbol{z}}^{\star}
is defined in (
37
); and the set
\mathcal{L}_{\boldsymbol{z}}(\delta_{Q,\boldsymbol{z}}^{\star})
is defined in (
36
).
Second, in Theorem
10
, it is shown that the probability
P^{\left(Q,\lambda\right)}_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}|\boldsymbol{Z}=\boldsymbol{z}}(\mathcal{N}_{Q,\boldsymbol{z}}(\lambda))
strictly increases when
\lambda
tends to zero. More importantly, in Theorem
11
, it is shown that the limit of the probability
P^{\left(Q,\lambda\right)}_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}|\boldsymbol{Z}=\boldsymbol{z}}(\mathcal{N}_{Q,\boldsymbol{z}}(\lambda))
, when
\lambda\to 0
, is equal to one. These observations justify referring to the set
\mathcal{N}_{Q,\boldsymbol{z}}^{\star}
as the
limit set
.
These observations are complementary to those stated in Section
III-B
and Section
III-C
.
This section ends by showing that the probability measure
P^{\left(Q,\lambda\right)}_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}|\boldsymbol{Z}=\boldsymbol{z}}
concentrates on a specific subset
\mathcal{L}^{\star}_{Q,\boldsymbol{z}}
in (
38
) of the set
\mathcal{N}_{Q,\boldsymbol{z}}^{\star}
. At the light of this observation, the set
\mathcal{L}^{\star}_{Q,\boldsymbol{z}}
is referred to as the
nonnegligible limit set
.
Finally, it is shown that when the
\sigma
-finite measure
Q
in (
19
) is coherent, the sets
\mathcal{N}_{Q,\boldsymbol{z}}^{\star}
and

\mathcal{L}^{\star}_{Q,\boldsymbol{z}}
are identical.

IX-A

The Limit Set

The set
\mathcal{N}_{Q,\boldsymbol{z}}(\lambda)
in (
168
), with
\lambda\in\mathcal{K}_{Q,\boldsymbol{z}}
and
\mathcal{K}_{Q,\boldsymbol{z}}
in (
23
), contains all the models that induce an empirical risk that is smaller than or equal to
\mathsf{R}_{\boldsymbol{z}}\left(P^{\left(Q,\lambda\right)}_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}|\boldsymbol{Z}=\boldsymbol{z}}\right)
, i.e., the ERM-RER-optimal expected empirical risk in (
106
). This observation unveils the existence of a relation between the set
\mathcal{N}_{Q,\boldsymbol{z}}^{\star}
in (
169
) and the set
\mathcal{T}\left(\boldsymbol{z}\right)
in (
5
), as shown by the following lemma.

Lemma 24

The set
\mathcal{N}_{Q,\boldsymbol{z}}^{\star}
in (
169
) satisfies

\mathcal{T}\left(\boldsymbol{z}\right)\subseteq\mathcal{N}_{Q,\boldsymbol{z}}^{\star},

(170)

where the set
\mathcal{T}\left(\boldsymbol{z}\right)
is in (
5
).
Moreover,

\mathcal{T}\left(\boldsymbol{z}\right)=\mathcal{N}_{Q,\boldsymbol{z}}^{\star},

(171)

if and only if
(a)
the ERM problem in (
4
) possesses a solution; and
(b)
the reference measure
Q
in (
19
) is coherent.

Proof:

If the set
\mathcal{T}\left(\boldsymbol{z}\right)
in (
5
) is empty, the inclusion in (
170
) is trivially true.
Assume that
\left|\mathcal{T}\left(\boldsymbol{z}\right)\right|>0
. Hence, the proof of the inclusion in (
170
) follows from observing that for all
\boldsymbol{\theta}\in\mathcal{T}\left(\boldsymbol{z}\right)
, it holds that
\mathsf{L}_{\boldsymbol{z}}\left(\boldsymbol{\theta}\right)=\rho^{\star}\leqslant\delta_{Q,\boldsymbol{z}}^{\star}
, with
\delta_{Q,\boldsymbol{z}}^{\star}
in (
37
) and
\rho^{\star}
in (
49
). Hence,
\boldsymbol{\theta}\in\mathcal{N}_{Q,\boldsymbol{z}}^{\star}
. This completes the proof of the inclusion in (
170
).

The proof of the equality in (
171
) is presented in two parts. In the first part, it is proved that if (
171
) holds, then the ERM problem in (
4
) possesses a solution and the measure
Q
is coherent.
The second part proves the converse.
The proof of the first part is as follows. Under the assumption that
\mathcal{T}\left(\boldsymbol{z}\right)=\mathcal{N}_{Q,\boldsymbol{z}}^{\star}
holds, it follows that
\delta_{Q,\boldsymbol{z}}^{\star}=\rho^{\star}
, with
\rho^{\star}
in (
49
), which implies that the ERM problem in (
4
) possesses a solution. Moreover, for all
\delta\in\left(\rho^{\star},+\infty\right)
, it holds that
Q\left(\mathcal{L}_{\boldsymbol{z}}\left(\delta\right)\right)>0
, which verifies that the measure
Q
is coherent and completes the proof of the first part.
The proof of the second part is as follows. Under the assumption that the ERM problem in (
4
) possesses a solution and the measure
Q
is coherent, it follows that
\delta_{Q,\boldsymbol{z}}^{\star}=\rho^{\star}
. Hence,
\mathcal{T}\left(\boldsymbol{z}\right)=\mathcal{N}_{Q,\boldsymbol{z}}^{\star}
, which completes the proof of the second part.
∎

The following theorem highlights that the set
\mathcal{N}_{Q,\boldsymbol{z}}\left(\lambda\right)
is decreasing with
\lambda
.

Theorem 9

For all
(\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2})\in\mathcal{K}_{Q,\boldsymbol{z}}\times\mathcal{K}_{Q,\boldsymbol{z}}
, with
\mathcal{K}_{Q,\boldsymbol{z}}
in (
23
) and
\lambda_{1}>\lambda_{2}
, the sets
\mathcal{N}_{Q,\boldsymbol{z}}\left(\lambda_{1}\right)
and
\mathcal{N}_{Q,\boldsymbol{z}}\left(\lambda_{2}\right)
in (
168
) satisfy

\displaystyle\mathcal{M}\supseteq\mathcal{N}_{Q,\boldsymbol{z}}(\lambda_{1})\supseteq\mathcal{N}_{Q,\boldsymbol{z}}(\lambda_{2})\supseteq\mathcal{N}_{Q,\boldsymbol{z}}^{\star},

(172)

with
\mathcal{N}_{Q,\boldsymbol{z}}^{\star}
being the set defined in (
169
).
Moreover, if the empirical risk function
\mathsf{L}_{\boldsymbol{z}}
in (
3
) is continuous on
\mathcal{M}
and separable with respect to the measure
Q
in (
19
), then,

\displaystyle\mathcal{M}\supset\mathcal{N}_{Q,\boldsymbol{z}}(\lambda_{1})\supset\mathcal{N}_{Q,\boldsymbol{z}}(\lambda_{2})\supset\mathcal{N}_{Q,\boldsymbol{z}}^{\star}.

(173)

Proof:

The proof is presented in Appendix
Q
.
∎An interesting observation is that for all
\lambda\in\mathcal{K}_{Q,\boldsymbol{z}}
, with
\mathcal{K}_{Q,\boldsymbol{z}}
in (
23
), only a subset of
\mathcal{N}_{Q,\boldsymbol{z}}\left(\lambda\right)
might exhibit nonzero probability with respect to the measure
P^{\left(Q,\lambda\right)}_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}|\boldsymbol{Z}=\boldsymbol{z}}
in (
25
).
Consider for instance that the measure
Q
in (
19
) is noncoherent (Definition
3
). That is,
\delta_{Q,\boldsymbol{z}}^{\star}>\rho^{\star}
, with
\delta_{Q,\boldsymbol{z}}^{\star}
in (
37
) and
\rho^{\star}
in (
49
). Thus, for all
\gamma\in\left(\rho^{\star},\delta_{Q,\boldsymbol{z}}^{\star}\right)
, it holds that
Q\left(\mathcal{L}_{\boldsymbol{z}}\left(\gamma\right)\right)=0
, with the set
\mathcal{L}_{\boldsymbol{z}}(\cdot)
in (
36
).
From Lemma
3
, this implies that for all
\gamma\in\left(\rho^{\star},\delta_{Q,\boldsymbol{z}}^{\star}\right)
, the measure
P^{\left(Q,\lambda\right)}_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}|\boldsymbol{Z}=\boldsymbol{z}}
in (
25
) satisfies
P^{\left(Q,\lambda\right)}_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}|\boldsymbol{Z}=\boldsymbol{z}}\left(\mathcal{L}_{\boldsymbol{z}}\left(\gamma\right)\right)=0
, while verifying that
\mathcal{L}_{\boldsymbol{z}}\left(\gamma\right)\subseteq\mathcal{N}_{Q,\boldsymbol{z}}\left(\lambda\right)
.
These observations lead to the analysis of the asymptotic concentration of probability in the following section.

IX-B

The Nonnegligible Limit Set

The first step in the analysis of the asymptotic concentration of the probability measure
P^{\left(Q,\lambda\right)}_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}|\boldsymbol{Z}=\boldsymbol{z}}
in (
25
)
is to show that the probability
P^{\left(Q,\lambda\right)}_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}|\boldsymbol{Z}=\boldsymbol{z}}\left(\mathcal{N}_{Q,\boldsymbol{z}}(\lambda)\right)
increases when
\lambda
tends to zero, as shown by the following theorem.

Theorem 10

For all
(\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2})\in\mathcal{K}_{Q,\boldsymbol{z}}\times\mathcal{K}_{Q,\boldsymbol{z}}
, with
\mathcal{K}_{Q,\boldsymbol{z}}
in (
23
) and
\lambda_{1}>\lambda_{2}
, assume that the measures
P^{\left(Q,\lambda_{1}\right)}_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}|\boldsymbol{Z}=\boldsymbol{z}}
and
P^{\left(Q,\lambda_{2}\right)}_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}|\boldsymbol{Z}=\boldsymbol{z}}
satisfy (
25
) with
\lambda=\lambda_{1}
and
\lambda=\lambda_{2}
, respectively. Then, the set
\mathcal{N}_{Q,\boldsymbol{z}}\left(\lambda_{2}\right)
in (
168
) satisfies

\displaystyle 0<P^{\left(Q,\lambda_{1}\right)}_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}|\boldsymbol{Z}=\boldsymbol{z}}(\mathcal{N}_{Q,\boldsymbol{z}}(\lambda_{2}))\leqslant P^{\left(Q,\lambda_{2}\right)}_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}|\boldsymbol{Z}=\boldsymbol{z}}(\mathcal{N}_{Q,\boldsymbol{z}}(\lambda_{2})),

(174)

where strict inequality holds if and only if the function
\mathsf{L}_{\boldsymbol{z}}
is separable with respect to the
\sigma
-finite measure
Q
.

Proof:

The proof is presented in Appendix
R
.
∎

The following lemma highlights a case in which a stronger concentration of probability is observed.

Lemma 25

Let the function
\mathsf{L}_{\boldsymbol{z}}
in (
3
) be separable with respect to the
\sigma
-finite measure
Q
in (
19
). Let also
\left(\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2}\right)\in\mathcal{K}_{Q,\boldsymbol{z}}\times\mathcal{K}_{Q,\boldsymbol{z}}
, with
\mathcal{K}_{Q,\boldsymbol{z}}
in (
23
), be two positive reals such that
\lambda_{1}>\lambda_{2}
and

Q\bigg(\mathcal{N}_{Q,\boldsymbol{z}}\left(\lambda_{1}\right)\cap\left(\mathcal{N}_{Q,\boldsymbol{z}}\left(\lambda_{2}\right)\right)^{\textsf{c}}\bigg)=0,

(175)

with the complement with respect to the set of models
\mathcal{M}
.
Then, two measures
P^{\left(Q,\lambda_{1}\right)}_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}|\boldsymbol{Z}=\boldsymbol{z}}
and
P^{\left(Q,\lambda_{2}\right)}_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}|\boldsymbol{Z}=\boldsymbol{z}}
that respectively satisfy (
25
) with
\lambda=\lambda_{1}
and
\lambda=\lambda_{2}
verify that

\displaystyle P^{\left(Q,\lambda_{1}\right)}_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}|\boldsymbol{Z}=\boldsymbol{z}}(\mathcal{N}_{Q,\boldsymbol{z}}(\lambda_{1}))<P^{\left(Q,\lambda_{2}\right)}_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}|\boldsymbol{Z}=\boldsymbol{z}}(\mathcal{N}_{Q,\boldsymbol{z}}(\lambda_{2})),

(176)

where, the set
\mathcal{N}_{Q,\boldsymbol{z}}\left(\cdot\right)
is defined in (
168
).

Proof:

The proof is presented in Appendix
S
.
∎

The following example shows the relevance of Lemma
25
in the case in which the empirical risk function
\mathsf{L}_{\boldsymbol{z}}
in (
3
) is a simple function and separable with respect to the
\sigma
-finite measure
Q
in (
19
).

Example 2

Consider Example
1
. Note that, for all
\lambda>0
,

0<\mathsf{R}_{\boldsymbol{z}}\left(P^{\left(Q,\lambda\right)}_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}|\boldsymbol{Z}=\boldsymbol{z}}\right)<1,

(177)

where
\mathsf{R}_{\boldsymbol{z}}\left(P^{\left(Q,\lambda\right)}_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}|\boldsymbol{Z}=\boldsymbol{z}}\right)
is the ERM-RER-optimal expected empirical risk in (
106
).
The equality in (
177
) implies that given two reals
\lambda_{1}
and
\lambda_{2}
such that
\lambda_{1}>\lambda_{2}>0
, it holds that,

\displaystyle\mathcal{N}_{Q,\boldsymbol{z}}\left(\lambda_{1}\right)\cap\left(\mathcal{N}_{Q,\boldsymbol{z}}\left(\lambda_{2}\right)\right)^{\textsf{c}}

(178)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left\{\boldsymbol{\nu}\in\mathcal{M}:\mathsf{R}_{\boldsymbol{z}}\left(P^{\left(Q,\lambda_{2}\right)}_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}|\boldsymbol{Z}=\boldsymbol{z}}\right)<\mathsf{L}_{\boldsymbol{z}}\left(\boldsymbol{\nu}\right)\leqslant\mathsf{R}_{\boldsymbol{z}}\left(P^{\left(Q,\lambda_{1}\right)}_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}|\boldsymbol{Z}=\boldsymbol{z}}\right)\right\}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\emptyset,

(179)

and moreover,
\mathcal{N}_{Q,\boldsymbol{z}}(\lambda_{1})=\mathcal{N}_{Q,\boldsymbol{z}}(\lambda_{2})
. Finally, from Lemma
25
,

\displaystyle P^{\left(Q,\lambda_{1}\right)}_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}|\boldsymbol{Z}=\boldsymbol{z}}(\mathcal{N}_{Q,\boldsymbol{z}}(\lambda_{1}))<P^{\left(Q,\lambda_{2}\right)}_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}|\boldsymbol{Z}=\boldsymbol{z}}(\mathcal{N}_{Q,\boldsymbol{z}}(\lambda_{2})).

(180)

The main result of this section is presented by the following theorem.

Theorem 11

The probability measure
P^{\left(Q,\lambda\right)}_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}|\boldsymbol{Z}=\boldsymbol{z}}
in (
25
) satisfies

\lim_{\lambda\rightarrow 0^{+}}P^{\left(Q,\lambda\right)}_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}|\boldsymbol{Z}=\boldsymbol{z}}\left(\mathcal{N}_{Q,\boldsymbol{z}}\left(\lambda\right)\right)=1,

(181)

where, the set
\mathcal{N}_{Q,\boldsymbol{z}}\left(\lambda\right)
is defined in (
168
).

Proof:

The proof follows immediately from Lemma
7
and by noticing that for all
\lambda\in\mathcal{K}_{Q,\boldsymbol{z}}
, with
\mathcal{K}_{Q,\boldsymbol{z}}
in (
23
), the sets
\mathcal{L}^{\star}_{Q,\boldsymbol{z}}
in (
38
) and
\mathcal{N}_{Q,\boldsymbol{z}}\left(\lambda\right)
in (
168
) satisfies
\mathcal{L}^{\star}_{Q,\boldsymbol{z}}\subseteq\mathcal{N}_{Q,\boldsymbol{z}}\left(\lambda\right)
.
∎

Note that Theorem
11
and Lemma
7
lead to the following conclusion

\lim_{\lambda\rightarrow 0^{+}}P^{\left(Q,\lambda\right)}_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}|\boldsymbol{Z}=\boldsymbol{z}}\left(\mathcal{N}_{Q,\boldsymbol{z}}\left(\lambda\right)\setminus\mathcal{L}^{\star}_{Q,\boldsymbol{z}}\right)=0,

(182)

which follows from the fact that
\mathcal{L}^{\star}_{Q,\boldsymbol{z}}\subset\mathcal{N}_{Q,\boldsymbol{z}}\left(\lambda\right)
, with
\mathcal{L}^{\star}_{Q,\boldsymbol{z}}
in (
38
). This justifies referring to the set
\mathcal{L}^{\star}_{Q,\boldsymbol{z}}
as the nonnegligible limit set.

X
(\delta,\epsilon)
-Optimality

This section introduces a PAC guarantee of optimality for the models that are sampled from the probability measure
P^{\left(Q,\lambda\right)}_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}|\boldsymbol{Z}=\boldsymbol{z}}
in (
25
) with respect to the ERM problem in (
4
). Such guarantee is defined as follows.

Definition 6
(
(\delta,\epsilon)
-Optimality)

Given a pair of positive reals
(\delta,\epsilon)
, with
\epsilon<1
, the probability measure
P^{\left(Q,\lambda\right)}_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}|\boldsymbol{Z}=\boldsymbol{z}}
in (
25
) is said to be
(\delta,\epsilon)
-optimal, if the set
\mathcal{L}_{\boldsymbol{z}}\left(\delta\right)
in (
36
) satisfies

P^{\left(Q,\lambda\right)}_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}|\boldsymbol{Z}=\boldsymbol{z}}\left(\mathcal{L}_{\boldsymbol{z}}\left(\delta\right)\right)>1-\epsilon.

(183)

If the probability measure
P^{\left(Q,\lambda\right)}_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}|\boldsymbol{Z}=\boldsymbol{z}}
in (
25
) is
(\delta,\epsilon)
-optimal, then it assigns a probability that is always greater than
1-\epsilon
to a set that contains models that induce an empirical risk that is smaller than
\delta
. From this perspective, particular interest is given to the smallest
\delta
and
\epsilon
for which
P^{\left(Q,\lambda\right)}_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}|\boldsymbol{Z}=\boldsymbol{z}}
is
\left(\delta,\epsilon\right)
-optimal.

The main result of this section is presented by the following theorem.

Theorem 12

For all
(\delta,\epsilon)\in(\delta_{Q,\boldsymbol{z}}^{\star},+\infty)\times(0,1)
, with
\delta_{Q,\boldsymbol{z}}^{\star}
in (
37
), there exists a real
\lambda\in\mathcal{K}_{Q,\boldsymbol{z}}
, with
\mathcal{K}_{Q,\boldsymbol{z}}
in (
23
), such that the probability measure
P^{\left(Q,\lambda\right)}_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}|\boldsymbol{Z}=\boldsymbol{z}}
is
\left(\delta,\epsilon\right)
-optimal.

Proof:

Let
\delta
be a real in
\left(\delta_{Q,\boldsymbol{z}}^{\star},+\infty\right)
, with
\delta_{Q,\boldsymbol{z}}^{\star}
in (
37
). Let also
\gamma\in\mathcal{K}_{Q,\boldsymbol{z}}
satisfy the following equality:

K^{(1)}_{Q,\boldsymbol{z}}\left(-\frac{1}{\gamma}\right)\leqslant\delta.

(184)

Note that from Lemma
15
, it follows that the function
K^{(1)}_{Q,\boldsymbol{z}}
is continuous. Moreover, from Theorem
6
, it follows that such a
\gamma
in (
184
) always exists.
From (
36
) and (
168
), it holds that

\mathcal{N}_{Q,\boldsymbol{z}}(\gamma)\subseteq\mathcal{L}_{\boldsymbol{z}}\left(\delta\right),

(185)

and thus,

P^{\left(Q,\gamma\right)}_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}|\boldsymbol{Z}=\boldsymbol{z}}\left(\mathcal{L}_{\boldsymbol{z}}\left(\delta\right)\right)\geqslant P^{\left(Q,\gamma\right)}_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}|\boldsymbol{Z}=\boldsymbol{z}}\left(\mathcal{N}_{Q,\boldsymbol{z}}(\gamma)\right).

(186)

Let
\lambda
be a positive real such that
\lambda\leqslant\gamma
and

P^{\left(Q,\lambda\right)}_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}|\boldsymbol{Z}=\boldsymbol{z}}(\mathcal{N}_{Q,\boldsymbol{z}}(\lambda))>1-\epsilon.

(187)

The existence of such a positive real
\lambda
follows from Theorem
11
.
Hence, from (
187
), it holds that,

\displaystyle 1-\epsilon

\displaystyle<

\displaystyle P^{\left(Q,\lambda\right)}_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}|\boldsymbol{Z}=\boldsymbol{z}}(\mathcal{N}_{Q,\boldsymbol{z}}(\lambda))

(188)

\displaystyle\leqslant

\displaystyle P^{\left(Q,\lambda\right)}_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}|\boldsymbol{Z}=\boldsymbol{z}}\left(\mathcal{L}_{\boldsymbol{z}}\left(\delta\right)\right),

(189)

where
the inequality in (
189
) follows from the fact that
\mathcal{N}_{Q,\boldsymbol{z}}(\lambda)\subseteq\mathcal{N}_{Q,\boldsymbol{z}}(\gamma)\subseteq\mathcal{L}_{\boldsymbol{z}}\left(\delta\right)
.
Finally, the inequality in (
189
) implies that the probability measure
P^{\left(Q,\lambda\right)}_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}|\boldsymbol{Z}=\boldsymbol{z}}
is
\left(\delta,\epsilon\right)
-optimal (Definition
6
).
This completes the proof.
∎

A stronger optimality claim can be stated when the reference measure is coherent.

Theorem 13

For all
(\delta,\epsilon)\in(\rho^{\star},+\infty)\times(0,1)
, with
\rho^{\star}
in (
49
), there always exists a
\lambda\in\mathcal{K}_{Q,\boldsymbol{z}}
, with
\mathcal{K}_{Q,\boldsymbol{z}}
in (
23
), such that the probability measure
P^{\left(Q,\lambda\right)}_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}|\boldsymbol{Z}=\boldsymbol{z}}
is
\left(\delta,\epsilon\right)
-optimal if and only if the reference measure
Q
is coherent.

Proof:

The proof is divided into two parts. The first part shows that if for all
(\delta,\epsilon)\in(\rho^{\star},+\infty)\times(0,1)
, there always exists a
\lambda\in\mathcal{K}_{Q,\boldsymbol{z}}
, with
\mathcal{K}_{Q,\boldsymbol{z}}
in (
23
), such that the probability measure
P^{\left(Q,\lambda\right)}_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}|\boldsymbol{Z}=\boldsymbol{z}}
in (
25
) is
(\delta,\epsilon)
-optimal, then, the measure
Q
is coherent.
The second part deals with the converse.

The first part is as follows. Let
\gamma\in\mathcal{K}_{Q,\boldsymbol{z}}
be such that

\displaystyle P^{\left(Q,\gamma\right)}_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}|\boldsymbol{Z}=\boldsymbol{z}}\left(\mathcal{L}_{\boldsymbol{z}}\left(\delta\right)\right)

\displaystyle>

\displaystyle 1-\epsilon,

(190)

then, for all measurable subsets
\mathcal{A}
of
\mathcal{L}_{\boldsymbol{z}}\left(\delta\right)
, it holds that

\displaystyle 1-\epsilon

\displaystyle<

\displaystyle P^{\left(Q,\gamma\right)}_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}|\boldsymbol{Z}=\boldsymbol{z}}\left(\mathcal{L}_{\boldsymbol{z}}\left(\delta\right)\right)

(191)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int_{\mathcal{A}}\frac{\mathrm{d}P^{\left(Q,\gamma\right)}_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}|\boldsymbol{Z}=\boldsymbol{z}}}{\mathrm{d}Q}\left(\boldsymbol{\nu}\right)\mathrm{d}Q\left(\boldsymbol{\nu}\right)+\int_{\mathcal{L}_{\boldsymbol{z}}\left(\delta\right)\setminus\mathcal{A}}\frac{\mathrm{d}P^{\left(Q,\gamma\right)}_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}|\boldsymbol{Z}=\boldsymbol{z}}}{\mathrm{d}Q}\left(\boldsymbol{\nu}\right)\mathrm{d}Q\left(\boldsymbol{\nu}\right),

which, together with Lemma
2
, implies that there exists at least one measurable subset
\mathcal{A}
for which
Q\left(\mathcal{A}\right)>0
, and thus,

\displaystyle Q\left(\mathcal{L}_{\boldsymbol{z}}\left(\delta\right)\right)

\displaystyle>

\displaystyle Q\left(\mathcal{A}\right)>0,

(192)

which implies that the measure
Q
is coherent. This completes the first part of the proof.

The second part of the proof is as follows. Under the assumption that the measure
Q
is coherent, it follows that
\delta_{Q,\boldsymbol{z}}^{\star}=\rho^{\star}
. Then, from Theorem
12
, it follows that for all
(\delta,\epsilon)\in(\delta_{Q,\boldsymbol{z}}^{\star},+\infty)\times(0,1)
, there always exists a
\lambda\in\mathcal{K}_{Q,\boldsymbol{z}}
, with
\mathcal{K}_{Q,\boldsymbol{z}}
in (
23
), such that the probability measure
P^{\left(Q,\lambda\right)}_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}|\boldsymbol{Z}=\boldsymbol{z}}
is
(\delta,\epsilon)
-optimal. This completes the second part of the proof.
∎

XI
Sensitivity and Generalization

This section introduces the notion of sensitivity and establishes its connections with the notion of generalization error of the Gibbs algorithm, cf.
[
9
]
.

XI-A

Sensitivity

The sensitivity of the expected empirical risk
\mathsf{R}_{\boldsymbol{z}}
in (
18
) to deviations from the probability measure
P^{\left(Q,\lambda\right)}_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}|\boldsymbol{Z}=\boldsymbol{z}}
in (
25
) towards an alternative probability measure
P\in\triangle\left(\mathcal{M},\mathscr{B}\left(\mathcal{M}\right)\right)
is introduced as a novel metric to evaluate the generalization capabilities of the ERM-RER-optimal measure
P^{\left(Q,\lambda\right)}_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}|\boldsymbol{Z}=\boldsymbol{z}}
.
Deviations from the probability measure
P^{\left(Q,\lambda\right)}_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}|\boldsymbol{Z}=\boldsymbol{z}}
towards an alternative probability measure
P
would allow comparing the ERM-RER-optimal measure with alternative measures (or algorithms). For instance, if new datasets become available, a new ERM-RER problem can be formulated using a larger dataset obtained by aggregating the old and the new datasets, cf.
[
11
]
and
[
72
]
. Intuitively, the ERM-RER-optimal measure obtained after the aggregation of datasets might exhibit better generalization capabilities, see for instance
[
11
]
. This analysis is the motivation of the sensitivity, which is defined as follows.

Definition 7
(Sensitivity)

Given the
\sigma
-finite measure
Q
and the positive real
\lambda>0
in (
19
), let
\mathsf{S}_{Q,\lambda}:\left(\mathcal{X}\times\mathcal{Y}\right)^{n}\times\triangle_{Q}\left(\mathcal{M},\mathscr{B}\left(\mathcal{M}\right)\right)\rightarrow\left(-\infty,+\infty\right]
be a functional such that

\displaystyle\mathsf{S}_{Q,\lambda}\left(\boldsymbol{z},P\right)=\left\{\begin{array}[]{cl}\mathsf{R}_{\boldsymbol{z}}\left(P\right)-\mathsf{R}_{\boldsymbol{z}}\left(P^{\left(Q,\lambda\right)}_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}|\boldsymbol{Z}=\boldsymbol{z}}\right)&\text{ if }\lambda\in\mathcal{K}_{Q,\boldsymbol{z}}\\
+\infty&\text{ otherwise,}\end{array}\right.

where the functional
\mathsf{R}_{\boldsymbol{z}}
is defined in (
18
) and the probability measure
P^{\left(Q,\lambda\right)}_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}|\boldsymbol{Z}=\boldsymbol{z}}
is in (
25
).
The sensitivity of the expected empirical risk
\mathsf{R}_{\boldsymbol{z}}
due to a deviation from
P^{\left(Q,\lambda\right)}_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}|\boldsymbol{Z}=\boldsymbol{z}}
to
P
is
\mathsf{S}_{Q,\lambda}\left(\boldsymbol{z},P\right)
.

Recently, the following exact expression for the sensitivity
\mathsf{S}_{Q,\lambda}\left(\boldsymbol{z},P\right)
in (
7
) was introduced in
[
11
]
.

Theorem 14
(Theorem 1 in
[
11
]
)

The sensitivity
\mathsf{S}_{Q,\lambda}\left(\boldsymbol{z},P\right)
in (
7
) satisfies

\displaystyle\mathsf{S}_{Q,\lambda}\left(\boldsymbol{z},P\right)

(196)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\lambda\Big(D\left(P^{\left(Q,\lambda\right)}_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}|\boldsymbol{Z}=\boldsymbol{z}}\|Q\right)+D\left(P\|P^{\left(Q,\lambda\right)}_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}|\boldsymbol{Z}=\boldsymbol{z}}\right)-D\left(P\|Q\right)\Big),

where the probability measure
P^{\left(Q,\lambda\right)}_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}|\boldsymbol{Z}=\boldsymbol{z}}
is in (
25
).

The following theorem introduces an upper bound on the absolute value of the sensitivity
\mathsf{S}_{Q,\lambda}\left(\boldsymbol{z},P\right)
in (
7
), which requires the calculation of only one of the relative entropies in Theorem
14
.

Theorem 15

For all
P\in\triangle_{Q}\left(\mathcal{M},\mathscr{B}\left(\mathcal{M}\right)\right)
, the sensitivity
\mathsf{S}_{Q,\lambda}\left(\boldsymbol{z},P\right)
in (
7
) satisfies

\displaystyle\left|\mathsf{S}_{Q,\lambda}\left(\boldsymbol{z},P\right)\right|

\displaystyle\leqslant

\displaystyle\sqrt{2\beta_{Q,\boldsymbol{z}}^{2}D\left(P\|P^{\left(Q,\lambda\right)}_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}|\boldsymbol{Z}=\boldsymbol{z}}\right)},

(197)

where the constant
\beta_{Q,\boldsymbol{z}}
is defined in (
165
).

Proof:

The proof is presented in Appendix
T
.
∎

Note that equality holds in (
197
) in the trivial case in which the empirical risk function is not separable with respect to
Q
(Definition
5
). In such case, for all
P\in\triangle_{Q}\left(\mathcal{M},\mathscr{B}\left(\mathcal{M}\right)\right)
, it holds that
\mathsf{S}_{Q,\lambda}\left(\boldsymbol{z},P\right)=0
and
\beta_{Q,\boldsymbol{z}}=0
.

Theorem
15
establishes an upper and a lower bound on the increase and decrease of the expected empirical risk that can be obtained by deviating from the optimal solution of the ERM-RER problem in (
19
). More specifically, note that for all probability measures
P\in\triangle_{Q}\left(\mathcal{M},\mathscr{B}\left(\mathcal{M}\right)\right)
, it holds that,

\displaystyle\mathsf{R}_{\boldsymbol{z}}\left(P\right)

\displaystyle\geqslant

\displaystyle\mathsf{R}_{\boldsymbol{z}}\left(P^{\left(Q,\lambda\right)}_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}|\boldsymbol{Z}=\boldsymbol{z}}\right)-\sqrt{2\beta_{Q,\boldsymbol{z}}^{2}D\left(P\|P^{\left(Q,\lambda\right)}_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}|\boldsymbol{Z}=\boldsymbol{z}}\right)}\mbox{ and }

(198)

\displaystyle\mathsf{R}_{\boldsymbol{z}}\left(P\right)

\displaystyle\leqslant

\displaystyle\mathsf{R}_{\boldsymbol{z}}\left(P^{\left(Q,\lambda\right)}_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}|\boldsymbol{Z}=\boldsymbol{z}}\right)+\sqrt{2\beta_{Q,\boldsymbol{z}}^{2}D\left(P\|P^{\left(Q,\lambda\right)}_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}|\boldsymbol{Z}=\boldsymbol{z}}\right)}.

(199)

XI-B

Generalization Error

This section unveils the interesting connection between the notion of sensitivity and the notion of generalization error of the Gibbs algorithm, cf.
[
9
]
. The Generalization error is defined under the assumption that datasets are sampled from a probability measure

\displaystyle P_{\boldsymbol{Z}}\in\triangle\left(\left(\mathcal{X}\times\mathcal{Y}\right)^{n},\mathscr{F}\right),

(200)

where
\mathscr{F}
denotes a given
\sigma
-field on the set
\left(\mathcal{X}\times\mathcal{Y}\right)^{n}
.
For such a probability measure
P_{\boldsymbol{Z}}
in (
200
), let the set
\mathcal{K}_{Q,P_{\boldsymbol{Z}}}\subset\mathds{R}
be

\mathcal{K}_{Q,P_{\boldsymbol{Z}}}=\bigcap_{\boldsymbol{z}\in\mathrm{supp}\,P_{\boldsymbol{Z}}}\mathcal{K}_{Q,\boldsymbol{z}},

(201)

where the
\sigma
-finite measure
Q
is in (
19
).
The set
\mathcal{K}_{Q,P_{\boldsymbol{Z}}}
in (
201
) can be empty for some choices of the
\sigma
-finite measure
Q
. Nonetheless, from Lemma
1
, it follows that if
Q
is a probability measure, then,

\mathcal{K}_{Q,P_{\boldsymbol{Z}}}=\left(0,+\infty\right).

(202)

Under the assumption that datasets are sampled from
P_{\boldsymbol{Z}}
in (
200
), the generalization error of the Gibbs algorithm with parameters
Q
and
\lambda
, is defined as the expectation with respect to the product measure
P^{\left(Q,\lambda\right)}_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}|\boldsymbol{Z}}\cdot P_{\boldsymbol{Z}}
, with
P^{\left(Q,\lambda\right)}_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}|\boldsymbol{Z}}
in (
25
), of difference between:
(a)
the
population risk
due to a model
\boldsymbol{\theta}\in\mathcal{M}
,

\displaystyle\int\mathsf{L}_{\boldsymbol{z}}\left(\boldsymbol{\theta}\right)\mathrm{d}P_{\boldsymbol{Z}}\left(\boldsymbol{z}\right)

(203)

with the function
\mathsf{L}_{\boldsymbol{z}}
defined in (
3
); and
(b)
The empirical risk induced by the model
\boldsymbol{\theta}
with respect to a training dataset
\boldsymbol{\boldsymbol{z}}
, that is,
\mathsf{L}_{\boldsymbol{z}}\left(\boldsymbol{\theta}\right)
.
More specifically, the generalization error of the Gibbs algorithm with parameters
Q
and
\lambda
is

\displaystyle\int\int\left(\int\mathsf{L}_{\boldsymbol{z}}\left(\boldsymbol{\theta}\right)\mathrm{d}P_{\boldsymbol{Z}}\left(\boldsymbol{z}\right)-\mathsf{L}_{\boldsymbol{\nu}}\left(\boldsymbol{\theta}\right)\right)\mathrm{d}P^{\left(Q,\lambda\right)}_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}|\boldsymbol{Z}=\boldsymbol{\nu}}\left(\boldsymbol{\theta}\right)\mathrm{d}P_{\boldsymbol{Z}}(\boldsymbol{\nu})

(205)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int\left(\int\mathsf{L}_{\boldsymbol{z}}\left(\boldsymbol{\theta}\right)\mathrm{d}P_{\boldsymbol{Z}}\left(\boldsymbol{z}\right)\right)\mathrm{d}P^{\left(Q,\lambda\right)}_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}}\left(\boldsymbol{\theta}\right)

\displaystyle-\int\mathsf{L}_{\boldsymbol{\nu}}\left(\boldsymbol{\theta}\right)\mathrm{d}P^{\left(Q,\lambda\right)}_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}|\boldsymbol{Z}=\boldsymbol{\nu}}\left(\boldsymbol{\theta}\right)\mathrm{d}P_{\boldsymbol{Z}}(\boldsymbol{\nu})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int\left(\int\mathsf{L}_{\boldsymbol{z}}\left(\boldsymbol{\theta}\right)\mathrm{d}P^{\left(Q,\lambda\right)}_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}}\left(\boldsymbol{\theta}\right)\right)\mathrm{d}P_{\boldsymbol{Z}}\left(\boldsymbol{z}\right)

\displaystyle-\int\mathsf{L}_{\boldsymbol{\nu}}\left(\boldsymbol{\theta}\right)\mathrm{d}P^{\left(Q,\lambda\right)}_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}|\boldsymbol{Z}=\boldsymbol{\nu}}\left(\boldsymbol{\theta}\right)\mathrm{d}P_{\boldsymbol{Z}}(\boldsymbol{\nu})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int\left(\mathsf{R}_{\boldsymbol{\nu}}\left(P^{\left(Q,\lambda\right)}_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}}\right)-\mathsf{R}_{\boldsymbol{\nu}}\left(P^{\left(Q,\lambda\right)}_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}|\boldsymbol{Z}=\boldsymbol{\nu}}\right)\right)\mathrm{d}P_{\boldsymbol{Z}}(\boldsymbol{\nu}),

(206)

where the probability measure
P^{\left(Q,\lambda\right)}_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}}
satisfies for all sets
\mathcal{A}\in\mathscr{B}\left(\mathcal{M}\right)
,

P^{\left(Q,\lambda\right)}_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}}\left(\mathcal{A}\right)=\int P^{\left(Q,\lambda\right)}_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}|\boldsymbol{Z}=\boldsymbol{\nu}}\left(\mathcal{A}\right)\mathrm{d}P_{\boldsymbol{Z}}\left(\boldsymbol{\nu}\right),

(207)

and the functional
\mathsf{R}_{\boldsymbol{\nu}}
is defined in (
18
).

The following theorem establishes a connection between sensitivity and generalization error in the particular case in which
Q
in (
19
) is a probability measure.

Theorem 16

Under the assumption that datasets are sampled from
P_{\boldsymbol{Z}}
in (
200
), the generalization error of the Gibbs algorithm with parameters
Q
(a probability measure) and
\lambda>0
, is

\displaystyle\int\hskip-3.44444pt\mathsf{S}_{Q,\lambda}\left(\boldsymbol{\nu},P^{\left(Q,\lambda\right)}_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}}\right)\mathrm{d}P_{\boldsymbol{Z}}(\boldsymbol{\nu}),

(208)

where the functional
\mathsf{S}_{Q,\lambda}
is in (
7
); and the probability measure
P^{\left(Q,\lambda\right)}_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}}
is in (
207
).

Proof:

The proof uses the fact that under the assumption that
Q
is a probability measure, for all
\boldsymbol{\nu}\in\mathrm{supp}\,P_{\boldsymbol{Z}}
, it follows from Lemma
1
that
\mathcal{K}_{Q,\boldsymbol{\nu}}=\left(0,+\infty\right)
. This implies that for all
\boldsymbol{z}\in\mathrm{supp}\,P_{\boldsymbol{Z}}
and for all
\lambda>0
, the ERM-RER problem in (
19
), always possesses as solution the measure
P^{\left(Q,\lambda\right)}_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}|\boldsymbol{Z}=\boldsymbol{z}}
in (
25
).
Thus, the measure
P^{\left(Q,\lambda\right)}_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}}
in (
207
) is well defined.
Moreover,
\mathsf{S}_{Q,\lambda}\left(\boldsymbol{z},P^{\left(Q,\lambda\right)}_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}}\right)=\mathsf{R}_{\boldsymbol{z}}\left(P^{\left(Q,\lambda\right)}_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}}\right)-\mathsf{R}_{\boldsymbol{z}}\left(P^{\left(Q,\lambda\right)}_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}|\boldsymbol{Z}=\boldsymbol{z}}\right)
and the integral in (
206
) is also well defined, which completes the proof.
∎

Theorem
16
provides an interesting viewpoint of the generalization error.
For instance, the probability measure
P^{\left(Q,\lambda\right)}_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}}
in (
200
) can be understood as the barycenter of a subset of
\triangle\left(\mathcal{M},\mathscr{B}\left(\mathcal{M}\right)\right)
containing the solutions to ERM-RER problems of the form in (
19
), with
\boldsymbol{z}\in\mathrm{supp}\,P_{\boldsymbol{Z}}
in (
200
).
Hence, the generalization error of the Gibbs algorithm is the expectation (with respect to
P_{\boldsymbol{Z}}
) of the sensitivity of the expected empirical risks
\mathsf{R}_{\boldsymbol{z}}
in (
18
) to variations from the ERM-RER-optimal measure
P^{\left(Q,\lambda\right)}_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}|\boldsymbol{Z}=\boldsymbol{z}}
towards the barycenter, i.e., the measure
P^{\left(Q,\lambda\right)}_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}}
.

The following definition extends the notion of generalization error to Gibbs algorithms obtained by assuming that the reference measure
Q
in (
19
) is a
\sigma
-finite measure. This definition also exploits the relation between the notions of sensitivity and generalization error introduced by Theorem
16
.

Definition 8
(Generalization Error of the Gibbs Algorithm)

Given a
\sigma
-finite measure
Q\in\triangle\left(\mathcal{M},\mathscr{B}\left(\mathcal{M}\right)\right)
and a real
\lambda>0
, let the functional
\mathsf{G}_{Q,\lambda}:\triangle\left(\left(\mathcal{X}\times\mathcal{Y}\right)^{n},\mathscr{F}\right)\to\left(-\infty,+\infty\right]
be such that

\displaystyle\mathsf{G}_{Q,\lambda}\left(P_{\boldsymbol{Z}}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left\{\begin{array}[]{cl}\displaystyle\hskip-6.02773pt\int\mathsf{S}_{Q,\lambda}\left(\boldsymbol{\nu},P^{\left(Q,\lambda\right)}_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}}\right)\mathrm{d}P_{\boldsymbol{Z}}(\boldsymbol{\nu})&\hskip-8.61108pt\text{ if }\lambda\in\mathcal{K}_{Q,P_{\boldsymbol{Z}}}\\
+\infty&\hskip-8.61108pt\text{ otherwise,}\end{array}\right.

where the functional
\mathsf{S}_{Q,\lambda}
is in (
7
); the set
\mathcal{K}_{Q,P_{\boldsymbol{Z}}}
is in (
201
); and the probability measure
P^{\left(Q,\lambda\right)}_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}}
is in (
207
). The generalization error induced by the Gibbs algorithm with parameters
Q
and
\lambda
under the assumption that datasets are sampled from the probability measure
P_{\boldsymbol{Z}}
, is
\mathsf{G}_{Q,\lambda}\left(P_{\boldsymbol{Z}}\right)
.

The main difficulty for extending the notion of generalization error to Gibbs algorithms obtained under the assumption that the reference measure is not a probability measure, but a
\sigma
-finite measure, is that the integrals in (
206
) and (
207
) might not be well defined.
This is essentially due to the fact that, while the ERM-RER problem in (
19
) always possesses a solution when
Q
is a probability measure, the existence of a solution when
Q
is not a probability measure is subject to the condition that for all
\boldsymbol{z}\in\mathrm{supp}\,P_{\boldsymbol{Z}}
,
\lambda\in\mathcal{K}_{Q,\boldsymbol{z}}
, with
\mathcal{K}_{Q,\boldsymbol{z}}
in (
23
). This leads to the condition that
\lambda\in\mathcal{K}_{Q,P_{\boldsymbol{Z}}}
, with the set
\mathcal{K}_{Q,P_{\boldsymbol{Z}}}
in (
201
). When such a condition is not met, the definition of sensitivity is void.

The following theorem provides a closed-form expression for the generalization error of the Gibbs algorithm in the general case in which the reference measure
Q
in (
19
) is a
\sigma
-finite measure.

Theorem 17

If
\lambda\in\mathcal{K}_{Q,P_{\boldsymbol{Z}}}
, with
\mathcal{K}_{Q,P_{\boldsymbol{Z}}}
in (
201
),
the generalization error
\mathsf{G}_{Q,\lambda}\left(P_{\boldsymbol{Z}}\right)
in (
8
) satisfies

\displaystyle\mathsf{G}_{Q,\lambda}\left(P_{\boldsymbol{Z}}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\lambda\Bigg(\int D\left(P^{\left(Q,\lambda\right)}_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}|\boldsymbol{Z}=\boldsymbol{\nu}}\|P^{\left(Q,\lambda\right)}_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}}\right)\mathrm{d}P_{\boldsymbol{Z}}(\boldsymbol{\nu})

(212)
