\displaystyle+\int D\left(P^{\left(Q,\lambda\right)}_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}}\|P^{\left(Q,\lambda\right)}_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}|\boldsymbol{Z}=\boldsymbol{\nu}}\right)\mathrm{d}P_{\boldsymbol{Z}}(\boldsymbol{\nu})\Bigg),

where for all
\boldsymbol{z}\in\mathrm{supp}\,P_{\boldsymbol{Z}}
, the probability measure
P^{\left(Q,\lambda\right)}_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}|\boldsymbol{Z}=\boldsymbol{z}}
is in (
25
); and
the probability measure
P^{\left(Q,\lambda\right)}_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}}
is defined in (
207
).

Proof:

The proof is presented in Appendix
U
.
∎

The terms
\int D\left(P^{\left(Q,\lambda\right)}_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}|\boldsymbol{Z}=\boldsymbol{\nu}}\|P^{\left(Q,\lambda\right)}_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}}\right)\mathrm{d}P_{\boldsymbol{Z}}(\boldsymbol{\nu})
and
\int D\left(P^{\left(Q,\lambda\right)}_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}}\|P^{\left(Q,\lambda\right)}_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}|\boldsymbol{Z}=\boldsymbol{\nu}}\right)\mathrm{d}P_{\boldsymbol{Z}}(\boldsymbol{\nu})
in the right-hand side of (
212
) are respectively the mutual and the lautum information
[
56
]
induced by a joint probability measure
P_{\boldsymbol{\Theta},\boldsymbol{Z}}
whose marginals are
P_{\boldsymbol{Z}}
in (
200
) and
P^{\left(Q,\lambda\right)}_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}}
in (
207
).
When the reference measure
Q
in (
19
) is a probability measure, Theorem
17
reduces to
[
9
, Theorem
1
]
.
Interestingly, independently of whether the reference measure
Q
in (
19
) is a probability measure, or whether the
n
data points in the datasets are independent and identically distributed, the generalization error
\mathsf{G}_{Q,\lambda}\left(P_{\boldsymbol{Z}}\right)
in (
8
) is always a factor of the sum of the mutual and lautum information induced by the joint probability measure
P_{\boldsymbol{\Theta},\boldsymbol{Z}}
mentioned above.

Theorem
17
also provides an alternative interpretation of the generalization error
\mathsf{G}_{Q,\lambda}\left(P_{\boldsymbol{Z}}\right)
in (
8
). Note that by writing one of the factors in the right-hand side of (
212
) as

\displaystyle\int\left(D\left(P^{\left(Q,\lambda\right)}_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}|\boldsymbol{Z}=\boldsymbol{\nu}}\|P^{\left(Q,\lambda\right)}_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}}\right)+D\left(P^{\left(Q,\lambda\right)}_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}}\|P^{\left(Q,\lambda\right)}_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}|\boldsymbol{Z}=\boldsymbol{\nu}}\right)\right)\mathrm{d}P_{\boldsymbol{Z}}(\boldsymbol{\nu}),

it becomes clear that
\mathsf{G}_{Q,\lambda}\left(P_{\boldsymbol{Z}}\right)
is the expectation with respect to
P_{\boldsymbol{Z}}
of the symmetrized Kullback-Leibler divergence, also known as Jeffrey’s divergence
[
69
]
, of the probability measures
P^{\left(Q,\lambda\right)}_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}|\boldsymbol{Z}=\boldsymbol{z}}
and
P^{\left(Q,\lambda\right)}_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}}
. That is, the solution to the ERM-RER problem in (
19
) and the barycenter induced by
P_{\boldsymbol{Z}}
.

The following theorem provides an upper-bound on the generalization error of the Gibbs algorithm only in terms of the lautum information induced by such a joint probability measure
P_{\boldsymbol{\Theta},\boldsymbol{Z}}
.

Theorem 18

The generalization error
\mathsf{G}_{Q,\lambda}\left(P_{\boldsymbol{Z}}\right)
in (
8
) satisfies for all
\lambda\in\mathcal{K}_{Q,P_{\boldsymbol{Z}}}
,

\displaystyle 0\leqslant

\displaystyle\mathsf{G}_{Q,\lambda}\left(P_{\boldsymbol{Z}}\right)

\displaystyle\leqslant\sqrt{2\sigma^{2}_{Q}\int D\left(P^{\left(Q,\lambda\right)}_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}}\|P^{\left(Q,\lambda\right)}_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}|\boldsymbol{Z}=\boldsymbol{\nu}}\right)\mathrm{d}P_{\boldsymbol{Z}}(\boldsymbol{\nu})},

(213)

where for all
\boldsymbol{z}\in\mathrm{supp}\,P_{\boldsymbol{Z}}
, the probability measure
P^{\left(Q,\lambda\right)}_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}|\boldsymbol{Z}=\boldsymbol{z}}
is in (
25
);
the probability measure
P^{\left(Q,\lambda\right)}_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}}
is defined in (
207
); and

\displaystyle\sigma_{Q}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sup\left\{\beta_{Q,\boldsymbol{z}}:\boldsymbol{z}\in\left(\mathcal{X}\times\mathcal{Y}\right)^{n}\right\},

(214)

with
\beta_{Q,\boldsymbol{z}}
in (
165
).

Proof:

The proof of the inequality
\mathsf{G}_{Q,\lambda}\left(P_{\boldsymbol{Z}}\right)\geqslant 0
follows from observing that for all
\boldsymbol{\nu}\in\left(\mathcal{X}\times\mathcal{Y}\right)^{n}
, the terms

D\left(P^{\left(Q,\lambda\right)}_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}|\boldsymbol{Z}=\boldsymbol{\nu}}\|P^{\left(Q,\lambda\right)}_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}}\right)
and
D\left(P^{\left(Q,\lambda\right)}_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}}\|P^{\left(Q,\lambda\right)}_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}|\boldsymbol{Z}=\boldsymbol{\nu}}\right)
in (
212
) are nonnegative (Theorem
1
).
The proof of the remaining inequality follows from (
8
) and the following inequalities:

\displaystyle\mathsf{G}_{Q,\lambda}\left(P_{\boldsymbol{Z}}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left|\int\mathsf{S}_{Q,\lambda}\left(\boldsymbol{\nu},P^{\left(Q,\lambda\right)}_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}}\right)\mathrm{d}P_{\boldsymbol{Z}}(\boldsymbol{\nu})\right|

(215)

\displaystyle\leqslant

\displaystyle\int\left|\mathsf{S}_{Q,\lambda}\left(\boldsymbol{\nu},P^{\left(Q,\lambda\right)}_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}}\right)\right|\mathrm{d}P_{\boldsymbol{Z}}(\boldsymbol{\nu})

(216)

\displaystyle\leqslant

\displaystyle\int\sqrt{2\beta_{Q,\boldsymbol{\nu}}D\left(P^{\left(Q,\lambda\right)}_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}}\|P^{\left(Q,\lambda\right)}_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}|\boldsymbol{Z}=\boldsymbol{\nu}}\right)}\mathrm{d}P_{\boldsymbol{Z}}(\boldsymbol{\nu}),

(217)

\displaystyle\leqslant

\displaystyle\int\hskip-4.30554pt\sqrt{2\sigma_{Q}^{2}D\left(P^{\left(Q,\lambda\right)}_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}}\|P^{\left(Q,\lambda\right)}_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}|\boldsymbol{Z}=\boldsymbol{\nu}}\right)}\mathrm{d}P_{\boldsymbol{Z}}(\boldsymbol{\nu})

(218)

\displaystyle\leqslant

\displaystyle\sqrt{2\sigma_{Q}^{2}\int D\left(P^{\left(Q,\lambda\right)}_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}}\|P^{\left(Q,\lambda\right)}_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}|\boldsymbol{Z}=\boldsymbol{\nu}}\right)\mathrm{d}P_{\boldsymbol{Z}}(\boldsymbol{\nu})},

(219)

where
the equality in (
215
) follows from (
8
);
the inequality in (
216
) follows from
[
59
, Theorem
1.5.9
(c)]
;
the inequality in (
217
) follows from Theorem
15
;
the inequality in (
218
) follows from (
214
); and
the inequality in (
219
) follows from Jensen’s inequality
[
59
, Section
6.3.5
]
.
This completes the proof.
∎In a nutshell, the generalization error
\mathsf{G}_{Q,\lambda}\left(P_{\boldsymbol{Z}}\right)
in (
8
) is upper bounded up to a constant factor by the square root of the lautum information induced by the joint probability measure
P_{\boldsymbol{\Theta},\boldsymbol{Z}}
mentioned above.
Theorem
18
is reminiscent of
[
33
, Theorem
1
]
, which provides a similar upper-bound on
\mathsf{G}_{Q,\lambda}\left(P_{\boldsymbol{Z}}\right)
using the mutual information instead of the lautum information induced by the joint probability measure
P_{\boldsymbol{\Theta},\boldsymbol{Z}}
.
The interest in Theorem
18
for the specific case of the Gibbs algorithm, lies on the fact that it holds under milder conditions than those in
[
33
, Theorem
1
]
. For instance, no additional conditions on the loss function
\ell
in (
2
) concerning sub-Gaussianity are assumed. Moreover, the probability measure
P_{\boldsymbol{Z}}
from which datasets are sampled is not necessarily a product measure.

XII
Conclusions and Final Remarks

The classical ERM-RER problem in (
19
) has been studied under the assumption that the reference measure
Q
is a
\sigma
-finite measure, instead of a probability measure, which leads to a more general problem that includes the ERM problem with (discrete or differential) entropy regularization and the information-risk minimization problem. While in the case in which the reference measure is a probability measure the solution to the ERM-RER problem always exists, in this general case, the existence of a solution is subject to a condition that depends on the loss function, the reference measure, the regularization factor, and the training dataset. When a solution exists, it has been proved that it is unique. Additionally, if it exists, such a solution and the reference measure are mutually absolutely continuous. Interestingly, the empirical risk observed when models are sampled from the ERM-RER-optimal probability measure is a sub-Gaussian random variable that exhibits a PAC guarantee for the ERM problem. That is, for some positive
\delta
and
\epsilon
, it is shown that there always exist some parameters for the ERM-RER problem such that the set of models that induce an empirical risk smaller than
\delta
exhibits a probability that is not smaller that
1-\epsilon
. Interestingly, none of these results relies on statistical assumptions on the datasets.

The sensitivity of the expected empirical risk to deviations from the ERM-RER-optimal measure to alternative measures is introduced as a new performance metric to evaluate the generalization capabilities of the Gibbs algorithm. In particular, an upper bound on the absolute value of the sensitivity, which depends on the training dataset, is presented. This bound is formed by a constant factor and the square root of the relative entropy of the alternative measure (the deviation) with respect to the ERM-RER solution.
Finally, it is shown that the expectation of the sensitivity (with respect to the datasets) to deviations towards a particular measure is equivalent to the generalization error of the Gibbs algorithm. Equipped with this observation, the generalization error is shown to be in the most general case, up to a constant factor, the sum of the mutual and lautum information between the models and the datasets, which was a result known exclusively for the case in which the reference is a probability measure, cf.
[
9
]
.
From this perspective, it is argued that the study of the generalization capabilities of the Gibbs algorithm based on generalization error is a significantly narrow view. This is essentially because it is looking at an expectation of the sensitivity to deviations to a particular measure, i.e., the barycenter of the set of ERM-RER solutions induced by a prior on the datasets.
A broader view is offered by the study of the sensitivity to deviations towards other measures, i.e., ERM-RER-optimal measures obtained with different training data sets. This approach has lead already to a few initial results in
[
11
]
that highlight the connections to sensitivity, training error, and test error. Nonetheless, the study of the sensitivity in the aim of describing the generalization capabilities of learning algorithms remains by now as an open problem.

Appendix A
Proof of Theorem
2

Consider the function
f:[0,+\infty)\to\mathds{R}
such that

\displaystyle f(x)=\left\{\begin{array}[]{rcl}x\log(x)&\text{ if }&x>0\\
0&\text{ if }&x=0,\end{array}\right.

and note that it is strictly convex.
From the assumption that for all
i\in\{1,2\}
,
P_{i}
and
Q_{i}
are both measures on the same measurable space
\left(\Omega,\mathscr{F}\right)
, with
P_{i}
absolutely continuous with respect to
Q_{i}
, let
g:\Omega\to[0,\infty)
be the function

\displaystyle g(x)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{\mathrm{d}\left(\lambda P_{1}+(1-\lambda)P_{2}\right)}{\mathrm{d}\left(\lambda Q_{1}+(1-\lambda)Q_{2}\right)}\left(x\right),

(223)

where
\frac{\mathrm{d}\left(\lambda P_{1}+(1-\lambda)P_{2}\right)}{\mathrm{d}\left(\lambda Q_{1}+(1-\lambda)Q_{2}\right)}
is the Radon-Nikodym derivative of the measure
\lambda P_{1}+(1-\lambda)P_{2}
with respect to
\lambda Q_{1}+(1-\lambda)Q_{2}
.
Using this notation, for all
\lambda\in(0,1)
,

\displaystyle D\left(\lambda P_{1}+(1-\lambda)P_{2}\|\lambda Q_{1}+(1-\lambda)Q_{2}\right)

(224)

\displaystyle-\lambda D\left(P_{1}\|Q_{1}\right)+(1-\lambda)D\left(P_{2}\|Q_{2}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int\log\left(g\left(x\right)\right)\mathrm{d}\left(\lambda P_{1}+(1-\lambda)P_{2}\right)(x)

\displaystyle-\lambda\int\log\left(\frac{\mathrm{d}P_{1}}{\mathrm{d}Q_{1}}(x)\right)\mathrm{d}P_{1}(x)-(1-\lambda)\int\log\left(\frac{\mathrm{d}P_{2}}{\mathrm{d}Q_{2}}(x)\right)\mathrm{d}P_{2}(x)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\lambda\int\log\left(g\left(x\right)\right)\mathrm{d}P_{1}(x)+(1-\lambda)\int\log\left(g\left(x\right)\right)\mathrm{d}P_{2}(x)

\displaystyle-\lambda\int\log\left(\frac{\mathrm{d}P_{1}}{\mathrm{d}Q_{1}}(x)\right)\mathrm{d}P_{1}(x)-(1-\lambda)\int\log\left(\frac{\mathrm{d}P_{2}}{\mathrm{d}Q_{2}}(x)\right)\mathrm{d}P_{2}(x)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\lambda\int\log\left(\left(\frac{\mathrm{d}P_{1}}{\mathrm{d}Q_{1}}(x)\right)^{-1}g\left(x\right)\right)\mathrm{d}P_{1}(x)

\displaystyle+(1-\lambda)\int\log\left(\left(\frac{\mathrm{d}P_{2}}{\mathrm{d}Q_{2}}(x)\right)^{-1}g\left(x\right)\right)\mathrm{d}P_{2}(x)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\lambda\int\frac{\mathrm{d}P_{1}}{\mathrm{d}Q_{1}}(x)\log\left(\left(\frac{\mathrm{d}P_{1}}{\mathrm{d}Q_{1}}(x)\right)^{-1}g\left(x\right)\right)\mathrm{d}Q_{1}(x)

\displaystyle+(1-\lambda)\int\frac{\mathrm{d}P_{2}}{\mathrm{d}Q_{2}}(x)\log\left(\left(\frac{\mathrm{d}P_{2}}{\mathrm{d}Q_{2}}(x)\right)^{-1}g\left(x\right)\right)\mathrm{d}Q_{2}(x)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\lambda\int\frac{g(x)\frac{\mathrm{d}P_{1}}{\mathrm{d}Q_{1}}(x)}{g(x)}\log\left(\left(\frac{\mathrm{d}P_{1}}{\mathrm{d}Q_{1}}(x)\right)^{-1}g\left(x\right)\right)\mathrm{d}Q_{1}(x)

\displaystyle+(1-\lambda)\int\frac{g(x)\frac{\mathrm{d}P_{2}}{\mathrm{d}Q_{2}}(x)}{g(x)}\log\left(\left(\frac{\mathrm{d}P_{2}}{\mathrm{d}Q_{2}}(x)\right)^{-1}g\left(x\right)\right)\mathrm{d}Q_{2}(x)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\lambda\int g(x)f\left(\frac{\mathrm{d}P_{1}}{\mathrm{d}Q_{1}}(x)\left(g(x)\right)^{-1}\right)\mathrm{d}Q_{1}(x)

\displaystyle-(1-\lambda)\int g(x)f\left(\frac{\mathrm{d}P_{2}}{\mathrm{d}Q_{2}}(x)\left(g(x)\right)^{-1}\right)\mathrm{d}Q_{2}(x),

(225)

where the function
f
is defined in (
A
).
Let
\beta_{1}
and
\beta_{2}
be the following constants:

\displaystyle\beta_{1}\triangleq\int g(\nu)\mathrm{d}Q_{1}(\nu)\mbox{ and }\beta_{2}\triangleq\int g(\nu)\mathrm{d}Q_{2}(\nu).

(226)

From (
225
) and (
226
), it follows that for all
\lambda\in(0,1)
,

\displaystyle D\left(\lambda P_{1}+(1-\lambda)P_{2}\|\lambda Q_{1}+(1-\lambda)Q_{2}\right)

(227)

\displaystyle-\lambda D\left(P_{1}\|Q_{1}\right)+(1-\lambda)D\left(P_{2}\|Q_{2}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\lambda\beta_{1}\int\frac{g(x)}{\beta_{1}}f\left(\frac{\mathrm{d}P_{1}}{\mathrm{d}Q_{1}}(x)\left(g(x)\right)^{-1}\right)\mathrm{d}Q_{1}(x)

\displaystyle-(1-\lambda)\beta_{2}\int\frac{g(x)}{\beta_{2}}f\left(\frac{\mathrm{d}P_{2}}{\mathrm{d}Q_{2}}(x)\left(g(x)\right)^{-1}\right)\mathrm{d}Q_{2}(x)

\displaystyle\leqslant

\displaystyle-\lambda\beta_{1}f\left(\int\frac{g(x)}{\beta_{1}}\frac{\mathrm{d}P_{1}}{\mathrm{d}Q_{1}}(x)\left(g(x)\right)^{-1}\mathrm{d}Q_{1}(x)\right)

\displaystyle-(1-\lambda)\beta_{2}f\left(\int\frac{g(x)}{\beta_{2}}\frac{\mathrm{d}P_{2}}{\mathrm{d}Q_{2}}(x)\left(g(x)\right)^{-1}\mathrm{d}Q_{2}(x)\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\lambda\beta_{1}f\left(\frac{1}{\beta_{1}}\int\mathrm{d}P_{1}(x)\right)-(1-\lambda)\beta_{2}f\left(\frac{1}{\beta_{2}}\int\mathrm{d}P_{2}(x)\right)

(228)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\lambda\beta_{1}f\left(\frac{1}{\beta_{1}}\right)-(1-\lambda)\beta_{2}f\left(\frac{1}{\beta_{2}}\right)

(229)

\displaystyle\leqslant

\displaystyle-f\left(\lambda\beta_{1}\frac{1}{\beta_{1}}+(1-\lambda)\beta_{2}\frac{1}{\beta_{2}}\right)

(230)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-f\left(1\right)

(231)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 0,

(232)

where the inequalities in (
227
) and (
230
) follow from Jensen’s inequality
[
59
, Section
6.3.5
]
and the fact that the function
f
in (
225
) is strictly concave. Note that from (
226
), in (
227
), for all
i\in\{1,2\}
,
\int\frac{g(x)}{\beta_{i}}\mathrm{d}Q_{i}(x)=1
; while in (
230
),

\displaystyle\lambda\beta_{1}+(1-\lambda)\beta_{2}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int g(\nu)\mathrm{d}\left(\lambda Q_{1}+(1-\lambda)Q_{2}\right)(\nu)

(233)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int\mathrm{d}\left(\lambda P_{1}+(1-\lambda)P_{2}\right)(\nu)

(234)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\lambda\int\mathrm{d}P_{1}(\nu)+(1-\lambda)\int\mathrm{d}P_{2}(\nu)

(235)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 1.

(236)

Given the strict convexity of the function
f
in (
A
), equality in (
227
) and (
230
) hold if and only if
P_{1}=P_{2}
and
Q_{1}=Q_{2}
.
This completes the proof.

Appendix B
Proof of Lemma
1

The proof is divided into two parts.
The first part is as follows. Under the assumption that the set
\mathcal{K}_{Q,\boldsymbol{z}}
in (
23
) is empty, there is nothing to prove. Alternatively, under the assumption that the set
\mathcal{K}_{Q,\boldsymbol{z}}
is not empty, there always exists a real
b\in\mathcal{K}_{Q,\boldsymbol{z}}
, such that
K_{Q,\boldsymbol{z}}\left(-\frac{1}{b}\right)<+\infty
.
Note that for all
\boldsymbol{\theta}\in\mathcal{M}
,

\displaystyle\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}\exp\left(-\frac{1}{t}\;\mathsf{L}_{\boldsymbol{z}}\left(\boldsymbol{\theta}\right)\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{t^{2}}\mathsf{L}_{\boldsymbol{z}}\left(\boldsymbol{\theta}\right)\exp\left(-\frac{1}{t}\;\mathsf{L}_{\boldsymbol{z}}\left(\boldsymbol{\theta}\right)\right)\geqslant 0,

(237)

with
\mathsf{L}_{\boldsymbol{z}}
in (
3
).
Thus, from (
22
), it follows that
K_{Q,\boldsymbol{z}}\left(-\frac{1}{b}\right)
is nondecreasing with
b
.
This implies that
(0,b]\subseteq\mathcal{K}_{Q,\boldsymbol{z}}
.

Let
b^{\star}\in(0,+\infty]
be

b^{\star}=\sup\mathcal{K}_{Q,\boldsymbol{z}}.

(238)

Hence, if
b^{\star}=+\infty
, it follows from (
23
) that

\mathcal{K}_{Q,\boldsymbol{z}}=(0,+\infty).

(239)

Alternatively, if
b^{\star}<+\infty
, it holds that

(0,b^{\star})\subseteq\mathcal{K}_{Q,\boldsymbol{z}}\subseteq(0,b^{\star}].

(240)

In either case, it follows that
\mathcal{K}_{Q,\boldsymbol{z}}
is a convex set. This completes the first part of the proof.

The second part of the proof is under the assumption that
Q
is a probability measure. Under this assumption, for all
\boldsymbol{\theta}\in\mathcal{M}
and for all for all
t>0
, it follows that

\exp\left(-\frac{1}{t}\;\mathsf{L}_{\boldsymbol{z}}\left(\boldsymbol{\theta}\right)\right)\leqslant 1,

(241)

with
\mathsf{L}_{\boldsymbol{z}}
in (
3
).
Thus,

\displaystyle K_{Q,\boldsymbol{z}}\left(-\frac{1}{t}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\log\left(\int\exp\left(-\frac{1}{t}\;\mathsf{L}_{\boldsymbol{z}}\left(\boldsymbol{\theta}\right)\right)\mathrm{d}Q(\boldsymbol{\theta})\right)

(242)

\displaystyle\leqslant

\displaystyle\log\left(\int\mathrm{d}Q(\boldsymbol{\theta})\right)

(243)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 0,

(244)

which implies that
(0,+\infty)\subseteq\mathcal{K}_{Q,\boldsymbol{z}}
. Thus, if
Q
is a probability measure, from (
23
), it holds that
\mathcal{K}_{Q,\boldsymbol{z}}=(0,+\infty)
, which completes the proof.

Appendix C
Proof of Theorem
3

The optimization problem in (
19
) can be re-written in terms of the Radon-Nikodym derivative of the optimization measure
P
with respect to the measure
Q
, denoted by
\frac{\mathrm{d}P}{\mathrm{d}Q}:\mathcal{M}\to[0,\infty)
, which yields:

\displaystyle\min_{P\in\triangle_{Q}\left(\mathcal{M},\mathscr{B}\left(\mathcal{M}\right)\right)}

\displaystyle\int\mathsf{L}_{\boldsymbol{z}}\left(\boldsymbol{\theta}\right)\frac{\mathrm{d}P}{\mathrm{d}Q}(\boldsymbol{\theta})\mathrm{d}Q(\boldsymbol{\theta})

(245a)

\displaystyle+\lambda\int\frac{\mathrm{d}P}{\mathrm{d}Q}(\boldsymbol{\theta})\log\left(\frac{\mathrm{d}P}{\mathrm{d}Q}(\boldsymbol{\theta})\right)\mathrm{d}Q\left(\boldsymbol{\theta}\right)

\displaystyle\mathrm{s.~t.}

\displaystyle\int\frac{\mathrm{d}P}{\mathrm{d}Q}(\boldsymbol{\theta})\mathrm{d}Q\left(\boldsymbol{\theta}\right)=1.

(245b)

The remainder of the proof focuses on the problem in which the optimization is over the function
\frac{\mathrm{d}P}{\mathrm{d}Q}
instead of the measure
P
. This is due to the fact that for all
P\in\bigtriangleup_{Q}\left(\mathcal{M}\right)
, the Radon-Nikodym derivate
\frac{\mathrm{d}P}{\mathrm{d}Q}
is unique up to sets of zero measure with respect to the measure
Q
.
Let
\mathscr{M}
be the set of measurable functions
\mathcal{M}\to\mathds{R}
with respect to the measurable spaces
\left(\mathcal{M},\mathscr{B}\left(\mathcal{M}\right)\right)
and
\left(\mathds{R},\mathscr{B}\left(\mathds{R}\right)\right)
that are absolutely integrable with respect to
Q
.
That is, for all
\hat{g}\in\mathscr{M}
, it holds that

\displaystyle\int\left|\hat{g}(\boldsymbol{\theta})\right|\mathrm{d}Q(\boldsymbol{\theta})

\displaystyle<

\displaystyle\infty.

(246)

Hence, the optimization problem of interest is:

\displaystyle\min_{g\in\mathscr{M}}

\displaystyle\int\mathsf{L}_{\boldsymbol{z}}\left(\boldsymbol{\theta}\right)g(\boldsymbol{\theta})\mathrm{d}Q(\boldsymbol{\theta})+\lambda\int g(\boldsymbol{\theta})\log\left(g(\boldsymbol{\theta})\right)\mathrm{d}Q\left(\boldsymbol{\theta}\right)

(247a)

\displaystyle\mathrm{s.~t.}

\displaystyle\int g(\boldsymbol{\theta})\mathrm{d}Q\left(\boldsymbol{\theta}\right)=1.

(247b)

Let the Lagrangian of the optimization problem in (
247
) be the functional
L:\mathscr{M}\times\mathds{R}\rightarrow\mathds{R}
such that

\displaystyle L\left(g,\beta\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\displaystyle\int\mathsf{L}_{\boldsymbol{z}}\left(\boldsymbol{\nu}\right)g\left(\boldsymbol{\nu}\right)\mathrm{d}Q\left(\boldsymbol{\nu}\right)+\lambda\displaystyle\int g\left(\boldsymbol{\nu}\right)\log\left(g\left(\boldsymbol{\nu}\right)\right)\mathrm{d}Q\left(\boldsymbol{\nu}\right)

(248)

\displaystyle+\beta\left(\displaystyle\int g\left(\boldsymbol{\nu}\right)\mathrm{d}Q\left(\boldsymbol{\nu}\right)-1\right),

where
\beta
is a real that acts as a Lagrangian multiplier due to the constraint (
247b
).
Let
\hat{g}:\mathcal{M}\rightarrow\mathds{R}
be a function in
\mathscr{M}
.
The Gateaux differential of the functional
L
in (
248
) at
\left(g,\beta\right)\in\mathscr{M}\times\mathds{R}
in the direction of
\hat{g}
, if it exists, is

\displaystyle\partial L(g,\beta;\hat{g})

\displaystyle\triangleq

\displaystyle\left.\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}\gamma}L(g+\gamma\hat{g},\beta)\right|_{\gamma=0}.

(249)

The proof continues under the assumption that the functions
g
and
\hat{g}
are such that the Gateaux differential in (
249
) exists.
Under such an assumption, let the function
r:\mathds{R}\rightarrow\mathds{R}
satisfy for all
\alpha\in(-\epsilon,\epsilon)
, with
\epsilon
arbitrarily small, that

\displaystyle r(\alpha)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\displaystyle\int\mathsf{L}_{\boldsymbol{z}}\left(\boldsymbol{\nu}\right)\left(g\left(\boldsymbol{\nu}\right)+\alpha\hat{g}\left(\boldsymbol{\nu}\right)\right)\mathrm{d}Q\left(\boldsymbol{\nu}\right)

(250)

\displaystyle+\beta\left(\displaystyle\int\left(g\left(\boldsymbol{\nu}\right)+\alpha\hat{g}\left(\boldsymbol{\nu}\right)\right)\mathrm{d}Q\left(\boldsymbol{\nu}\right)-1\right)

\displaystyle+\lambda\int\left(\hat{g}\left(\boldsymbol{\nu}\right)+\alpha\hat{g}\left(\boldsymbol{\nu}\right)\right)\log\left(g\left(\boldsymbol{\nu}\right)+\alpha\hat{g}\left(\boldsymbol{\nu}\right)\right)\mathrm{d}Q\left(\boldsymbol{\nu}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\displaystyle\int g\left(\boldsymbol{\nu}\right)\left(\mathsf{L}_{\boldsymbol{z}}\left(\boldsymbol{\nu}\right)+\beta\right)\mathrm{d}Q\left(\boldsymbol{\nu}\right)-\beta

\displaystyle+\alpha\left(\displaystyle\int\hat{g}\left(\boldsymbol{\nu}\right)\left(\mathsf{L}_{\boldsymbol{z}}\left(\boldsymbol{\nu}\right)+\beta\right)\mathrm{d}Q\left(\boldsymbol{\nu}\right)\right)

\displaystyle+\lambda\int\left(g\left(\boldsymbol{\nu}\right)+\alpha\hat{g}\left(\boldsymbol{\nu}\right)\right)\log\left(g\left(\boldsymbol{\nu}\right)+\alpha\hat{g}\left(\boldsymbol{\nu}\right)\right)\mathrm{d}Q\left(\boldsymbol{\nu}\right),

(251)

where the last equality is simply an algebraic re-arrangement of terms.
From the assumption that the functions
g
and
\hat{g}
are such that the Gateaux differential in (
249
) exists, it follows that the function
r
in (
251
) is differentiable at zero.
Note that the first two terms in (
251
) are independent of
\alpha
; the third term is linear with
\alpha
; and the fourth term can be written using the function
\hat{r}:\mathds{R}\to\mathds{R}
such that for all
\alpha\in(-\epsilon,\epsilon)
, with
\epsilon
arbitrarily small, satisfies

\displaystyle\hat{r}(\alpha)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\lambda\int\left(g\left(\boldsymbol{\nu}\right)+\alpha\hat{g}\left(\boldsymbol{\nu}\right)\right)\log\left(g\left(\boldsymbol{\nu}\right)+\alpha\hat{g}\left(\boldsymbol{\nu}\right)\right)\mathrm{d}Q\left(\boldsymbol{\nu}\right)

(252)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\lambda\int f\left(g\left(\boldsymbol{\nu}\right)+\alpha\hat{g}\left(\boldsymbol{\nu}\right)\right)\mathrm{d}Q\left(\boldsymbol{\nu}\right),

(253)

where
f:(0,+\infty)\to\mathds{R}
is such that
f(t)=t\log(t)
.
Under the same assumption, it follows that the function
\hat{r}
in (
252
) is differentiable at zero. That is, the limit

\displaystyle\lim_{\delta\to 0}\frac{1}{\delta}(\hat{r}(\gamma+\delta)-\hat{r}(\gamma))

(254)

exists for all
\gamma\in(-\epsilon,\epsilon)
, with
\epsilon
arbitrarily small.
Note that the function
f
in (
253
) is continuous and differentiable (with finite derivate) in
(0,+\infty)
.
Thus, the function
f
is also Lipschitz continuous.
This implies that for all
\boldsymbol{\theta}\in\mathrm{supp}\,Q
, and for all
\gamma\in(-\epsilon,\epsilon)
, with
\epsilon>0
arbitrarily small, it holds that

\displaystyle\left|f(g(\boldsymbol{\theta})+(\gamma+\delta)\hat{g}(\boldsymbol{\theta}))-f(g(\boldsymbol{\theta})+\gamma\hat{g}(\boldsymbol{\theta}))\right|

\displaystyle\leqslant

\displaystyle c\left|\hat{g}(\boldsymbol{\theta})\right|\left|\delta\right|,

(255)

with
\delta>0
, for some constant
c
positive and finite. This implies that

\displaystyle\left|\frac{f(g(\boldsymbol{\theta})+(\gamma+\delta)\hat{g}(\boldsymbol{\theta}))-f(g(\boldsymbol{\theta})+\gamma\hat{g}(\boldsymbol{\theta}))}{\delta}\right|

\displaystyle\leqslant

\displaystyle c\left|\hat{g}(\boldsymbol{\theta})\right|.

(256)

Using these arguments, the limit in (
254
) satisfies for all
\gamma\in(-\epsilon,\epsilon)
, with
\epsilon>0
arbitrarily small, that

\displaystyle\lim_{\delta\to 0}\frac{1}{\delta}(\hat{r}(\gamma+\delta)-\hat{r}(\gamma))

(257)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\lambda\lim_{\delta\to 0}\int\frac{f(g(\boldsymbol{\theta})+(\gamma+\delta)\hat{g}(\boldsymbol{\theta}))-f(g(\boldsymbol{\theta})+\gamma\hat{g}(\boldsymbol{\theta}))}{\delta}\,\mathrm{d}Q(\boldsymbol{\theta})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\lambda\int\dot{f}(g(\boldsymbol{\theta})+\gamma\hat{g}(\boldsymbol{\theta}))\hat{g}(\boldsymbol{\theta})\,\mathrm{d}Q(\boldsymbol{\theta})

\displaystyle<

\displaystyle\infty,

(258)

where the function
\dot{f}:\left(0,+\infty\right)\to\mathds{R}
is the derivative of
f
. That is,
\dot{f}(t)=1+\log(t)
.
The equality in (
257
) and the inequality in (
258
) follow from noticing that the conditions for the dominated convergence theorem hold
[
59
, Theorem
1.6.9
]
, namely:

•

For all
\gamma\in(-\epsilon,\epsilon)
, with
\epsilon>0
, the inequality in (
256
) holds;

•

The function
\hat{g}
in (
256
) satisfies the inequality in (
246
); and

•

For all
\boldsymbol{\theta}\in\mathrm{supp}\,Q
and for all
\gamma\in(-\epsilon,\epsilon)
, with
\epsilon>0
arbitrarily small, it holds that

\displaystyle\lim_{\delta\to 0}\frac{f(g(\boldsymbol{\theta})+(\gamma+\delta)\hat{g}(\boldsymbol{\theta}))-f(g(\boldsymbol{\theta})+\gamma\hat{g}(\boldsymbol{\theta}))}{\delta}

(259)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}\gamma}f(g(\boldsymbol{\theta})+\gamma\hat{g}(\boldsymbol{\theta}))

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\dot{f}(g(\boldsymbol{\theta})+\gamma\hat{g}(\boldsymbol{\theta}))\hat{g}(\boldsymbol{\theta}).

(260)

Hence, the derivative of the real function
r
in (
251
) is

\displaystyle\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}\alpha}r(\alpha)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\displaystyle\int\mathsf{L}_{\boldsymbol{z}}\left(\boldsymbol{\nu}\right)\hat{g}\left(\boldsymbol{\nu}\right)\mathrm{d}Q\left(\boldsymbol{\nu}\right)+\beta\displaystyle\int\hat{g}\left(\boldsymbol{\nu}\right)\mathrm{d}Q\left(\boldsymbol{\nu}\right)

(261)

\displaystyle+

\displaystyle\lambda\displaystyle\int\hat{g}\left(\boldsymbol{\nu}\right)\left(1+\log\left(g\left(\boldsymbol{\nu}\right)+\alpha\hat{g}\left(\boldsymbol{\nu}\right)\right)\right)\mathrm{d}Q\left(\boldsymbol{\nu}\right).

From (
249
) and (
261
), it follows that

\displaystyle\partial L\left(g,\beta;\hat{g}\right)

(262)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\displaystyle\int\hat{g}\left(\boldsymbol{\nu}\right)\left(\mathsf{L}_{\boldsymbol{z}}\left(\boldsymbol{\nu}\right)+\lambda\left(1+\log\left(g\left(\boldsymbol{\nu}\right)\right)\right)+\beta\right)\mathrm{d}Q\left(\boldsymbol{\nu}\right).

The relevance of the Gateaux differential in (
262
) stems from
[
73
, Theorem
1
, page
178
]
, which unveils the fact that a necessary condition for the functional
L
in (
248
) to have a minimum at
\left(\frac{\mathrm{d}P^{\left(Q,\lambda\right)}_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}|\boldsymbol{Z}=\boldsymbol{z}}}{\mathrm{d}Q},\beta\right)\in\mathscr{M}\times\mathds{R}
is that for all functions
\hat{g}\in\mathscr{M}
,

\partial L\left(\frac{\mathrm{d}P^{\left(Q,\lambda\right)}_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}|\boldsymbol{Z}=\boldsymbol{z}}}{\mathrm{d}Q},\beta;\hat{g}\right)=0.

(263)

From (
263
), it follows that
\frac{\mathrm{d}P^{\left(Q,\lambda\right)}_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}|\boldsymbol{Z}=\boldsymbol{z}}}{\mathrm{d}Q}~
must satisfy for all functions
\hat{g}
in
\mathscr{M}
that

\displaystyle 0=

\displaystyle\displaystyle\int\hat{g}\left(\boldsymbol{\nu}\right)\left(\mathsf{L}_{\boldsymbol{z}}\left(\boldsymbol{\nu}\right)+\lambda\left(1+\log\left(\frac{\mathrm{d}P^{\left(Q,\lambda\right)}_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}|\boldsymbol{Z}=\boldsymbol{z}}}{\mathrm{d}Q}\left(\boldsymbol{\nu}\right)\right)\right)+\beta\right)\mathrm{d}Q\left(\boldsymbol{\nu}\right),

which implies that for all
\boldsymbol{\nu}\in\mathrm{supp}\,Q
,

\mathsf{L}_{\boldsymbol{z}}\left(\boldsymbol{\nu}\right)+\lambda\left(1+\log\left(\frac{\mathrm{d}P^{\left(Q,\lambda\right)}_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}|\boldsymbol{Z}=\boldsymbol{z}}}{\mathrm{d}Q}\left(\boldsymbol{\nu}\right)\right)\right)+\beta=0,

(264)

and thus,

\frac{\mathrm{d}P^{\left(Q,\lambda\right)}_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}|\boldsymbol{Z}=\boldsymbol{z}}}{\mathrm{d}Q}\left(\boldsymbol{\nu}\right)=\exp\left(-\frac{\beta+\lambda}{\lambda}\right)\exp\left(-\frac{\mathsf{L}_{\boldsymbol{z}}\left(\boldsymbol{\nu}\right)}{\lambda}\right),

(265)

with
\beta
chosen to satisfy (
245b
).
That is,

\displaystyle\frac{\mathrm{d}P^{\left(Q,\lambda\right)}_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}|\boldsymbol{Z}=\boldsymbol{z}}}{\mathrm{d}Q}\left(\boldsymbol{\nu}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{\exp\left(-\frac{\mathsf{L}_{\boldsymbol{z}}\left(\boldsymbol{\nu}\right)}{\lambda}\right)}{\displaystyle\int\exp\left(-\frac{\mathsf{L}_{\boldsymbol{z}}\left(\boldsymbol{\theta}\right)}{\lambda}\right)\mathrm{d}Q\left(\boldsymbol{\theta}\right)}

(266)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\exp\left(-K_{Q,\boldsymbol{z}}\left(-\frac{1}{\lambda}\right)-\frac{1}{\lambda}\mathsf{L}_{\boldsymbol{z}}\left(\boldsymbol{\nu}\right)\right).

(267)

The proof continues by verifying that the measure
P^{\left(Q,\lambda\right)}_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}|\boldsymbol{Z}=\boldsymbol{z}}
that satisfies (
266
) is the unique solution to the ERM-RER problem in (
19
). Such verification is done by showing that the objective function in (
19
) is strictly convex with the optimization variable. Let
P_{1}
and
P_{2}
be two different probability measures in
\left(\mathcal{M},\mathscr{B}\left(\mathcal{M}\right)\right)
and let
\alpha
be in
(0,1)
. Hence,

\displaystyle\mathsf{R}_{\boldsymbol{z}}\left(\alpha P_{1}+(1-\alpha)P_{2}\right)+\lambda D\left(\alpha P_{1}+(1-\alpha)P_{2}\|Q\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\alpha\mathsf{R}_{\boldsymbol{z}}\left(P_{1}\right)+(1-\alpha)\mathsf{R}_{\boldsymbol{z}}\left(P_{2}\right)+\lambda D\left(\alpha P_{1}+(1-\alpha)P_{2}\|Q\right)

\displaystyle>

\displaystyle\alpha\left(\mathsf{R}_{\boldsymbol{z}}\left(P_{1}\right)+\lambda D\left(P_{1}\|Q\right)\right)+(1-\alpha)\left(\mathsf{R}_{\boldsymbol{z}}\left(P_{2}\right)+\lambda D\left(P_{2}\|Q\right)\right)

where the functional
\mathsf{R}_{\boldsymbol{z}}
is defined in (
18
). The equality above follows from the properties of the Lebesgue integral, while the inequality follows from Theorem
2
. This proves that the solution is unique due to the strict concavity of the objective function, which completes the proof.

Appendix D
Proof of Lemma
2

From Theorem
3
, it follows that for all
\boldsymbol{\theta}\in\mathrm{supp}\,Q
,

\displaystyle\frac{\mathrm{d}P^{\left(Q,\lambda\right)}_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}|\boldsymbol{Z}=\boldsymbol{z}}}{\mathrm{d}Q}\left(\boldsymbol{\theta}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\exp\left(-K_{Q,\boldsymbol{z}}\left(-\frac{1}{\lambda}\right)-\frac{1}{\lambda}\mathsf{L}_{\boldsymbol{z}}\left(\boldsymbol{\theta}\right)\right)

(268)

\displaystyle\leqslant

\displaystyle\exp\left(-K_{Q,\boldsymbol{z}}\left(-\frac{1}{\lambda}\right)\right)

(269)

\displaystyle<

\displaystyle+\infty,

(270)

where the inequality in (
269
) follows from the fact that the function
\mathsf{L}_{\boldsymbol{z}}
is nonnegative; and the equality in (
270
) follows from the fact that
\lambda\in\mathcal{K}_{Q,\boldsymbol{z}}
.
This completes the proof of finiteness.

The proof of positivity follows from observing that
\lambda\in\mathcal{K}_{Q,\boldsymbol{z}}
and thus,
K_{Q,\boldsymbol{z}}\left(-\frac{1}{\lambda}\right)<+\infty
.
Moreover, for all
\boldsymbol{\theta}\in\mathrm{supp}\,Q
, it holds that
\mathsf{L}_{\boldsymbol{z}}\left(\boldsymbol{\theta}\right)\leqslant+\infty
, with equality if and only if
\mathsf{L}_{\boldsymbol{z}}\left(\boldsymbol{\theta}\right)=+\infty
.
These two observations put together yield

\displaystyle\frac{\mathrm{d}P^{\left(Q,\lambda\right)}_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}|\boldsymbol{Z}=\boldsymbol{z}}}{\mathrm{d}Q}\left(\boldsymbol{\theta}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\exp\left(-K_{Q,\boldsymbol{z}}\left(-\frac{1}{\lambda}\right)-\frac{1}{\lambda}\mathsf{L}_{\boldsymbol{z}}\left(\boldsymbol{\theta}\right)\right)

(271)

\displaystyle\geqslant

\displaystyle 0,

(272)

with equality if and only if
\mathsf{L}_{\boldsymbol{z}}\left(\boldsymbol{\theta}\right)=+\infty
.
The proof continues by showing that
Q\left(\left\{\boldsymbol{\theta}\in\mathcal{M}:\mathsf{L}_{\boldsymbol{z}}\left(\boldsymbol{\theta}\right)=+\infty\right\}\right)=0
. From the assumption that
\lambda\in\mathcal{K}_{Q,\boldsymbol{z}}
, it follows that

\displaystyle+\infty

\displaystyle>

\displaystyle\log\left(\int\exp\left(-\frac{1}{\lambda}\mathsf{L}_{\boldsymbol{z}}\left(\boldsymbol{\theta}\right)\right)\mathrm{d}Q\left(\boldsymbol{\theta}\right)\right)

(273)

\displaystyle\geqslant

\displaystyle\log\left(\int\left(1-\frac{1}{\lambda}\mathsf{L}_{\boldsymbol{z}}\left(\boldsymbol{\theta}\right)\right)\mathrm{d}Q\left(\boldsymbol{\theta}\right)\right)

(274)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\log\left(Q\left(\mathcal{M}\right)-\frac{1}{\lambda}\int\mathsf{L}_{\boldsymbol{z}}\left(\boldsymbol{\theta}\right)\mathrm{d}Q\left(\boldsymbol{\theta}\right)\right),

(275)

where the inequality in (
274
) holds from the fact that for all
x\geqslant 0
,
\exp(x)>1+x
.
Hence, the inequality in (
275
) implies that

\displaystyle Q\left(\mathcal{M}\right)

\displaystyle<

\displaystyle+\infty\mbox{ and }

(276)

\displaystyle\int\mathsf{L}_{\boldsymbol{z}}\left(\boldsymbol{\theta}\right)\mathrm{d}Q\left(\boldsymbol{\theta}\right)

\displaystyle<

\displaystyle+\infty.

(277)

Finally, from
[
59
, Lemma
1.6.6
]
and the inequality in (
277
) it follows that
Q\left(\left\{\boldsymbol{\theta}\in\mathcal{M}:\mathsf{L}_{\boldsymbol{z}}\left(\boldsymbol{\theta}\right)=+\infty\right\}\right)=0
. Hence,

\displaystyle\frac{\mathrm{d}P^{\left(Q,\lambda\right)}_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}|\boldsymbol{Z}=\boldsymbol{z}}}{\mathrm{d}Q}\left(\boldsymbol{\theta}\right)

\displaystyle>

\displaystyle 0,

(278)

almost surely with respect to the measure
Q
. And this completes the proof.

Appendix E
Proof of Lemma
3

The probability measure
P^{\left(Q,\lambda\right)}_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}|\boldsymbol{Z}=\boldsymbol{z}}
in (
25
) satisfies for all
\mathcal{C}\in\mathscr{B}\left(\mathcal{M}\right)
,

\displaystyle P^{\left(Q,\lambda\right)}_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}|\boldsymbol{Z}=\boldsymbol{z}}\left(\mathcal{C}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int_{\mathcal{C}}\frac{\mathrm{d}P^{\left(Q,\lambda\right)}_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}|\boldsymbol{Z}=\boldsymbol{z}}}{\mathrm{d}Q}\left(\boldsymbol{\theta}\right)\mathrm{d}Q\left(\boldsymbol{\theta}\right),

(279)

and thus, if
Q\left(\mathcal{C}\right)=0
, then

\displaystyle P^{\left(Q,\lambda\right)}_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}|\boldsymbol{Z}=\boldsymbol{z}}\left(\mathcal{C}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 0,

(280)

which implies the absolute continuity of
P^{\left(Q,\lambda\right)}_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}|\boldsymbol{Z}=\boldsymbol{z}}
with respect to
Q
.

Alternatively, given a set
\mathcal{C}\in\mathscr{B}\left(\mathcal{M}\right)
, assume now that
P^{\left(Q,\lambda\right)}_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}|\boldsymbol{Z}=\boldsymbol{z}}\left(\mathcal{C}\right)=0
. Hence, it follows that

\displaystyle 0

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle P^{\left(Q,\lambda\right)}_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}|\boldsymbol{Z}=\boldsymbol{z}}\left(\mathcal{C}\right)

(281)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int_{\mathcal{C}}\frac{\mathrm{d}P^{\left(Q,\lambda\right)}_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}|\boldsymbol{Z}=\boldsymbol{z}}}{\mathrm{d}Q}\left(\boldsymbol{\theta}\right)\mathrm{d}Q\left(\boldsymbol{\theta}\right).

(282)

From Lemma
2
, it follows that
Q\left(\left\{\boldsymbol{\theta}\in\mathcal{M}:\frac{\mathrm{d}P^{\left(Q,\lambda\right)}_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}|\boldsymbol{Z}=\boldsymbol{z}}}{\mathrm{d}Q}\left(\boldsymbol{\theta}\right)=0\right\}\right)=0
. Hence,

\displaystyle\int_{\mathcal{C}}\frac{\mathrm{d}P^{\left(Q,\lambda\right)}_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}|\boldsymbol{Z}=\boldsymbol{z}}}{\mathrm{d}Q}\left(\boldsymbol{\theta}\right)\mathrm{d}Q\left(\boldsymbol{\theta}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 0,

(283)

if and only if
Q\left(\mathcal{C}\right)=0
. This verifies the absolute continuity of
Q
with respect to
P^{\left(Q,\lambda\right)}_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}|\boldsymbol{Z}=\boldsymbol{z}}
, and completes the proof.

Appendix F
Proof of Lemma
4

Consider the function
g:\mathcal{M}\rightarrow[0,+\infty)
,

g(\boldsymbol{\theta})=\frac{\mathrm{d}P^{(Q,\alpha)}_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}|\boldsymbol{Z}=\boldsymbol{z}}}{\mathrm{d}Q}\left(\boldsymbol{\theta}\right)\left(\frac{\mathrm{d}P^{(Q,\beta)}_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}|\boldsymbol{Z}=\boldsymbol{z}}}{\mathrm{d}Q}\left(\boldsymbol{\theta}\right)\right)^{-1},

(284)

and note that for all
\boldsymbol{\theta}\in\mathrm{supp}\,Q

\setminus

\big\{\boldsymbol{\nu}\in\mathcal{M}
:
\mathsf{L}_{\boldsymbol{z}}\left(\boldsymbol{\nu}\right)=+\infty\big\}
,
g\left(\boldsymbol{\theta}\right)>0
.
Alternatively, for all
\boldsymbol{\theta}\in\left\{\boldsymbol{\nu}\in\mathcal{M}:\mathsf{L}_{\boldsymbol{z}}\left(\boldsymbol{\nu}\right)=+\infty\right\}~
,
g\left(\boldsymbol{\theta}\right)=0
, which follows from the assumption
0\cdot\frac{1}{0}=0
.

Consider a measure
P
on
\left(\mathcal{M},\mathscr{B}\left(\mathcal{M}\right)\right)
, such that for all sets
\mathcal{A}\in\mathscr{B}\left(\mathcal{M}\right)
,

P\left(\mathcal{A}\right)=\int_{\mathcal{A}}g(\boldsymbol{\theta})\mathrm{d}P^{(Q,\beta)}_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}|\boldsymbol{Z}=\boldsymbol{z}}(\boldsymbol{\theta}),

(285)

and note that if
P^{(Q,\beta)}_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}|\boldsymbol{Z}=\boldsymbol{z}}(\mathcal{A})=0
, then
P\left(\mathcal{A}\right)=0
. This implies that
P
is absolutely continuous with respect to
P^{(Q,\beta)}_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}|\boldsymbol{Z}=\boldsymbol{z}}(\mathcal{A})
.
Moreover, from (
285
), it follows that

\displaystyle P\left(\mathcal{A}\right)

(286)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int_{\mathcal{A}}\frac{\mathrm{d}P^{(Q,\alpha)}_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}|\boldsymbol{Z}=\boldsymbol{z}}}{\mathrm{d}Q}\left(\boldsymbol{\theta}\right)\left(\frac{\mathrm{d}P^{(Q,\beta)}_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}|\boldsymbol{Z}=\boldsymbol{z}}}{\mathrm{d}Q}\left(\boldsymbol{\theta}\right)\right)^{-1}\mathrm{d}P^{(Q,\beta)}_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}|\boldsymbol{Z}=\boldsymbol{z}}(\boldsymbol{\theta})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\hskip-4.30554pt\int_{\mathcal{A}}\frac{\mathrm{d}P^{(Q,\alpha)}_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}|\boldsymbol{Z}=\boldsymbol{z}}}{\mathrm{d}Q}\left(\boldsymbol{\theta}\right)\left(\frac{\mathrm{d}P^{(Q,\beta)}_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}|\boldsymbol{Z}=\boldsymbol{z}}}{\mathrm{d}Q}\left(\boldsymbol{\theta}\right)\right)^{-1}\frac{\mathrm{d}P^{(Q,\beta)}_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}|\boldsymbol{Z}=\boldsymbol{z}}}{\mathrm{d}Q}\left(\boldsymbol{\theta}\right)\mathrm{d}Q(\boldsymbol{\theta})

(287)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int_{\mathcal{A}}\frac{\mathrm{d}P^{(Q,\alpha)}_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}|\boldsymbol{Z}=\boldsymbol{z}}}{\mathrm{d}Q}\left(\boldsymbol{\theta}\right)\mathrm{d}Q(\boldsymbol{\theta})

(288)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int_{\mathcal{A}}\mathrm{d}P^{(Q,\alpha)}_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}|\boldsymbol{Z}=\boldsymbol{z}}(\boldsymbol{\theta})

(289)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle P^{(Q,\alpha)}_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}|\boldsymbol{Z}=\boldsymbol{z}}(\mathcal{A}),

(290)

which implies that the probability measures
P
in (
285
) and
P^{(Q,\alpha)}_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}|\boldsymbol{Z}=\boldsymbol{z}}
are identical.
Thus,
P^{(Q,\alpha)}_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}|\boldsymbol{Z}=\boldsymbol{z}}
is absolutely continuous with respect to
P^{(Q,\beta)}_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}|\boldsymbol{Z}=\boldsymbol{z}}
.
The proof that
P^{(Q,\beta)}_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}|\boldsymbol{Z}=\boldsymbol{z}}
is absolutely continuous with respect to
P^{(Q,\alpha)}_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}|\boldsymbol{Z}=\boldsymbol{z}}
follows the same argument. This completes the proof.

Appendix G
Proof of Lemma
6

From Theorem
3
, the probability measure
P^{\left(Q,\lambda\right)}_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}|\boldsymbol{Z}=\boldsymbol{z}}
in (
25
) satisfies for all
\boldsymbol{\theta}\in\mathrm{supp}\,Q
,

\displaystyle\frac{\mathrm{d}P^{\left(Q,\lambda\right)}_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}|\boldsymbol{Z}=\boldsymbol{z}}}{\mathrm{d}Q}\left(\boldsymbol{\theta}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{\exp\left(-\frac{\mathsf{L}_{\boldsymbol{z}}\left(\boldsymbol{\theta}\right)}{\lambda}\right)}{\displaystyle\int\exp\left(-\frac{\mathsf{L}_{\boldsymbol{z}}\left(\boldsymbol{\nu}\right)}{\lambda}\right)\mathrm{d}Q\left(\boldsymbol{\nu}\right)}

(291)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left(\exp\left(\frac{\mathsf{L}_{\boldsymbol{z}}\left(\boldsymbol{\theta}\right)}{\lambda}\right)\displaystyle\int\exp\left(-\frac{\mathsf{L}_{\boldsymbol{z}}\left(\boldsymbol{\nu}\right)}{\lambda}\right)\mathrm{d}Q\left(\boldsymbol{\nu}\right)\right)^{-1}

(292)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left(\displaystyle\int\exp\left(\frac{1}{\lambda}\left(\mathsf{L}_{\boldsymbol{z}}\left(\boldsymbol{\theta}\right)-\mathsf{L}_{\boldsymbol{z}}\left(\boldsymbol{\nu}\right)\right)\right)\mathrm{d}Q\left(\boldsymbol{\nu}\right)\right)^{-1}.

(293)

Given
\boldsymbol{\theta}\in\mathrm{supp}\,Q
, consider the partition of
\mathrm{supp}\,Q
formed by the sets
\mathcal{A}_{0}\left(\boldsymbol{\theta}\right)
,
\mathcal{A}_{1}\left(\boldsymbol{\theta}\right)
, and
\mathcal{A}_{2}\left(\boldsymbol{\theta}\right)
, which satisfy the following:

\displaystyle\mathcal{A}_{0}\left(\boldsymbol{\theta}\right)

\displaystyle\triangleq

\displaystyle\left\{\boldsymbol{\nu}\in\mathrm{supp}\,Q:\mathsf{L}_{\boldsymbol{z}}\left(\boldsymbol{\theta}\right)-\mathsf{L}_{\boldsymbol{z}}\left(\boldsymbol{\nu}\right)=0\right\},

(294a)

\displaystyle\mathcal{A}_{1}\left(\boldsymbol{\theta}\right)

\displaystyle\triangleq

\displaystyle\left\{\boldsymbol{\nu}\in\mathrm{supp}\,Q:\mathsf{L}_{\boldsymbol{z}}\left(\boldsymbol{\theta}\right)-\mathsf{L}_{\boldsymbol{z}}\left(\boldsymbol{\nu}\right)<0\right\},\mbox{ and }

(294b)

\displaystyle\mathcal{A}_{2}\left(\boldsymbol{\theta}\right)

\displaystyle\triangleq

\displaystyle\left\{\boldsymbol{\nu}\in\mathrm{supp}\,Q:\mathsf{L}_{\boldsymbol{z}}\left(\boldsymbol{\theta}\right)-\mathsf{L}_{\boldsymbol{z}}\left(\boldsymbol{\nu}\right)>0\right\}.

(294c)

Using the sets
\mathcal{A}_{0}\left(\boldsymbol{\theta}\right)
,
\mathcal{A}_{1}\left(\boldsymbol{\theta}\right)
, and
\mathcal{A}_{2}\left(\boldsymbol{\theta}\right)
in (
293
), the following holds for all
\boldsymbol{\theta}\in\mathrm{supp}\,Q
,

\displaystyle\frac{\mathrm{d}P^{\left(Q,\lambda\right)}_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}|\boldsymbol{Z}=\boldsymbol{z}}}{\mathrm{d}Q}\left(\boldsymbol{\theta}\right)

(295)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\Bigg(\displaystyle\int_{\mathcal{A}_{0}\left(\boldsymbol{\theta}\right)}\exp\left(\frac{1}{\lambda}\left(\mathsf{L}_{\boldsymbol{z}}\left(\boldsymbol{\theta}\right)-\mathsf{L}_{\boldsymbol{z}}\left(\boldsymbol{\nu}\right)\right)\right)\mathrm{d}Q\left(\boldsymbol{\nu}\right)

\displaystyle+

\displaystyle\displaystyle\int_{\mathcal{A}_{1}\left(\boldsymbol{\theta}\right)}\exp\left(\frac{1}{\lambda}\left(\mathsf{L}_{\boldsymbol{z}}\left(\boldsymbol{\theta}\right)-\mathsf{L}_{\boldsymbol{z}}\left(\boldsymbol{\nu}\right)\right)\right)\mathrm{d}Q\left(\boldsymbol{\nu}\right)

\displaystyle+

\displaystyle\displaystyle\int_{\mathcal{A}_{2}\left(\boldsymbol{\theta}\right)}\exp\left(\frac{1}{\lambda}\left(\mathsf{L}_{\boldsymbol{z}}\left(\boldsymbol{\theta}\right)-\mathsf{L}_{\boldsymbol{z}}\left(\boldsymbol{\nu}\right)\right)\right)\mathrm{d}Q\left(\boldsymbol{\nu}\right)\Bigg)^{-1}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\Bigg(Q\left(\mathcal{A}_{0}\left(\boldsymbol{\theta}\right)\right)+\displaystyle\int_{\mathcal{A}_{1}\left(\boldsymbol{\theta}\right)}\exp\left(\frac{1}{\lambda}\left(\mathsf{L}_{\boldsymbol{z}}\left(\boldsymbol{\theta}\right)-\mathsf{L}_{\boldsymbol{z}}\left(\boldsymbol{\nu}\right)\right)\right)\mathrm{d}Q\left(\boldsymbol{\nu}\right)

\displaystyle+

\displaystyle\displaystyle\int_{\mathcal{A}_{2}\left(\boldsymbol{\theta}\right)}\exp\left(\frac{1}{\lambda}\left(\mathsf{L}_{\boldsymbol{z}}\left(\boldsymbol{\theta}\right)-\mathsf{L}_{\boldsymbol{z}}\left(\boldsymbol{\nu}\right)\right)\right)\mathrm{d}Q\left(\boldsymbol{\nu}\right)\Bigg)^{-1}.

(296)

Note that the sets

\displaystyle\left\{\boldsymbol{\nu}\in\mathrm{supp}\,Q:\mathsf{L}_{\boldsymbol{z}}\left(\boldsymbol{\nu}\right)=\delta_{Q,\boldsymbol{z}}^{\star}\right\},

(297)

\displaystyle\left\{\boldsymbol{\nu}\in\mathrm{supp}\,Q:\mathsf{L}_{\boldsymbol{z}}\left(\boldsymbol{\nu}\right)>\delta_{Q,\boldsymbol{z}}^{\star}\right\}\mbox{, and}

(298)

\displaystyle\left\{\boldsymbol{\nu}\in\mathrm{supp}\,Q:\mathsf{L}_{\boldsymbol{z}}\left(\boldsymbol{\nu}\right)<\delta_{Q,\boldsymbol{z}}^{\star}\right\},

(299)

with
\delta_{Q,\boldsymbol{z}}^{\star}
in (
37
), form a partition of the set
\mathrm{supp}\,Q
. Following this observation, the rest of the proof is divided into three parts.
The first part evaluates
\lim_{\lambda\rightarrow 0^{+}}\frac{\mathrm{d}P^{\left(Q,\lambda\right)}_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}|\boldsymbol{Z}=\boldsymbol{z}}}{\mathrm{d}Q}\left(\boldsymbol{\theta}\right)
, with
\boldsymbol{\theta}\in\left\{\boldsymbol{\nu}\in\mathcal{M}:\mathsf{L}_{\boldsymbol{z}}\left(\boldsymbol{\nu}\right)=\delta_{Q,\boldsymbol{z}}^{\star}\right\}
.
The second part considers the case in which
\boldsymbol{\theta}\in\left\{\boldsymbol{\nu}\in\mathcal{M}:\mathsf{L}_{\boldsymbol{z}}\left(\boldsymbol{\nu}\right)>\delta_{Q,\boldsymbol{z}}^{\star}\right\}
.
The third part considers the remaining case.

The first part is as follows.
Consider that
\boldsymbol{\theta}\in\left\{\boldsymbol{\nu}\in\mathcal{M}:\mathsf{L}_{\boldsymbol{z}}\left(\boldsymbol{\nu}\right)=\delta_{Q,\boldsymbol{z}}^{\star}\right\}
and note that
\left\{\boldsymbol{\nu}\in\mathcal{M}:\mathsf{L}_{\boldsymbol{z}}\left(\boldsymbol{\nu}\right)=\delta_{Q,\boldsymbol{z}}^{\star}\right\}=\mathcal{L}^{\star}_{Q,\boldsymbol{z}}
. Hence, the sets
\mathcal{A}_{0}\left(\boldsymbol{\theta}\right)
,
\mathcal{A}_{1}\left(\boldsymbol{\theta}\right)
, and
\mathcal{A}_{2}\left(\boldsymbol{\theta}\right)
in (
294
) satisfy the following:

\displaystyle\mathcal{A}_{0}\left(\boldsymbol{\theta}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}^{\star}_{Q,\boldsymbol{z}},

(300a)

\displaystyle\mathcal{A}_{1}\left(\boldsymbol{\theta}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left\{\boldsymbol{\mu}\in\mathrm{supp}\,Q:\mathsf{L}_{\boldsymbol{z}}\left(\boldsymbol{\mu}\right)>\delta_{Q,\boldsymbol{z}}^{\star}\right\},\mbox{ and }

(300b)

\displaystyle\mathcal{A}_{2}\left(\boldsymbol{\theta}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left\{\boldsymbol{\mu}\in\mathrm{supp}\,Q:\mathsf{L}_{\boldsymbol{z}}\left(\boldsymbol{\mu}\right)<\delta_{Q,\boldsymbol{z}}^{\star}\right\}.

(300c)

From the definition of
\delta_{Q,\boldsymbol{z}}^{\star}
in (
37
), it follows that
Q\left(\mathcal{A}_{2}\left(\boldsymbol{\theta}\right)\right)=0
. Plugging the equalities in (
300
) in (
296
) yields for all
\boldsymbol{\theta}\in\left\{\boldsymbol{\nu}\in\mathcal{M}:\mathsf{L}_{\boldsymbol{z}}\left(\boldsymbol{\nu}\right)=\delta_{Q,\boldsymbol{z}}^{\star}\right\}
,

\displaystyle\frac{\mathrm{d}P^{\left(Q,\lambda\right)}_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}|\boldsymbol{Z}=\boldsymbol{z}}}{\mathrm{d}Q}\left(\boldsymbol{\theta}\right)

(301)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\bigg(Q\left(\mathcal{L}^{\star}_{Q,\boldsymbol{z}}\right)+\displaystyle\int_{\mathcal{A}_{1}\left(\boldsymbol{\theta}\right)}\hskip-8.61108pt\exp\left(\frac{1}{\lambda}\left(\mathsf{L}_{\boldsymbol{z}}\left(\boldsymbol{\theta}\right)-\mathsf{L}_{\boldsymbol{z}}\left(\boldsymbol{\nu}\right)\right)\right)\mathrm{d}Q\left(\boldsymbol{\nu}\right)\bigg)^{-1}.

The equality in (
301
) implies that for all
\boldsymbol{\theta}\in\left\{\boldsymbol{\nu}\in\mathcal{M}:\mathsf{L}_{\boldsymbol{z}}\left(\boldsymbol{\nu}\right)=\delta_{Q,\boldsymbol{z}}^{\star}\right\}~
,

\displaystyle\lim_{\lambda\rightarrow 0^{+}}\frac{\mathrm{d}P^{\left(Q,\lambda\right)}_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}|\boldsymbol{Z}=\boldsymbol{z}}}{\mathrm{d}Q}\left(\boldsymbol{\theta}\right)

(302)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\bigg(\lim_{\lambda\rightarrow 0^{+}}\displaystyle\int_{\mathcal{A}_{1}\left(\boldsymbol{\theta}\right)}\exp\left(\frac{1}{\lambda}\left(\mathsf{L}_{\boldsymbol{z}}\left(\boldsymbol{\theta}\right)-\mathsf{L}_{\boldsymbol{z}}\left(\boldsymbol{\nu}\right)\right)\right)\mathrm{d}Q\left(\boldsymbol{\nu}\right)+

\displaystyle+

\displaystyle Q\left(\mathcal{L}^{\star}_{Q,\boldsymbol{z}}\right)\bigg)^{-1}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left\{\begin{array}[]{cl}+\infty&\text{ if }Q\left(\mathcal{L}^{\star}_{Q,\boldsymbol{z}}\right)=0\\
\frac{1}{Q\left(\mathcal{L}^{\star}_{Q,\boldsymbol{z}}\right)}&\text{ otherwise}.\end{array}\right.

where
the equality in (
G
) follows from verifying that the dominated convergence theorem
[
59
, Theorem
2.6.9
]
holds. That is,

(a)
For all
\boldsymbol{\nu}\in\mathcal{A}_{1}\left(\boldsymbol{\theta}\right)
, it holds that
\exp\left(\frac{1}{\lambda}\left(\mathsf{L}_{\boldsymbol{z}}\left(\boldsymbol{\theta}\right)-\mathsf{L}_{\boldsymbol{z}}\left(\boldsymbol{\nu}\right)\right)\right)

<1
; and

(b)
For all
\boldsymbol{\nu}\in\mathcal{A}_{1}\left(\boldsymbol{\theta}\right)
, it holds that

\lim_{\lambda\rightarrow 0^{+}}\exp\left(\frac{1}{\lambda}\left(\mathsf{L}_{\boldsymbol{z}}\left(\boldsymbol{\theta}\right)-\mathsf{L}_{\boldsymbol{z}}\left(\boldsymbol{\nu}\right)\right)\right)=0.

(306)

This completes the first part of the proof.

The second part is as follows. For all
\delta>\delta_{Q,\boldsymbol{z}}^{\star}
and for all
\boldsymbol{\theta}

\in

\big\{\boldsymbol{\nu}\in\mathrm{supp}\,Q:

\mathsf{L}_{\boldsymbol{z}}\left(\boldsymbol{\nu}\right)=\delta\big\}
, the sets
\mathcal{A}_{0}\left(\boldsymbol{\theta}\right)
,
\mathcal{A}_{1}\left(\boldsymbol{\theta}\right)
, and
\mathcal{A}_{2}\left(\boldsymbol{\theta}\right)
in (
294
) satisfy the following:

\displaystyle\mathcal{A}_{0}\left(\boldsymbol{\theta}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left\{\boldsymbol{\mu}\in\mathrm{supp}\,Q:\mathsf{L}_{\boldsymbol{z}}\left(\boldsymbol{\mu}\right)=\delta\right\},

(307a)

\displaystyle\mathcal{A}_{1}\left(\boldsymbol{\theta}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left\{\boldsymbol{\mu}\in\mathrm{supp}\,Q:\mathsf{L}_{\boldsymbol{z}}\left(\boldsymbol{\mu}\right)>\delta\right\},\mbox{ and }

(307b)

\displaystyle\mathcal{A}_{2}\left(\boldsymbol{\theta}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left\{\boldsymbol{\mu}\in\mathrm{supp}\,Q:\mathsf{L}_{\boldsymbol{z}}\left(\boldsymbol{\mu}\right)<\delta\right\}.

(307c)

Consider the sets

\displaystyle\mathcal{A}_{2,1}\left(\boldsymbol{\theta}\right)

\displaystyle\triangleq

\displaystyle\left\{\boldsymbol{\mu}\in\mathcal{A}_{2}\left(\boldsymbol{\theta}\right):\mathsf{L}_{\boldsymbol{z}}\left(\boldsymbol{\mu}\right)<\delta_{Q,\boldsymbol{z}}^{\star}\right\},\mbox{ and }

(308)

\displaystyle\mathcal{A}_{2,2}\left(\boldsymbol{\theta}\right)

\displaystyle\triangleq

\displaystyle\left\{\boldsymbol{\mu}\in\mathcal{A}_{2}\left(\boldsymbol{\theta}\right):\delta_{Q,\boldsymbol{z}}^{\star}\leqslant\mathsf{L}_{\boldsymbol{z}}\left(\boldsymbol{\mu}\right)<\delta\right\},

(309)

and note that
\mathcal{A}_{2,1}\left(\boldsymbol{\theta}\right)
and
\mathcal{A}_{2,2}\left(\boldsymbol{\theta}\right)
form a partition of
\mathcal{A}_{2}\left(\boldsymbol{\theta}\right)
. Moreover, from the definition of
\delta_{Q,\boldsymbol{z}}^{\star}
in (
37
), it holds that

\displaystyle Q\left(\mathcal{A}_{2,1}\left(\boldsymbol{\theta}\right)\right)=0.

(310)

Hence, plugging the equalities in (
307
) and (
310
) in (
296
) yields, for all
\delta>\delta_{Q,\boldsymbol{z}}^{\star}
and for all
\boldsymbol{\theta}\in\left\{\boldsymbol{\nu}\in\mathcal{M}:\mathsf{L}_{\boldsymbol{z}}\left(\boldsymbol{\nu}\right)=\delta\right\}
,

\displaystyle\frac{\mathrm{d}P^{\left(Q,\lambda\right)}_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}|\boldsymbol{Z}=\boldsymbol{z}}}{\mathrm{d}Q}\left(\boldsymbol{\theta}\right)

(311)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\bigg(Q\left(\mathcal{A}_{0}\left(\boldsymbol{\theta}\right)\right)+\displaystyle\int_{\mathcal{A}_{1}\left(\boldsymbol{\theta}\right)}\exp\left(\frac{1}{\lambda}\left(\mathsf{L}_{\boldsymbol{z}}\left(\boldsymbol{\theta}\right)-\mathsf{L}_{\boldsymbol{z}}\left(\boldsymbol{\nu}\right)\right)\right)\mathrm{d}Q\left(\boldsymbol{\nu}\right)

\displaystyle+

\displaystyle\displaystyle\int_{\mathcal{A}_{2,2}\left(\boldsymbol{\theta}\right)}\exp\left(\frac{1}{\lambda}\left(\mathsf{L}_{\boldsymbol{z}}\left(\boldsymbol{\theta}\right)-\mathsf{L}_{\boldsymbol{z}}\left(\boldsymbol{\nu}\right)\right)\right)\mathrm{d}Q\left(\boldsymbol{\nu}\right)\bigg)^{-1}.

The equality in (
311
) implies that for all
\delta>\delta_{Q,\boldsymbol{z}}^{\star}
and for all
\boldsymbol{\theta}

\in

\big\{\boldsymbol{\nu}\in\mathcal{M}
:
\mathsf{L}_{\boldsymbol{z}}\left(\boldsymbol{\nu}\right)=\delta\big\}
,

\displaystyle\lim_{\lambda\rightarrow 0^{+}}\frac{\mathrm{d}P^{\left(Q,\lambda\right)}_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}|\boldsymbol{Z}=\boldsymbol{z}}}{\mathrm{d}Q}\left(\boldsymbol{\theta}\right)

(312)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\Bigg(\lim_{\lambda\rightarrow 0^{+}}\displaystyle\int_{\mathcal{A}_{1}\left(\boldsymbol{\theta}\right)}\exp\left(\frac{1}{\lambda}\left(\mathsf{L}_{\boldsymbol{z}}\left(\boldsymbol{\theta}\right)-\mathsf{L}_{\boldsymbol{z}}\left(\boldsymbol{\nu}\right)\right)\right)\mathrm{d}Q\left(\boldsymbol{\nu}\right)

\displaystyle+

\displaystyle\lim_{\lambda\rightarrow 0^{+}}\displaystyle\int_{\mathcal{A}_{2,2}\left(\boldsymbol{\theta}\right)}\exp\left(\frac{1}{\lambda}\left(\mathsf{L}_{\boldsymbol{z}}\left(\boldsymbol{\theta}\right)-\mathsf{L}_{\boldsymbol{z}}\left(\boldsymbol{\nu}\right)\right)\right)\mathrm{d}Q\left(\boldsymbol{\nu}\right)

\displaystyle+

\displaystyle Q\left(\mathcal{A}_{0}\left(\boldsymbol{\theta}\right)\right)\Bigg)^{-1}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\Bigg(\lim_{\lambda\rightarrow 0^{+}}\displaystyle\int_{\mathcal{A}_{2,2}\left(\boldsymbol{\theta}\right)}\exp\left(\frac{1}{\lambda}\left(\mathsf{L}_{\boldsymbol{z}}\left(\boldsymbol{\theta}\right)-\mathsf{L}_{\boldsymbol{z}}\left(\boldsymbol{\nu}\right)\right)\right)\mathrm{d}Q\left(\boldsymbol{\nu}\right)

\displaystyle+

\displaystyle Q\left(\mathcal{A}_{0}\left(\boldsymbol{\theta}\right)\right)\Bigg)^{-1}

(313)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\big(Q\left(\mathcal{A}_{0}\left(\boldsymbol{\theta}\right)\right)+\infty\big)^{-1}

(314)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 0,

(315)

where
the equality in (
313
) follows by verifying that the dominated convergence theorem
[
59
, Theorem
2.6.9
]
holds. That is,

(a)
For all
\boldsymbol{\nu}\in\mathcal{A}_{1}\left(\boldsymbol{\theta}\right)
, it holds that
\exp\left(\frac{1}{\lambda}\left(\mathsf{L}_{\boldsymbol{z}}\left(\boldsymbol{\theta}\right)-\mathsf{L}_{\boldsymbol{z}}\left(\boldsymbol{\nu}\right)\right)\right)

<1
; and

(b)
For all
\boldsymbol{\nu}\in\mathcal{A}_{1}\left(\boldsymbol{\theta}\right)
, it holds that

\lim_{\lambda\rightarrow 0^{+}}\exp\left(\frac{1}{\lambda}\left(\mathsf{L}_{\boldsymbol{z}}\left(\boldsymbol{\theta}\right)-\mathsf{L}_{\boldsymbol{z}}\left(\boldsymbol{\nu}\right)\right)\right)=0.

(316)

This completes the second part.

The third part of the proof follows by noticing that the set
\big\{\boldsymbol{\nu}\in\mathrm{supp}\,Q
:
\mathsf{L}_{\boldsymbol{z}}\left(\boldsymbol{\nu}\right)<\delta_{Q,\boldsymbol{z}}^{\star}\big\}
is a negligible set with respect to
Q
and thus, for all
\boldsymbol{\theta}\in\big\{\boldsymbol{\nu}\in\mathrm{supp}\,Q:

\mathsf{L}_{\boldsymbol{z}}\left(\boldsymbol{\nu}\right)<\delta_{Q,\boldsymbol{z}}^{\star}\big\}
, the value
\frac{\mathrm{d}P^{\left(Q,\lambda\right)}_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}|\boldsymbol{Z}=\boldsymbol{z}}}{\mathrm{d}Q}\left(\boldsymbol{\theta}\right)
is immaterial. Hence, it is arbitrarily assumed that for all
\boldsymbol{\theta}\in\left\{\boldsymbol{\nu}\in\mathrm{supp}\,Q:\mathsf{L}_{\boldsymbol{z}}\left(\boldsymbol{\nu}\right)<\delta_{Q,\boldsymbol{z}}^{\star}\right\}
, it holds that

\frac{\mathrm{d}P^{\left(Q,\lambda\right)}_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}|\boldsymbol{Z}=\boldsymbol{z}}}{\mathrm{d}Q}\left(\boldsymbol{\theta}\right)=0.

(317)

This completes the third part and completes the proof.

Appendix H
Proof of Lemma
7

Consider the following partition of the set
\mathcal{M}
formed by the sets

\displaystyle\mathcal{A}_{0}

\displaystyle\triangleq

\displaystyle\left\{\boldsymbol{\theta}\in\mathcal{M}:\mathsf{L}_{\boldsymbol{z}}\left(\boldsymbol{\theta}\right)=\delta_{Q,\boldsymbol{z}}^{\star}\right\},

(318a)

\displaystyle\mathcal{A}_{1}

\displaystyle\triangleq

\displaystyle\left\{\boldsymbol{\theta}\in\mathcal{M}:\mathsf{L}_{\boldsymbol{z}}\left(\boldsymbol{\theta}\right)<\delta_{Q,\boldsymbol{z}}^{\star}\right\},\mbox{ and }

(318b)

\displaystyle\mathcal{A}_{2}

\displaystyle\triangleq

\displaystyle\left\{\boldsymbol{\theta}\in\mathcal{M}:\mathsf{L}_{\boldsymbol{z}}\left(\boldsymbol{\theta}\right)>\delta_{Q,\boldsymbol{z}}^{\star}\right\},

(318c)

with
\delta_{Q,\boldsymbol{z}}^{\star}
in (
37
) and the function
\mathsf{L}_{\boldsymbol{z}}
in (
3
).
Note that
\mathcal{A}_{0}=\mathcal{L}^{\star}_{Q,\boldsymbol{z}}
, with
\mathcal{L}^{\star}_{Q,\boldsymbol{z}}
in (
38
) and

\displaystyle 1

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle P^{\left(Q,\lambda\right)}_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}|\boldsymbol{Z}=\boldsymbol{z}}\left(\mathcal{A}_{0}\right)+P^{\left(Q,\lambda\right)}_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}|\boldsymbol{Z}=\boldsymbol{z}}\left(\mathcal{A}_{1}\right)+P^{\left(Q,\lambda\right)}_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}|\boldsymbol{Z}=\boldsymbol{z}}\left(\mathcal{A}_{2}\right)

(319)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle P^{\left(Q,\lambda\right)}_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}|\boldsymbol{Z}=\boldsymbol{z}}\left(\mathcal{A}_{0}\right)+P^{\left(Q,\lambda\right)}_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}|\boldsymbol{Z}=\boldsymbol{z}}\left(\mathcal{A}_{2}\right)

(320)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle P^{\left(Q,\lambda\right)}_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}|\boldsymbol{Z}=\boldsymbol{z}}\left(\mathcal{A}_{0}\right)+\int_{\mathcal{A}_{2}}\mathrm{d}P^{\left(Q,\lambda\right)}_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}|\boldsymbol{Z}=\boldsymbol{z}}(\boldsymbol{\theta}),

(321)

where,
the equality in (
320
) follows from noticing that
P^{\left(Q,\lambda\right)}_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}|\boldsymbol{Z}=\boldsymbol{z}}(\mathcal{A}_{1})=0
, which follows from the definition of
\delta_{Q,\boldsymbol{z}}^{\star}
in (
37
) and the fact that the probability measure
P^{\left(Q,\lambda\right)}_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}|\boldsymbol{Z}=\boldsymbol{z}}
is absolutely continuous with respect to the measure
Q
.

The above implies that

\displaystyle 1

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\lim_{\lambda\rightarrow 0^{+}}P^{\left(Q,\lambda\right)}_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}|\boldsymbol{Z}=\boldsymbol{z}}\left(\mathcal{A}_{0}\right)+\lim_{\lambda\rightarrow 0^{+}}\int_{\mathcal{A}_{2}}\frac{\mathrm{d}P^{\left(Q,\lambda\right)}_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}|\boldsymbol{Z}=\boldsymbol{z}}}{\mathrm{d}Q}\left(\boldsymbol{\theta}\right)\mathrm{d}Q(\boldsymbol{\theta})

(322)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\lim_{\lambda\rightarrow 0^{+}}P^{\left(Q,\lambda\right)}_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}|\boldsymbol{Z}=\boldsymbol{z}}\left(\mathcal{A}_{0}\right)+\int_{\mathcal{A}_{2}}\lim_{\lambda\rightarrow 0^{+}}\frac{\mathrm{d}P^{\left(Q,\lambda\right)}_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}|\boldsymbol{Z}=\boldsymbol{z}}}{\mathrm{d}Q}\left(\boldsymbol{\theta}\right)\mathrm{d}Q(\boldsymbol{\theta})

(323)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\lim_{\lambda\rightarrow 0^{+}}P^{\left(Q,\lambda\right)}_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}|\boldsymbol{Z}=\boldsymbol{z}}\left(\mathcal{A}_{0}\right),

(324)

where,
the equality in (
323
) follows from the dominated convergence theorem
[
59
, Theorem
1.6.9
]
, given that the Randon-Nikodym derivative
\frac{\mathrm{d}P^{\left(Q,\lambda\right)}_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}|\boldsymbol{Z}=\boldsymbol{z}}}{\mathrm{d}Q}
is positive and finite (Lemma
2
); and
the inequality in (
324
) holds from the fact that for all
\boldsymbol{\theta}\in\mathcal{A}_{2}
, it holds that
\lim_{\lambda\rightarrow 0^{+}}\frac{\mathrm{d}P^{\left(Q,\lambda\right)}_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}|\boldsymbol{Z}=\boldsymbol{z}}}{\mathrm{d}Q}\left(\boldsymbol{\theta}\right)=0
(Lemma
6
).
Hence, it finally holds that

\lim_{\lambda\rightarrow 0^{+}}P^{\left(Q,\lambda\right)}_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}|\boldsymbol{Z}=\boldsymbol{z}}\left(\mathcal{L}^{\star}_{Q,\boldsymbol{z}}\right)=1,

(325)

which completes the proof.

Appendix I
Proof of Lemma
8

The proof is presented in two parts. The first part shows that if for all
\delta\in\left(\rho^{\star},+\infty\right)
, the inequality in (
51
) holds, then,
Q
is coherent. The second part shows that if
Q
is not coherent, then there exists a
\delta\in\left(\rho^{\star},+\infty\right)
such that

\displaystyle P^{\left(Q,\lambda\right)}_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}|\boldsymbol{Z}=\boldsymbol{z}}\left(\mathcal{L}_{\boldsymbol{z}}\left(\delta\right)\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 0.

(326)

The first part is as follows. Note that for all
\delta\in\left(\rho^{\star},+\infty\right)
and for all
\boldsymbol{\theta}\in\mathcal{L}_{\boldsymbol{z}}\left(\delta\right)\cap\mathrm{supp}\,Q
, it holds from Lemma
2
that

\frac{\mathrm{d}P^{\left(Q,\lambda\right)}_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}|\boldsymbol{Z}=\boldsymbol{z}}}{\mathrm{d}Q}\left(\boldsymbol{\theta}\right)>0.

(327)

Hence, if for all
\delta\in\left(\rho^{\star},+\infty\right)
, the inequality in (
51
) holds, then

\displaystyle 0

\displaystyle<

\displaystyle P^{\left(Q,\lambda\right)}_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}|\boldsymbol{Z}=\boldsymbol{z}}\left(\mathcal{L}_{\boldsymbol{z}}\left(\delta\right)\right)

(328)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int_{\mathcal{L}_{\boldsymbol{z}}\left(\delta\right)}\mathrm{d}P^{\left(Q,\lambda\right)}_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}|\boldsymbol{Z}=\boldsymbol{z}}\left(\boldsymbol{\theta}\right)

(329)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int_{\mathcal{L}_{\boldsymbol{z}}\left(\delta\right)}\frac{\mathrm{d}P^{\left(Q,\lambda\right)}_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}|\boldsymbol{Z}=\boldsymbol{z}}}{\mathrm{d}Q}\left(\boldsymbol{\theta}\right)\mathrm{d}Q\left(\boldsymbol{\theta}\right),

(330)

which, together with (
327
), implies that for all
\delta\in\left(\rho^{\star},+\infty\right)
,
Q\left(\mathcal{L}_{\boldsymbol{z}}\left(\delta\right)\right)>0
. Hence,
Q
is coherent.

The second part is as follows. Assume that
Q
is not coherent. Then, there exists a
\delta\in\left(\rho^{\star},+\infty\right)
such that
Q\left(\mathcal{L}_{\boldsymbol{z}}\left(\delta\right)\right)=0
. Hence, from the fact that
P^{\left(Q,\lambda\right)}_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}|\boldsymbol{Z}=\boldsymbol{z}}
is absolutely continuous with respect to
Q
, it follows that
P^{\left(Q,\lambda\right)}_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}|\boldsymbol{Z}=\boldsymbol{z}}\left(\mathcal{L}_{\boldsymbol{z}}\left(\delta\right)\right)

=

0
.
This completes the proof.

Appendix J
Proof of Theorem
4

The optimization problem in (
84
) can be re-written in terms of the Radon-Nikodym derivative of the optimization measure
P
with respect to the measure
P^{\left(Q,\lambda\right)}_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}|\boldsymbol{Z}=\boldsymbol{z}}
, denoted by
\frac{\mathrm{d}P}{\mathrm{d}P^{\left(Q,\lambda\right)}_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}|\boldsymbol{Z}=\boldsymbol{z}}}:\mathcal{M}\to[0,+\infty)
, which yields:

\displaystyle\min_{P\in\triangle_{Q}\left(\mathcal{M},\mathscr{B}\left(\mathcal{M}\right)\right)}\displaystyle\int\mathsf{L}_{\boldsymbol{z}}\left(\boldsymbol{\nu}\right)\frac{\mathrm{d}P}{\mathrm{d}P^{\left(Q,\lambda\right)}_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}|\boldsymbol{Z}=\boldsymbol{z}}}\left(\boldsymbol{\nu}\right)\mathrm{d}P^{\left(Q,\lambda\right)}_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}|\boldsymbol{Z}=\boldsymbol{z}}\left(\boldsymbol{\nu}\right),

(331a)

subject to:

\displaystyle\int\frac{\mathrm{d}P}{\mathrm{d}P^{\left(Q,\lambda\right)}_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}|\boldsymbol{Z}=\boldsymbol{z}}}\left(\boldsymbol{\nu}\right)\log\left(\hskip-4.30554pt\frac{\mathrm{d}P}{\mathrm{d}P^{\left(Q,\lambda\right)}_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}|\boldsymbol{Z}=\boldsymbol{z}}}\left(\boldsymbol{\nu}\right)\hskip-4.30554pt\right)\mathrm{d}P^{\left(Q,\lambda\right)}_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}|\boldsymbol{Z}=\boldsymbol{z}}\left(\boldsymbol{\nu}\right)\leqslant c,\mbox{ and }

(331b)

\displaystyle\int\frac{\mathrm{d}P}{\mathrm{d}P^{\left(Q,\lambda\right)}_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}|\boldsymbol{Z}=\boldsymbol{z}}}\left(\boldsymbol{\theta}\right)\mathrm{d}P^{\left(Q,\lambda\right)}_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}|\boldsymbol{Z}=\boldsymbol{z}}(\boldsymbol{\theta})=1.

(331c)

The remainder of the proof focuses on the problem in which the optimization is over the function
\frac{\mathrm{d}P}{\mathrm{d}P^{\left(Q,\lambda\right)}_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}|\boldsymbol{Z}=\boldsymbol{z}}}
instead of the measure
P
. This is due to the fact that for all
P\in\bigtriangleup_{Q}\left(\mathcal{M}\right)
, the Radon-Nikodym derivate
\frac{\mathrm{d}P}{\mathrm{d}P^{\left(Q,\lambda\right)}_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}|\boldsymbol{Z}=\boldsymbol{z}}}
is unique up to sets of zero measure with respect to the measure
P^{\left(Q,\lambda\right)}_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}|\boldsymbol{Z}=\boldsymbol{z}}
.
Let
\mathscr{M}
be the set of measurable functions
\mathcal{M}\to\mathds{R}
with respect to the measurable spaces
\left(\mathcal{M},\mathscr{B}\left(\mathcal{M}\right)\right)
and
\left(\mathds{R},\mathscr{B}\left(\mathds{R}\right)\right)
that are absolutely integrable with respect to
P^{\left(Q,\lambda\right)}_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}|\boldsymbol{Z}=\boldsymbol{z}}
.
That is, for all
\hat{g}\in\mathscr{M}
, it holds that

\displaystyle\int\left|\hat{g}(\boldsymbol{\theta})\right|\mathrm{d}P^{\left(Q,\lambda\right)}_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}|\boldsymbol{Z}=\boldsymbol{z}}(\boldsymbol{\theta})

\displaystyle<

\displaystyle\infty.

(332)

Hence, the optimization problem of interest is:

\displaystyle\min_{g\in\mathscr{M}}

\displaystyle\displaystyle\int\mathsf{L}_{\boldsymbol{z}}\left(\boldsymbol{\nu}\right)g\left(\boldsymbol{\nu}\right)\mathrm{d}P^{\left(Q,\lambda\right)}_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}|\boldsymbol{Z}=\boldsymbol{z}}\left(\boldsymbol{\nu}\right)

(333a)

s.t:

\displaystyle\int g\left(\boldsymbol{\nu}\right)\log\left(g\left(\boldsymbol{\nu}\right)\right)\mathrm{d}P^{\left(Q,\lambda\right)}_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}|\boldsymbol{Z}=\boldsymbol{z}}\left(\boldsymbol{\nu}\right)\leqslant c,\mbox{ and }

(333b)

\displaystyle\int g\left(\boldsymbol{\theta}\right)\mathrm{d}P^{\left(Q,\lambda\right)}_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}|\boldsymbol{Z}=\boldsymbol{z}}(\boldsymbol{\theta})=1.

(333c)

The Lagrangian of the optimization problem in (
333
) is a functional
L:\mathscr{M}\times[0,+\infty)^{2}\rightarrow\mathds{R}
of the form

\displaystyle L\left(g,\alpha,\beta\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\displaystyle\int\mathsf{L}_{\boldsymbol{z}}\left(\boldsymbol{\nu}\right)g\left(\boldsymbol{\nu}\right)\mathrm{d}P^{\left(Q,\lambda\right)}_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}|\boldsymbol{Z}=\boldsymbol{z}}\left(\boldsymbol{\nu}\right)

(334)

\displaystyle+\alpha\left(\displaystyle\int g\left(\boldsymbol{\nu}\right)\log\left(g\left(\boldsymbol{\nu}\right)\right)\mathrm{d}P^{\left(Q,\lambda\right)}_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}|\boldsymbol{Z}=\boldsymbol{z}}\left(\boldsymbol{\nu}\right)-c\right)

\displaystyle+\beta\left(\displaystyle\int g\left(\boldsymbol{\nu}\right)\mathrm{d}P^{\left(Q,\lambda\right)}_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}|\boldsymbol{Z}=\boldsymbol{z}}\left(\boldsymbol{\nu}\right)-1\right),

where the reals
\alpha
and
\beta
are both nonnegative and act as Lagrangian multipliers due to the constraints (
333b
) and (
333c
), respectively.

Let
h:\mathcal{M}\rightarrow\mathds{R}
be a function in
\mathscr{M}
. The Gateaux differential of the functional
L
in (
334
) at
\left(g,\alpha,\beta\right)\in\mathscr{M}\times[0,+\infty)^{2}
in the direction of
h
, if it exists, is

\partial L\left(g,\alpha,\beta;h\right)\triangleq\left.\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}\gamma}r(\gamma)\right|_{\gamma=0},

(335)

where the real function
r:\mathds{R}\rightarrow\mathds{R}
is such that for all
\gamma\in\left(-\epsilon,\epsilon\right)
, with some
\epsilon>0
, satisfies

\displaystyle r(\gamma)

(336)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\displaystyle\int\mathsf{L}_{\boldsymbol{z}}\left(\boldsymbol{\nu}\right)\left(g\left(\boldsymbol{\nu}\right)+\gamma h\left(\boldsymbol{\nu}\right)\right)\mathrm{d}P^{\left(Q,\lambda\right)}_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}|\boldsymbol{Z}=\boldsymbol{z}}\left(\boldsymbol{\nu}\right)

\displaystyle+

\displaystyle\alpha\left(\displaystyle\int\left(g\left(\boldsymbol{\nu}\right)+\gamma h\left(\boldsymbol{\nu}\right)\right)\log\left(g\left(\boldsymbol{\nu}\right)+\gamma h\left(\boldsymbol{\nu}\right)\right)\mathrm{d}P^{\left(Q,\lambda\right)}_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}|\boldsymbol{Z}=\boldsymbol{z}}\left(\boldsymbol{\nu}\right)-c\right)

\displaystyle+

\displaystyle\beta\left(\displaystyle\int\left(g\left(\boldsymbol{\nu}\right)+\gamma h\left(\boldsymbol{\nu}\right)\right)\mathrm{d}P^{\left(Q,\lambda\right)}_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}|\boldsymbol{Z}=\boldsymbol{z}}\left(\boldsymbol{\nu}\right)-1\right).

The proof continues under the assumption that the functions
g
and
h
are such that the Gateaux differential in (
335
) exists.
That is, the function
r
in (
336
) is differentiable in
\left(-\epsilon,\epsilon\right)
, with some
\epsilon>0
.
Using the same arguments as in the proof of Theorem
3
, it follows that the derivative of the real function
r
in (
336
) is

\displaystyle\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}\gamma}r(\gamma)

(337)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\displaystyle\int\mathsf{L}_{\boldsymbol{z}}h\left(\boldsymbol{\nu}\right)\mathrm{d}P^{\left(Q,\lambda\right)}_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}|\boldsymbol{Z}=\boldsymbol{z}}\left(\boldsymbol{\nu}\right)+\alpha\displaystyle\int h\left(\boldsymbol{\nu}\right)\mathrm{d}P^{\left(Q,\lambda\right)}_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}|\boldsymbol{Z}=\boldsymbol{z}}\left(\boldsymbol{\nu}\right)

\displaystyle+

\displaystyle\alpha\displaystyle\int h\left(\boldsymbol{\nu}\right)\log\left(g\left(\boldsymbol{\nu}\right)+\gamma h\left(\boldsymbol{\nu}\right)\right)\mathrm{d}P^{\left(Q,\lambda\right)}_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}|\boldsymbol{Z}=\boldsymbol{z}}\left(\boldsymbol{\nu}\right)

\displaystyle+

\displaystyle\beta\displaystyle\int h\left(\boldsymbol{\nu}\right)\mathrm{d}P^{\left(Q,\lambda\right)}_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}|\boldsymbol{Z}=\boldsymbol{z}}\left(\boldsymbol{\nu}\right).

From (
335
) and (
337
), it follows that

\displaystyle\partial L\left(g,\alpha,\beta;h\right)

(338)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\displaystyle\int h\left(\boldsymbol{\nu}\right)\left(\mathsf{L}_{\boldsymbol{z}}\left(\boldsymbol{\nu}\right)+\alpha\left(1+\log g\left(\boldsymbol{\nu}\right)\right)+\beta\right)\mathrm{d}P^{\left(Q,\lambda\right)}_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}|\boldsymbol{Z}=\boldsymbol{z}}\left(\boldsymbol{\nu}\right).

From
[
73
, Theorem
1
, page
217
]
, it holds that a necessary condition for the functional
L
in (
334
) to have a minimum at
\left(g,\alpha,\beta\right)\in\mathscr{M}\times[0,+\infty)^{2}
is that for all functions
h~\in~\mathscr{M}
,

\partial L\left(g,\alpha,\beta;h\right)=0,

(339)

which implies that for all
\boldsymbol{\nu}\in\mathcal{M}
,

\mathsf{L}_{\boldsymbol{z}}\left(\boldsymbol{\nu}\right)+\alpha\left(1+\log g\left(\boldsymbol{\nu}\right)\right)+\beta=0.

(340)

Thus,

g\left(\boldsymbol{\nu}\right)=\exp\left(-\frac{\mathsf{L}_{\boldsymbol{z}}\left(\boldsymbol{\nu}\right)}{\alpha}\right)\exp\left(-\frac{\beta+\alpha}{\alpha}\right),

(341)

where
\alpha
and
\beta
are chosen to satisfy their corresponding constraints with equality.
Denote by
P^{\star}
the solution of the optimization problem in (
84
). Hence, from (
341
), it follows that

\frac{\mathrm{d}P^{\star}}{\mathrm{d}P^{\left(Q,\lambda\right)}_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}|\boldsymbol{Z}=\boldsymbol{z}}}\left(\boldsymbol{\nu}\right)=\frac{\exp\left(-\frac{\mathsf{L}_{\boldsymbol{z}}\left(\boldsymbol{\nu}\right)}{\alpha}\right)}{\displaystyle\int\exp\left(-\frac{\mathsf{L}_{\boldsymbol{z}}\left(\boldsymbol{\theta}\right)}{\alpha}\right)\mathrm{d}P^{\left(Q,\lambda\right)}_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}|\boldsymbol{Z}=\boldsymbol{z}}\left(\boldsymbol{\theta}\right)},

(342)

where
\alpha
is chosen to satisfy

\displaystyle D\left(P^{\star}\|P^{\left(Q,\lambda\right)}_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}|\boldsymbol{Z}=\boldsymbol{z}}\right)=c.

(343)

From Lemma
3
, it follows that the probability measure
P^{\star}
and the
\sigma
-finite measure
Q
satisfy,

\displaystyle\frac{\mathrm{d}P^{\star}}{\mathrm{d}Q}\left(\boldsymbol{\nu}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{\mathrm{d}P^{\star}}{\mathrm{d}P^{\left(Q,\lambda\right)}_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}|\boldsymbol{Z}=\boldsymbol{z}}}\left(\boldsymbol{\nu}\right)\frac{\mathrm{d}P^{\left(Q,\lambda\right)}_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}|\boldsymbol{Z}=\boldsymbol{z}}}{\mathrm{d}Q}\left(\boldsymbol{\nu}\right)

(346)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left(\frac{\exp\left(-\frac{\mathsf{L}_{\boldsymbol{z}}\left(\boldsymbol{\nu}\right)}{\alpha}\right)}{\displaystyle\int\exp\left(-\frac{\mathsf{L}_{\boldsymbol{z}}\left(\boldsymbol{\theta}\right)}{\alpha}\right)\mathrm{d}P^{\left(Q,\lambda\right)}_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}|\boldsymbol{Z}=\boldsymbol{z}}\left(\boldsymbol{\theta}\right)}\right)

\displaystyle\left(\frac{\exp\left(-\frac{\mathsf{L}_{\boldsymbol{z}}\left(\boldsymbol{\nu}\right)}{\lambda}\right)}{\displaystyle\int\exp\left(-\frac{\mathsf{L}_{\boldsymbol{z}}\left(\boldsymbol{\theta}\right)}{\lambda}\right)\mathrm{d}Q\left(\boldsymbol{\theta}\right)}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left(\frac{\exp\left(-\frac{\mathsf{L}_{\boldsymbol{z}}\left(\boldsymbol{\nu}\right)}{\alpha}\right)}{\displaystyle\int\frac{\exp\left(-\frac{\mathsf{L}_{\boldsymbol{z}}\left(\boldsymbol{\theta}\right)}{\alpha}\right)\exp\left(-\frac{\mathsf{L}_{\boldsymbol{z}}\left(\boldsymbol{\theta}\right)}{\lambda}\right)}{\displaystyle\int\exp\left(-\frac{\mathsf{L}_{\boldsymbol{z}}\left(\boldsymbol{\alpha}\right)}{\lambda}\right)\mathrm{d}Q\left(\boldsymbol{\alpha}\right)}\mathrm{d}Q\left(\boldsymbol{\theta}\right)}\right)

\displaystyle\left(\frac{\exp\left(-\frac{\mathsf{L}_{\boldsymbol{z}}\left(\boldsymbol{\nu}\right)}{\lambda}\right)}{\displaystyle\int\exp\left(-\frac{\mathsf{L}_{\boldsymbol{z}}\left(\boldsymbol{\theta}\right)}{\lambda}\right)\mathrm{d}Q\left(\boldsymbol{\theta}\right)}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{\exp\left(-\left(\frac{1}{\alpha}+\frac{1}{\lambda}\right)\mathsf{L}_{\boldsymbol{z}}\left(\boldsymbol{\nu}\right)\right)}{\displaystyle\int\exp\left(-\left(\frac{1}{\alpha}+\frac{1}{\lambda}\right)\mathsf{L}_{\boldsymbol{z}}\left(\boldsymbol{\nu}\right)\right)\mathrm{d}Q\left(\boldsymbol{\theta}\right)},

(347)

which implies that
P^{\star}
is a Gibbs probability measure on
\left(\mathcal{M},\mathscr{B}\left(\mathcal{M}\right)\right)
, with energy function
\mathsf{L}_{\boldsymbol{z}}
, reference measure
Q
, and regularization parameter
\frac{1}{\frac{1}{\alpha}+\frac{1}{\lambda}}
, where
\alpha
is chosen to satisfy (
343
).
Let the positive real
\omega
be
\omega\triangleq\frac{\alpha\lambda}{\alpha+\lambda}
and note that
\omega\in\left(0,\lambda\right]
and satisfies
D\left(P^{\left(Q,\omega\right)}_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}|\boldsymbol{Z}=\boldsymbol{z}}\left(\boldsymbol{\nu}\right)\|P^{\left(Q,\lambda\right)}_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}|\boldsymbol{Z}=\boldsymbol{z}}\right)=c
.
The proof ends by verifying that the objective function in (
334
) is strictly convex, and thus, the measure
P^{\left(Q,\omega\right)}_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}|\boldsymbol{Z}=\boldsymbol{z}}
is the unique minimizer.
This completes the proof.

Appendix K
Proof of Lemma
15

Note that for all
(\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2})\in\left\{x\in\mathds{R}:K_{Q,\boldsymbol{z}}(x)<+\infty\right\}^{2}
, such that
\lambda_{1}>\lambda_{2}
, it follows that for all
\boldsymbol{\theta}\in\mathrm{supp}\,Q
, the inequality
\exp\left(\lambda_{2}\;\mathsf{L}_{\boldsymbol{z}}\left(\boldsymbol{\theta}\right)\right)\leqslant\exp\left(\lambda_{1}\;\mathsf{L}_{\boldsymbol{z}}\left(\boldsymbol{\theta}\right)\right)
holds. This implies that
K_{Q,\boldsymbol{z}}\left(\lambda_{2}\right)\leqslant K_{Q,\boldsymbol{z}}\left(\lambda_{1}\right)<+\infty
, which proves that the function is nondecreasing.

The proof of continuity of the function
K_{Q,\boldsymbol{z}}
follows from observing that for all
\alpha\in\left\{x\in\mathds{R}:K_{Q,\boldsymbol{z}}(x)<+\infty\right\}
, it holds that

\displaystyle\lim_{t\to\alpha}K_{Q,\boldsymbol{z}}(t)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\lim_{t\to\alpha}\log\left(\int\exp\left(t\;\mathsf{L}_{\boldsymbol{z}}\left(\boldsymbol{\theta}\right)\right)\mathrm{d}Q(\boldsymbol{\theta})\right)

(348)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\log\left(\lim_{t\to\alpha}\int\exp\left(t\;\mathsf{L}_{\boldsymbol{z}}\left(\boldsymbol{\theta}\right)\right)\mathrm{d}Q(\boldsymbol{\theta})\right)

(349)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\log\left(\int\lim_{t\to\alpha}\exp\left(t\;\mathsf{L}_{\boldsymbol{z}}\left(\boldsymbol{\theta}\right)\right)\mathrm{d}Q(\boldsymbol{\theta})\right)

(350)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\log\left(\int\exp\left(\alpha\;\mathsf{L}_{\boldsymbol{z}}\left(\boldsymbol{\theta}\right)\right)\mathrm{d}Q(\boldsymbol{\theta})\right)

(351)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle K_{Q,\boldsymbol{z}}(\alpha),

(352)

where (
349
) and (
351
) follow from the fact that both the logarithmic and exponential functions are continuous; and the equality in (
350
) follows from the monotone convergence theorem
[
59
, Theorem
1.6.2
]
. This shows that the function
K_{Q,\boldsymbol{z}}
is continuous in
\left\{x\in\mathds{R}:K_{Q,\boldsymbol{z}}(x)<+\infty\right\}
.

The proof of differentiability follows by considering the transport of the
\sigma
-finite measure
Q
in (
22
) from the measure space
\left(\mathcal{M},\mathscr{B}\left(\mathcal{M}\right)\right)
